--- Page 74 ---
<meta>This page continues the definitions under the letter 'C', specifically focusing on the various types and degrees of heat (Calor) used in alchemical and chemical operations. It follows the previous page's discussion on copper-related minerals like vitriol and chalcitis.</meta>

# CHEMICAL DICTIONARY. 43

->D 4<-

**Heat of ashes** <note>original: *Calor cinerum*</note>, used when the more fixed <note>stable or non-volatile</note> parts are drawn out through ashes. This heat is a middle ground between the heat of a water bath and the heat of sand; therefore, by its power, not only are the more subtle substances—those approaching the nature of a simple balance—driven forth, but even colors and the more fixed parts are extracted.

**Heat of dung** <note>original: *Calor fimi*</note>, is when digestion occurs by placing the vessel within manure to contain the material. This heat is used for infusing, digesting, and putrefying things. Sometimes horse dung is used, in which the vessel is buried; occasionally a wooden vessel filled with hay or straw, to which water is applied by means of boiling vapor, is used in place of dung.

**Digestive heat** <note>original: *Calor digerens*</note>, is that by which the material to be dissolved is digested, and is achieved either in an <term>athanor</term> <note>a furnace designed to provide steady, uniform heat for long periods</note> or in dung.

**Strong heat** <note>original: *Calor fortis*</note>, is when things are separated by an increased and more intense fire.

**Heat of sand** <note>original: *Calor arenæ*</note>, is a middle ground between the heat of ashes and the heat of iron filings. It occurs when the vessel containing the material is placed in a sand-basin, surrounded by sand, and pushes forth a more fixed substance than ashes were able to drive out. Here it should be noted that fine sand provides a heat that is not as violent as coarse sand.

**Heat of filings** <note>original: *Calor scobis*</note>, or iron dross <note>original: *scoriæ ferri*</note>, is more intense than the heat mentioned above and closer to an open flame; it occurs when things are driven forth using iron filings or dross.

**Heat of coals** <note>original: *Calor carbonum*</note>, is when the heat of burning coals <note>original: *prunarum flagrantium*</note> touches the material or the vessel directly. This is used in infusions, <term>cementations</term> <note>the process of surrounding a metal with powder and heating it to change its properties</note>, trials, <term>calcinations</term> <note>heating a substance to high temperatures to reduce it to a powder or oxide</note>, and dissolutions.

**Heat of flames** <note>original: *Calor flammarum*</note>, is when the flames, supplied with suitable fuel, reach the material. This heat is called **living fire** <note>original: *ignis vivus*</note>, by which the bodies of all metals are reverberated <note>subjected to heat reflected from the roof of a furnace</note> and calcined, and by which spirits are finally driven forth.

<summary>This page defines the various grades and methods of applying heat in the alchemical laboratory, ranging from the gentle warmth of horse dung and ash to the intense "living fire" of direct flames.</summary>
<keywords>alchemical heat, ash bath, dung bath, athanor, sand bath, iron filings, calcination, living fire, distillation</keywords>

--- Page 75 ---
<meta>This page continues the definitions under the letter 'C', transitioning from the various types of laboratory heat to the chemical process of calcination. It follows the previous discussion of "heat of filings" by introducing "light heat of the bladder" and then categorizing the different ways substances are reduced to powder.</meta>

# CHEMICAL DICTIONARY. 44

**Light heat of the bladder** <note>original: *Calor vesicæ levis*</note>, is that by which a moist substance is drawn out from the material placed in a vessel <note>original: *vesicæ*; here referring to a copper heating vessel or a bladder-shaped flask</note> when an <term>alembic</term> <note>the cap or head of a still used to collect and condense vapors</note> is applied.

**Calcination of bodies** <note>original: *Calcinatio corporum*</note>, is a combustion performed with a greater fire, or it is the reduction of coagulated things into "lime" <note>original: *calcem*; in alchemy, this refers to any fine, dry powder resulting from heating or corrosion</note>. This <term>calcination</term> is done either through corrosion or through ignition <note>direct heating by fire</note>.

**Calcination by dry corrosion** <note>original: *Calcinatio corrosione sicca*</note> occurs when the bodies to be calcined are joined not by a liquid, but by dry corrosive materials in its place, by which they are calcined. This includes <term>cementation</term> <note>heating a solid metal surrounded by a powdered reagent</note> and mixing.

**Calcination by vaporous corrosion** <note>original: *Calcinatio corrosione vaporosa*</note> occurs when metallic bodies, having been reduced into thin plates, are calcined by sharp and corrosive smoke or vapor.

Sometimes these plated bodies are suspended over **strong waters** <note>original: *aquis fortibus*; concentrated mineral acids, usually nitric acid</note>, sometimes over vinegar, or over the remains of pressed grapes <note>original: *recrementis uvicarum*</note>; sometimes, especially with the more noble metals, they are rendered fragile and crushed by the "breath" <note>fumes</note> of molten lead or **quicksilver** <note>original: *argenti vivi*; mercury</note>; sometimes other methods are employed. For example:

To reduce **steel** <note>original: *Chalybem*</note> into a fine "saffron" <note>original: *crocum*; a term for a fine, usually reddish, metallic powder or oxide</note> by vaporous corrosion, I proceed thus: First, I prepare **strong water** <note>nitric acid</note> from equal parts of **saltpeter** <note>potassium nitrate</note> and **vitriol** <note>sulfate minerals</note> calcined to whiteness, and I pour it into a glass <term>cucurbit</term> <note>the lower, gourd-shaped part of a distilling apparatus</note>. Afterward, I suspend plates of steel in the upper part of the cucurbit, and I coat the joints of the vessel with <term>lute** <note>original: *luto*; a sealant or clay used to make laboratory equipment airtight</note> so that the spirits <note>vapors</note> of the strong water cannot exhale in any way, and I permit it to vaporize in warm sand for twenty-four hours. Then, having opened the vessel, I remove the plates, to which a "saffron" or very fine powder adheres, which I wipe off with a **hare's foot** <note>original: *lugopode* / *lagopode*; the soft paw of a hare, traditionally used as a brush in laboratories to collect fine powders</note>. Furthermore, I suspend the plates again in the said vessel and proceed in the manner described above, doing so until no more saffron is extracted by the spirits or found on the plates. In this way, lead suspended over vinegar is calcined into **ceruse** <note>white lead, a common pigment</note>; thus copper is calcined over grape remains, and in the same way other metals are corroded by vapors.

<meta>catchword: Calci-</meta>

<summary>This page defines the "light heat of the bladder" and details the methods of calcination, particularly focusing on "vaporous corrosion" where metal plates are turned into fine powders using acid fumes. It provides a specific recipe for creating "saffron of steel" using saltpeter, vitriol, and a sealed glass vessel.</summary>

<keywords>calcination, corrosion, saffron of steel, aqua fortis, saltpeter, vitriol, cucurbit, ceruse, alchemy, chemical apparatus</keywords>

--- Page 76 ---
<meta>This page continues the definitions under the letter 'C', moving from the general concept of calcination by corrosion discussed on the previous page to specific wet and reverberatory methods, as well as the classification of laboratory heat.</meta>

# CHEMICAL DICTIONARY. 45

**Immersive wet calcination** <note>original: *Calcinatio humida immersiva*</note> is when a body is immersed in some liquid which corrodes it and thereby calcinates it; it is completed either by <term>amalgamation</term> <note>the process of combining a metal with mercury</note> or by <term>precipitation</term>.

**Reverberatory calcination** <note>original: *Calcinatio reverberii*; heat reflected back onto the substance</note> is a dissolution into <term>lime</term> <note>original: *calcem*; in alchemy, any fine powder produced by fire or corrosion</note>. Lime, however, in its general meaning, is any powder ground into imperceptible parts, which they were also accustomed to call **alcool** <note>original: *alcool*; derived from Arabic, referring here to an extremely fine, purified powder rather than liquid alcohol</note>. Thus, it includes lime properly so-called, *Alcool* (which is the ash resulting from <term>levigation</term> <note>grinding a substance into a fine paste or powder while wet</note>), and the atoms of corrosion.

**Calcination by reverberation** is twofold:
**Calcination specifically so-called, and incineration.** <note>original: *cinefactio*; literally "making into ash"</note>

**Special calcination** is that by which the lime principally so-called is produced in a reverberatory combustion. Therefore, it could be more aptly named by the Greeks as **titanosis** <note>original: *τιτανῶσις*; the process of turning to lime</note> because it produces **titanon** <note>original: *τίτανον*; lime or white earth</note>, a certain species of which is **asbestos** <note>original: *ἄσβεστος*; meaning "unquenchable," used here for unslaked or "quick" lime</note>, which they name as unslaked or "living" lime, made from limestone. In this process, either a substance is calcined alone, or with additives that thoroughly assist the combustion—such as sulfur, nitre <note>saltpeter</note>, etc.—which we add when the structure of the matter is very strong and untameable. Otherwise, volatile components would be dissipated sooner than the fixed parts could be overcome by the flame. However, a breathing hole <note>original: *spiraculum*</note> must be provided for nitrous substances so that the vessel remains safe. See *Greater and lesser calcinatum* on the following page.

**Heat is either Free or Obstructed.**

**Free heat** <note>original: *Calor Liber*</note> is that which directly touches the matter or the vessel containing the matter. By this heat, those things are drawn out which stubbornly resist releasing their liquid, whether this occurs because of dryness or scarcity, or because the liquid adheres more tenaciously to the earthy parts due to its viscosity and homogeneity; it serves for calcination, fusion, and other operations.

**Obstructed heat** <note>original: *Calor impeditus*</note> is when the fire is prevented from touching the matter or vessel directly by another intervening vessel.

**Free heat** is produced by coals or flames.

**Obstructed heat** is produced by sand or filings <note>original: *scobis*; usually metal or wood filings used as a medium to distribute heat evenly</note>.

->C 5<-
<meta>catchword: Caballi</meta>

<summary>This page defines various modes of calcination, explaining how metals and minerals are reduced to fine powders (limes) through liquid corrosion or reflected heat. It also introduces a classification of laboratory heat into "free" (direct flame) and "obstructed" (using sand baths or other buffers).</summary>
<keywords>calcination, lime, Alcool, reverberatory furnace, titanosis, quicklime, chemical heat, laboratory apparatus, nitre</keywords>

--- Page 77 ---
<meta>This page continues the alphabetical definitions of alchemical and Paracelsian terms, moving from the technical processes of calcination into the spiritual and biological classifications of the "C" entries, specifically focusing on astral bodies, impurities of the body, and various metal oxides known as "limes."</meta>

# CHEMICAL DICTIONARY. 46

*Caballi, or Cabales*, are spirits <note>original: *lemures*</note> and the astral bodies of men who were extinguished by some sudden death before their predestined course of life was complete. These are thought to wander over the earth afterwards, like ghosts, until the term of their predestined life is fulfilled, so that what they were meant to live out corporally, they fulfill spiritually—if such a thing is lawful to believe and is not found to be discordant with true Christian religion according to the Holy Scriptures. It is from the Scriptures that one must seek the <term>touchstone</term> <note>original: *lapis lydius*; a stone used to test the purity of gold, here used metaphorically for a standard of truth</note> by which everything is rejected that is not forged from the gold of the Gospel.

*Cacedonium tartarum* is a harmful matter in the human body, caused by a separation performed by the body's sorting faculty when the expulsive faculty does not immediately follow. <note>In Paracelsian medicine, "tartar" refers to stony precipitates in the body that cause disease when the "internal alchemist" fails to expel waste.</note>

*Casa* is a word used for camphor.

*Cahos* <note>Chaos</note>, beyond being the heap of all things, is "air" according to Theophrastus <note>Paracelsus</note>, and is taken for the aforementioned *Iliastes* or *Iliastro*.

*Calcanthum* is vitriol <note>a sulfate of a metal, often copper or iron</note>. See the *preceding page*.

**Greater calcined** <note>original: *Calcinatum majus*</note> refers to everything that has been made sweet by the <term>spagyric</term> <note>the alchemical art of separating and recombining natural substances to produce medicines</note> art which was not so by its own nature, such as the "sweetness of mercury," lead or its soul, salt, and similar things that exist in a state of very rapid consolidation.

**Lesser calcined** <note>original: *Calcinatum minus*</note> is everything that is sweet by nature and heals very quickly, such as sugar, manna, honey-dew <note>original: *tereniabin*; a Persian term for manna</note>, star-jelly <note>original: *nostoch*; a type of algae or cyanobacteria believed to have fallen from the stars</note>, and all similar things.

*Callena* is a species of saltpeter.

*Caleruth* is an indication of a longing for the first eternal state, as when something desires to return into the prime matter from which it had come forth.

*Calliette* are yellow fungi found on juniper plants.

**Lime of mercury** <note>original: *Calx mercurii*</note> is precipitated mercury.

**Lime of Venus** <note>original: *Calx veneris*</note> is "green of bronze" <note>verdigris or copper acetate</note>.

**Lime of Saturn** <note>original: *Calx saturni*</note> is red lead <note>minium</note>.

**Lime of Jupiter** <note>original: *Calx Iovis*</note> is the spirit of tin.

<meta>catchword: Calx</meta>

# CHEMICAL DICTIONARY. 47

*Lime of Mars* <note>original: *Calx martis*</note> is saffron of steel or iron <note>referring to iron oxide, often used as a pigment or medicine</note>.

*Lime of the Sun* <note>original: *Calx solis*</note> is calcined gold.

*Lime of the Moon* <note>original: *Calx lunæ*</note> is silver, the lime of silver, or its blue flower.

*Permanent or fixed lime* <note>original: *Calx permanens, vel fixa*</note> is matter that cannot be burned away.

*Lime of pilgrims* <note>original: *Calx peregrinorum*</note> is tartar <note>the potassium bitartrate crystals found in wine barrels</note>.

*Lime of woods* <note>original: *Calx lignorum*</note> is their ash, which cannot be converted into glass or any other matter.

*Chaomancy* <note>original: *Chaomantia*</note> is the art by which a prophecy is taken from the air.

*Claretta* is the white of eggs.

*Cambuca* is an abscess or an ulcer in the groin. A swelling of the groin.

*Coals of heaven* <note>original: *Carbones cœli*</note> are the stars.

*Carbuncle* <note>original: *Carbunculus*</note> is an abscess or a pestilential ulcer arising from "black blood" <note>melancholic or corrupted blood</note>.

*Cardonium* is wine medicated with herbs.

*Carena* is the twentieth part of a single drop.

*Cassatum* is blood in the veins that is weak and dead, which obstructs the movement and passage of healthy blood.

<margin>Paracelsus in the Archidoxis.</margin>
*Prepared cheese* <note>original: *Caseus præparatus*</note> is the viscous residue remaining at the bottom of the milk that flows from cheese.

*Cathimia* is silver dross <note>original: *argenti spuma*; literally "foam of silver," likely litharge or lead oxide</note>.

*Tail of the red fox* <note>original: *Cauda vulpis rubicundi*</note> is minium made from lead <note>red lead oxide, used in paints and alchemy</note>.

*Precaution in the spagyric art* <note>original: *Cautela in arte spagyrica*</note> is a certain industry acquired through habit, by which the practitioners of this art more easily perform and complete their operations.

*Cautery* <note>original: *Cauterium*, or *Kautir*</note> is an instrument used by surgeons to open the skin and flesh by burning; it is also a medicine used in its place which opens the skin by its corrosive nature without pain.

*Celsa* is otherwise called the "muscle of life," the pulse that wanders through every part of the body.

*Unification* <note>original: *Coadunatio*</note> is the refinement or elaboration by which scattered things are joined together.

<meta>This page continues the alphabetical lexicon of alchemical and Paracelsian terms, transitioning from the previous page's discussion of biological residues and celestial metaphors into specific definitions for spirits of the dead, metabolic obstructions, and the "limes" or oxides of various metals.</meta>

# CHEMICAL DICTIONARY.

**Caballi**, or **Cabales**, are spirits <note>original: *lemures*</note>, and the astral bodies of men who were extinguished by some sudden death before their predestined course of life was completed. These are thought to wander afterward over the earth, like ghosts, until the term of their predestined life is fulfilled, so that what they were meant to live corporally, they fulfill spiritually—if it is right to believe this, and if it is not found to be discordant with the true Christian religion according to the Holy Scriptures, from which the touchstone <note>original: *lapis lydius*; a stone used to test the purity of gold</note> must be sought to reject everything that is not refined from the gold of the Gospel.

**Cacedonium tartar** <note>original: *Cacedonium tartarum*</note>, is a harmful matter <note>original: *materia peccans*; literally "sinning matter," a medical term for humors causing disease</note> in the human body, arising because of a separation made by the segregative faculty when the expulsive faculty does not immediately follow.

**Cafa**, is said for camphor.

**Cahos**, besides being the heap of all things, is for **Theophrastus** <note>referring to Theophrastus Paracelsus, the famous Swiss-German physician and alchemist</note> the air, and it is taken for the aforementioned **Iliaste** or **Iliastro** <note>Paracelsian terms for the primeval matter of the universe</note>. *And Chaos*.

**Calcanthum**, is vitriol <note>a sulfate of a metal, often copper or iron</note>. See the *previous page*.

**The Greater Calcinate** <note>original: *Calcinatum majus*</note>, is said of everything that has been made sweet by the spagyric art <note>the alchemical process of separating and recombining plant or mineral components</note> which was not so by its own nature, such as the sweetness of mercury, lead or its "soul," salt, and similar things, which result in very rapid consolidation.

**The Lesser Calcinate** <note>original: *Calcinatum minus*</note>, is everything that is sweet by nature and heals very quickly, such as sugar, manna, tereniabin <note>a type of edible manna gathered from plants</note>, nostoch <note>a jelly-like green algae once thought to have fallen from the stars</note>, and all similar things.

**Callena**, is a species of saltpeter <note>potassium nitrate</note>.

**Caleruth**, is a sign of the desire for the first eternity, as when something desires to return into the primary matter from which it had come forth.

**Calliette**, are the yellow fungi found on juniper plants.

**Lime of mercury** <note>original: *Calx mercurii*</note>, is precipitated mercury.

**Lime of Venus** <note>original: *Calx veneris*</note>, is verdigris <note>the green pigment or crust formed on copper; "Venus" is the alchemical name for copper</note>.

**Lime of Saturn** <note>original: *Calx saturni*</note>, is red lead <note>original: *minium*; Saturn is the alchemical name for lead</note>.

**Lime of Jupiter** <note>original: *Calx Iovis*</note>, is the spirit of Jupiter <note>referring to the oxide or essence of tin, as "Jupiter" is the alchemical name for tin</note>.

-># CHEMICAL DICTIONARY.<-

**Lime of Mars** <note>original: *Calx martis*</note>, is the saffron of steel <note>original: *crocus calybis*; a reddish-orange iron oxide</note> or iron.

**Lime of the Sun** <note>original: *Calx solis*</note>, is calcined gold <note>gold reduced to a fine powder through heat</note>.

**Lime of the Moon** <note>original: *Calx lunæ*</note>, is silver, the lime of silver, or its azure-blue flower <note>likely referring to a silver salt or oxide that appears bluish</note>.

**Permanent lime**, or fixed lime <note>original: *Calx permanens*</note>, is matter that cannot be consumed by fire.

**Lime of travelers** <note>original: *Calx peregrinorum*</note>, is tartar <note>the crust-like sediment found in wine barrels</note>.

**Lime of wood** <note>original: *Calx lignorum*</note>, is their ash, which is not converted into glass or any other matter.

**Aeromancy** <note>original: *Chaomantia*</note>, is the art by which a prophecy is drawn from the air <note>from *chaos*, which Paracelsus used to mean the element of air</note>.

**Claretta**, is the white of eggs.

**Cambuca**, is an abscess <note>original: *apostema*</note>, or an ulcer in the groin. A swelling of the groin.

**Coals of heaven** <note>original: *Carbones cæli*</note>, are the stars.

**Carbuncle** <note>original: *Carbunculus*</note>, is an abscess, or a pestilential ulcer arising from dark, "black" blood.

**Cardonium**, is wine medicated with herbs.

**Carena**, is the twentieth part of a drop.

**Cassatum**, is blood in the veins that is weak and dead, obstructing the passage and movement of healthy blood.
<margin>Paracelsus in his *Archidoxes* <note>a foundational work by Paracelsus on the "arch-doctrines" of alchemy and medicine</note>.</margin>

**Prepared cheese** <note>original: *Caseus præparatus*</note>, is the viscous residue remaining at the bottom of the milk flowing from cheese.

**Cathimia**, is the dross or "scum" of silver <note>original: *argenti spuma*; often refers to litharge or metallic slag</note>.

**Tail of the red fox** <note>original: *Cauda vulpis rubicundi*</note>, is red lead <note>original: *minium*</note> derived from lead.

**Skill in the spagyric art** <note>original: *Cautela in arte spagyrica*</note>, is a certain industry or expertise acquired through practice, by which the practitioners of this art more easily perform and complete their operations.

**Cautery** <note>original: *Cauterium, Kautir*</note>, is one of the instruments of surgeons with which they open the skin and flesh by burning; it is also a medicine substituted in its place which, by its corrosive nature, opens the flesh without pain.

**Celsa**, otherwise called the "muscle of life," is the pulse wandering through individual parts of the body.

**Unification** <note>original: *Coadunatio*</note>, is the process of refinement by which separate things are joined together.

<meta>This page continues the "C" section of the dictionary. While the previous page ended with "Cassatum" (stagnant blood), this page opens with the concluding thoughts on "Coadunatio" (the act of uniting) before transitioning into an extensive entry on "Coagulatio" (Coagulation).</meta>

-># CHEMICAL DICTIONARY.<-

It is sometimes called by the name of a more excellent species, **Coagulation**; from which arose that dogma of the philosophers, that the Chemical art consists in solution and coagulation <note>referring to the famous alchemical maxim *Solve et Coagula*: dissolve and coagulate</note>, which, however, refers rather to the mystery of the Stone <note>original: *lapidis mysterium*; the Philosophers' Stone</note>. Thus, **coadunation** <note>the act of joining different substances into one</note> is the reduction of a solution in those things in which it can be done. Its species are two: Composition and Coagulation.

*Coagulation*, is the thickening of things of the same nature from a thin and fluid consistency to a solid one. Therefore, those things which were thinned by watery, airy, or fiery resolution <note>the process of breaking a substance down into its elements</note> are reduced through this into a uniform body. And thus coagulation accompanies many operations, such as evaporation <note>original: *divaporationem*</note>, exaltation, sublimation, distillation, etc. It occurs in two ways: by Segregation and by Comprehension. And it is either Cold or Hot.

1. *Cold coagulation* is when things dissolved in heat are coagulated in the cold.

2. *Hot coagulation* is when, by a suitable heat, dissolved things are coagulated.

*Coagulation by segregation* is when, after certain things are separated, the remainder thickens. Thus, it could primarily be called "concretion" <term>sympexis <note>original: $\sigma\acute{\upsilon}\mu\pi\eta\xi\iota\varsigma$; a Greek term meaning a congealing or curdling together, often used by Paracelsus to describe how minerals form</note></term>. It is performed by heat, by which the moisture that was the cause of the fluid state gradually evaporates or exhales.

*Coagulation by comprehension* is when the whole matter is taken together and mixed into a uniform substance and coagulates.

*The Coagulum* <note>the curdling agent</note> *is Mercury.*

*Specific coagulum* is an earthy material essence, thickened into a dry consistency through coagulation.

*Coagulation* is fourfold, according to *Book 7 of The Nature of Things, page 452, Paracelsus, first volume* <note>original: *Paracel. pri: to:*; referring to the first volume of Paracelsus's collected works</note>.

*Coagulum, Mercury. Our Mercury.* <note>the refined, "philosophical" mercury used in alchemy</note>

*Coagulum, Coagulant, The Philosophers' Sulfur.*

<meta>catchword: first</meta>

<summary>This page defines coagulation as a central pillar of alchemy, detailing its various forms (hot, cold, segregation, and comprehension). It identifies Mercury and the Philosophers' Sulfur as the primary agents or "coagulants" responsible for bringing fluid matter into a solid state.</summary>

<keywords>Coagulation, Solution, Solve et Coagula, Paracelsus, Mercury, Philosophers' Sulfur, Sympexis, Alchemical operations</keywords>

-># CHEMICAL DICTIONARY. 49<-

...the first matter from which our gold is made. *Ferment of ferment* <note>original: *Fermentum fermenti*</note>, coagulating the spirit. *Sulfur coagulating Mercury*.

**Cobaſtoli**, is Ash.

**Smoke of Cobalts**, is Robolt <note>original: *Cobaltorum fumus, est Robolt*; likely referring to arsenic or cobalt ore residues known in German mining traditions</note>.

**Cocilio**, is a weight of 11 ounces.

**Heaven**, the essential forms of things. The *Quintessence* <note>original: *Quinta essentia*; the "fifth element" or the purest distilled essence of a substance</note>.

**Spagyric Heaven**, is the uppermost part of the Philosophical vessel <note>referring to the top of the distilling apparatus where vapors gather</note>.

**Heaven of the Philosophers**, is the name given to that which far transcends the nature of common elements, such as the Philosophers' Stone, the universal medicine, and every quintessence.

**Cœnum**, is mud or dung.

**Cement**, is a dry corrosion by which a certain metallic body, layered with corrosive salts and other drying things, is broken down and calcined <note>burnt to a powder</note>.

Furthermore, **Calcination** is performed in this way: The body to be cemented is reduced into thin plates and cut into small pieces with forceps. The corrosive species, however, are powdered and moistened with vinegar, urine, or *strong water* <term>aqua fortis <note>nitric acid</note></term>, so that they become like a paste (though some use these substances dry). Then, a certain portion of this paste or powder is placed into a cementing box, a plate is placed upon it, and another portion of the powder is sprinkled over the plate; this is called "stratification," or "layer upon layer" <note>original: *stratum super stratum*</note>. Another plate follows this, and so on until the box is filled or no plates remain; finally, it is closed with powder placed on top. The joints of the fortified box are blocked and secured with a tenacious clay, and it is placed in the fire or (what is better) in its own furnace. It is then "cemented" according to specific stages for 4, 6, 8, 12, or 24 hours; indeed, it may be kept there for several days, depending on the requirements of the work.

*For example:* When I wish to prepare **Saffron of Mars** <term>crocum Martis <note>a reddish powder of iron oxide used in medicines and pigments</note></term>, or steel, through cementation, I take as much fresh quicklime as...

<meta>catchword: work [opus]</meta>

<meta>This page continues the technical description of "Cementation" (dry calcination) and transitions into entries for blue pigments and the various methods of "Coloration" (dyeing and chemical tinting).</meta>

-># CHEMICAL DICTIONARY. 50<-

...it is necessary <note>completing the thought from the previous page regarding the preparation of cementing powders</note> and I moisten it for a while with male urine <note>A common source of nitrogen and salts in early chemistry</note>, so that it becomes like a paste. Afterward, I cover the bottom of a cementing box with this paste to the thickness of a finger's breadth, and I add plates of steel on top of it; I fill the vessel (as described above) and seal it with **lute** <term>lute <note>a clay or cement used to seal alchemical vessels airtight</note></term>. With everything duly prepared, I place the box in our cementing furnace and maintain a **fire of the third degree** <note>A moderate to high heat, often compared to a hot sand bath or glowing coals</note> continuously for 4 to 24 hours. Finally, having removed the vessel, I thoroughly crush the material contained within it in a mortar and pass it through a sieve so that it becomes a very fine powder. I then wash away all the lime from the steel with hot water, dry the residue, and I receive an excellent **Saffron of Mars** <term>Crocus Martis <note>A red-orange iron oxide used as a pigment or medicinal preparation</note></term>. In this same way, **Saffron of Venus** <term>Crocus Veneris <note>Copper oxide, typically red or green depending on the process</note></term> is also made, which can serve many chemical operations.

***Azure***, called *kyanos* <note>original Greek: κύανος</note> by the Greeks, and *caeruleum* by the Latins because of its color; it is **Lapis Lazuli**, a poisonous color, called *Lasur* by the Germans. The Arabs, who call it *Lasuli*, nearly imitate this name. **Rulandus** <note>Martin Ruland the Elder, author of a famous 1612 alchemical dictionary</note>, on page 161, lists 17 types, and continues on page 162.

***Coloration***, by the "drinking in" of an ingredient liquid, is a very frequent method of coloring through a liquid tincture which the objects to be colored are forced to drink or soak up. This is done according to their diverse nature. Those things that are porous are soaked in a liquid tincture, or even cooked together with it; otherwise, they are watered and drenched frequently, with drying periods in between, until the color is satisfactory. In this way, they commonly dye fleeces, linens, silks, cloths, and so on.

***External Coloration*** is when the color is intensified or improved only on the surfaces. This is done in various ways, by which the external face of the subject is arranged differently to reflect or limit light in various ways. From this, a different color arises, making the object more brilliant within its own kind—as if it were renewed—or by erasing the previous color to bring forth a more noble one.

***Coloration*** by heat alone is a method of coloring when [the color is brought] from potential to actual, or from the hidden to the manifest...

<meta>This page continues the definitions of chemical processes, concluding a section on the extraction of color and beginning a sequence of "C" entries, specifically focusing on various forms of lime (Calx) and their origins.</meta>

-># CHEMICAL DICTIONARY. 51<-

...the color is drawn forth, provided that only an altering heat is applied. This is done not only for the primary purpose of extraction, but it also serves for secret tinctures <note>arcane or medicinal liquid preparations</note>.

***Coloration*** through washing occurs when spiritual gloom and other impurities that darken or change the color are washed away with sharp waters <note>likely acidic or alkaline solutions used as solvents</note>; in this way, the color is brightened or exalted.

***Combustion*** is ignition, reducing bodies to **lime** <term>calx <note>the powdery residue remaining after a substance has been roasted or burned</note></term> by burning; it is either incineration or vitrification <note>the process of turning a substance into glass through heat</note>.

***Comisdi*** is Gum Arabic <note>a natural hardened sap used as a binder</note>.

***Composition*** is the joining together of different things. It is both a mixing and a bonding.

***Conder*** is Frankincense or Olibanum.

***Lime*** <note>original: *Calx*</note> in the chemical sense is any powder crushed into the finest and almost impalpable parts by the removal of superfluous moisture.

***Quicklime***, unslaked, or **asbestos** <note>derived from the Greek for "unquenchable," here referring to lime's reactive heat</note>, is made in various ways. In some places, it is made from stones or coastal pebbles of a blue-gray, ashen, or white color; these are either gathered from tunnels dug in coastal fields, simply collected on the shores, or even dug from its own mines, as occurs at Sula and other places. At the sea, it is made from oyster shells, the shells of sea snails, or from eggshells. Furthermore, **Quicklime** is of two types: white and blue. The power of all types is burning, fiery, and biting, inducing crusts; in short, it burns, disperses, and extracts. It naturally hates water, by which it is ignited, but loves oil, with which it is very easily mixed.

***Slaked Lime*** <note>original: *Calx Extincta*; lime that has been reacted with water to lose its caustic heat</note>.

***White glove-lime*** <note>original: *Calx manica alba*</note> is the white lime of pilgrims, made either from the bones of larger fish <margin>larger</margin>, or from white sea shells such as those they wear on their hats <note>referring to the scallop shells worn by pilgrims on the Way of St. James</note>.

***Roasted lime*** is alum made from apples <note>original: *Alumen ex pomis*. This may refer to a specific preparation of malic salts or a specialized alchemical reagent</note>.

-># CHEMICAL DICTIONARY. 52<-

**Camet** or **Cames** is silver <note>original: *argentum*</note>.

**Canales** are channels or tunnels <note>original: *cuniculi*</note>. To "open the channels" <note>original: *canales recludere*</note> or to "close the channels" <note>original: *canales occludere*</note>.

**Cancinpericon** is warm horse dung <note>original: *calidus equi fimus*; often used in alchemy as a source of gentle, steady heat for digestion or fermentation</note>.

**Canna** is a small tube or siphon <note>original: *syphunculus*</note>.

**Cantacon** is garden saffron <note>original: *crocus hortulanus*</note>.

**Capistrum auri** <note>literally "the muzzle of gold"</note> is **Borax** <note>a salt used as a flux to help metals melt and flow during soldering or refining</note>.

**Capsa** is a box with a bottom woven from iron wires.

**Canze**, **carmit**, **canna**, and **cusanum** are various types of vessels.

**Capsae** are containers into which veins <note>of ore</note> are thrown to be distributed and sorted.

**Capitellum** is soap-water <note>original: *aqua saponis*</note>.

**Caput mortuum** <term>dead head <note>The useless solid residue remaining in a flask after all the volatile spirits have been distilled or evaporated off</note></term> is dead earth or dregs. See Paracelsus <note>referred to here as Theophrastus</note>, page 28, in his work *On the Nature of Things*.

**Carbunculus** <note>a carbuncle; a red gemstone like a ruby or garnet</note> is taken to be a gem and takes its name from its similarity to fire, though it does not feel the fire itself; for this reason, it is called **apyrusta** <note>derived from the Greek *apyristos*, meaning "unaffected by fire"</note>. Martin Ruland <note>a prominent alchemical physician and lexicographer</note> lists six kinds of them on page 132. The **Males** possess a more brilliant flame that blazes in the sun; the **Females** shine more faintly.

**Carmiti** is an **obol** <note>original: *obulus*; a small unit of weight or a coin</note>.

**Carsia** is salt water <note>original: *aqua salis*</note>.

**Catillus ferreus** is an iron dish tempered from steel.

**Catinus arenarius** is a "sandy pot," an earthenware vessel shaped like a round hat with a rim three or four fingers wide, made from sticky clay.

**Catma** is gold filings <note>original: *limatura auri*</note>.

**Catrobil** is Earth.

**Catillus cinereus** <term>ash dish <note>Also known as a cupel, a porous vessel used in the process of cupellation to separate precious metals from lead or other impurities</note></term> is a melting vessel prepared in its own small mortar. It is made from the ashes of light wood that have been boiled and cleared of all charcoal and other impurities. It consists of two parts of those wood ashes and one part of ashes made from bones that lack marrow (taking care that these are not taken from pigs, which cause the greatest loss or failure <note>pig bone ash was considered too greasy, which could ruin the delicate process of metal separation</note>). These are ground together excellently and moistened into a mass with good beer, then thoroughly mixed. With the ashes...

<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for preparing assaying tools (specifically cupels) before moving into the alphabetical dictionary entries for the letter 'C', covering substances and processes from 'Cathochites' to 'Ceration.'</meta>

-># CHEMICAL DICTIONARY. 53<-

...once they have been moistened, this small mortar is filled, and the pestle (commonly called "the monk") <note>original: *monachus*</note> is driven into it with three strikes of a wooden mallet; finally, after sprinkling on ashes of calves' heads or stag’s horn through a hair sieve, one or two more strikes are added, and it is removed to dry. An ash-dish <term>cupel <note>A shallow, porous vessel used in assaying to separate precious metals from base metals like lead; the ashes absorb the base metals, leaving the pure silver or gold behind</note></term> is also made in a larger form, shaped in an iron ring.

**Cathochites**, just as the magnet <note>original: *magnes*</note> attracts iron, *sugda* wood, jet <note>original: *gagates*</note>, and amber <note>original: *succinum*</note> attract and hold straws: so the *cathochites* holds onto flesh and hands by an innate, very sticky gum or glue.

**Cardir** is tin <note>original: *Stannum*</note>.
**Cedria** is the gum of the Cedar tree.
**Cementatio** <term>Cementation <note>The process of heating a metal in contact with a powdered chemical agent—the "cement"—to change its properties, such as turning silver to gold or hardening steel</note></term> is a refinement of grade <note>original: *gradatio*</note> by means of cement.
**Cementum** <term>Cement <note>In this context, a dry, corrosive powder or paste used to refine or color metals</note></term> is a sharp and penetrating mineral material, with which metals are layered and then heated in a reverberatory furnace <note>an alchemical furnace where heat is reflected or "reverberated" onto the material from the roof</note> for the purpose of cementation. It is either simple or mixed, and takes the form of a powder or a paste.
**Cementare** or **to Stratify** <note>original: *Stratificiren*</note> is to place certain things in alternating layers.
**Centrum Ovi** <note>literally "center of the egg"</note> is the yolk of the egg.
**Cepa porci** <note>literally "pig's onion"</note> is the Squill <note>a sea onion used in various medicinal and chemical preparations</note>.
**Cerdac** is Quicksilver <note>original: *Mercurius*</note>.
**Ceracio** <term>Ceration <note>The process of softening a substance until it has a wax-like (cereal) consistency, often to prepare it for melting or further transformation</note></term> (which **Geber** <note>The Latinized name of the famous 8th-century polymath Jabir ibn Hayyan, often cited as the father of chemistry</note> defines) is the softening of a hard thing, or of a juice that cannot be melted, to the point of liquefaction. It is called *Ceracio* because it makes the "philosophical medicine" easily meltable, like wax <note>original: *cera*</note> brought near a fire. When the Philosophers wish to convert quicksilver into gold or silver, it is necessary that they have a flowing medicine which (to use Geber's words) suddenly adheres to it in its depths before it can flee <note>evaporate or sublimate</note>, joining with it in the smallest parts and thickening it; and by its own fixation, it preserves it in the fire until it can tolerate a greater heat, consuming its humidity...

<meta>catchword: ...consuming</meta>

<summary>This page defines various alchemical materials and processes, notably detailing the preparation of bone-ash cupels and the process of "ceration" used to soften substances to a wax-like state for transmutation.</summary>
<keywords>cupel, Cathochites, cementation, stratification, ceration, Geber, quicksilver, alchemical apparatus</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the alphabetical "C" section of the chemical dictionary, providing definitions for laboratory equipment, minerals, and weights. It maintains the technical focus on metallurgy and assaying seen on the previous page.</meta>

-># CHEMICAL DICTIONARY. 52<-

**Camet** or **Cames** is silver <note>original: *argentum*</note>.
**Canales** are channels or mine-tunnels. *To open the channels.* *To close the channels.*
**Cancinpericon** is warm horse dung <note>Used as a source of steady, gentle heat in alchemical digestion</note>.
**Canna** is a small pipe or siphon.
**Cantacon** is garden saffron <note>original: *Crocus hortulanus*</note>. **Capistrum auri** <term>Gold-halter <note>literally "the muzzle of gold"; a term for Borax, likely because it "restrains" or manages the flow of the metal during soldering or melting</note></term> is Borax.
**Capsa** is a box or case, the bottom of which is woven with iron wires.
**Canze, carmit, canna, cusanum** are various types of vessels.
**Capsæ** are boxes into which the ores to be sorted are cast.
**Capitellum** is soap-water <note>often referring to a strong lye or alkaline solution used in chemical processes</note>. **Caput mortuum** <term>Dead head <note>The useless chemical residue, dregs, or "earth" remaining after distillation or sublimation</note></term> is dead earth, or dregs. See Paracelsus <note>original: *Theophrastum*</note>, page 28, *On the Nature of Things*.
**Carbunculus** <term>Carbuncle <note>A deep-red gemstone, typically a ruby or garnet</note></term> is taken to mean a gemstone, and it derives its name from its resemblance to fire; since the stone itself is not affected by fire, it is called *apyrusta* <note>Greek: "unburnt" or fireproof</note>. Ruland <note>Martin Ruland the Elder (1532–1602), author of a definitive alchemical lexicon</note> lists six kinds of them on page 132. The "males" possess a brighter flame, glowing in the sun; "females" shine more dimly.
**Carmiti** is an obol <note>original: *obulus*; a small unit of weight or a coin</note>.
**Carsia** is salt water <note>brine</note>.
**Catillus ferreus** is an iron dish tempered with a steel edge.
**Catinus arenarius** <term>Sand-basin <note>An earthen vessel used for a "sand bath," allowing a chemist to heat a glass flask evenly by nesting it in heated sand</note></term> is an earthen vessel shaped like a round hat with a rim of three or four finger-widths, made from tough clay.
**Catma** are gold filings. **Catrobil** is Earth.
**Catillus cinereus** <term>Ash-dish <note>An assaying vessel, also known as a cupel, used to separate precious metals from base metals</note></term> is a melting vessel prepared in its own small mortar. It is made from two parts ashes of light wood (boiled and cleansed of all charcoal and other impurities) and one part well-ground bone-ashes lacking marrow—ensuring they are not taken from pigs, which cause the greatest loss <note>Pig bone ash was thought to be too greasy or porous, leading to the loss of precious metal during assaying</note>. These are moistened into a mass with good beer <note>original: *cervisia bona*; beer was frequently used as a binder to help the ash hold its shape</note> and thoroughly mixed together. With the ashes...

-># CHEMICAL DICTIONARY. 53<-

...for once they are moistened, this little mortar is filled, and the pestle (commonly called a "monk" <note>original: *monachus*</note>) is driven into it with three blows of a wooden mallet; finally, having sprinkled in ashes of calves' heads or stag's horn through a hair sieve, one or two more blows are added, and it is removed so that it may dry. A larger ash-cup <note>A cupel or small crucible made of bone ash used to separate precious metals from lead</note> is also made in a larger form, shaped within an iron ring.

***Cathochites***: Just as a magnet attracts iron, jet attracts wood, and jet <note>original: *gagates*</note> and amber attract chaff and hold them fast: so the *Cathochites* attracts and holds flesh and hands by an innate, extremely slow-flowing gum or glue.

***Cardir*** is Tin.

***Cedria*** is the gum or resin of the Cedar tree.

***Cementatio*** is the process of <term>gradation <note>A refining process intended to improve the quality, color, or "grade" of a metal</note></term> performed by means of "cement."

***Cementum*** <term>Cement <note>In alchemy, this refers not to building material but to a corrosive powder used to refine metals by eating away impurities when heated in layers</note></term> is a sharp and penetrating mineral matter with which metals, arranged in layers, are heated in a reverberatory furnace <note>original: *reverberantur*</note> for cementing. It is either simple or mixed, and takes the form of a powder or a paste.

***Cementare***, **To Stratify** <note>German: *Stratificiren*</note>, is to place certain things in alternating layers.

***Centrum Ovi*** <term>Center of the Egg</term> is the yolk of the egg.

***Cepa porci*** <term>Hog’s Onion</term> is the Squill <note>A sea onion or medicinal bulb</note>.

***Cerdac*** is Mercury <note>Quicksilver</note>.

***Ceracio*** <term>Ceration <note>From the Latin *cera*, meaning wax</note></term>: (**Geber** <note>The Latinized name of Jabir ibn Hayyan, the famous 8th-century Persian polymath and father of chemistry</note> defines this) as the softening of a hard substance, or of a juice that cannot be melted, to the point of liquefaction. It is called **Ceration** because it renders the "philosophical medicine" easily meltable, like wax held near a fire. When the Philosophers wish to convert quicksilver into gold or silver, it is necessary for them to have a "flowing medicine" which—to use the words of **Geber**—suddenly adheres to the mercury in its depths before it can escape <note>original: *ante fugam ejus*</note>, joining with it in its smallest parts and thickening it. By its own stability <note>original: *fixatione*</note> in the fire, it preserves the mercury until it can tolerate a greater heat, which consumes its moisture...

<meta>This page continues the alphabetical "C" section of the dictionary. -># CHEMICAL DICTIONARY. 54<-

...[proper]ty, and converts it by this artifice in a moment into a true gold-making and moistening substance <note>original: *solificum & humificum*</note>, according to that for which the medicine <note>In alchemy, "medicine" refers to the Elixir or Philosopher's Stone used to transform matter</note> was prepared. Moreover, **Ceration** <note>original: *Ceratio*; the process of softening a substance to a waxy consistency</note> occurs when a dry and hard body is drenched with its own moisture through repeated and frequent soaking. *Arnaldus* <note>Arnaldus de Villanova (c. 1240–1311), a celebrated medieval physician and alchemist</note> describes the sign of perfect ceration to us in the second book of his *Great Rosary*, chapter 27:

Namely, if the medicine, when cast upon a glowing hot plate, melts very quickly like wax without smoke. But I leave that to the *Philosophers* <note>A common term for alchemical practitioners</note>.

**To Wax** <note>original: *Cerare*</note> is to incorporate or mix.

**Brain of a Ram** <note>original: *Cerebrum arietis*</note>.

**Brain of an Ox** <note>original: *Cerebrum bovis*</note> is burnt tartar <note>Potassium carbonate, often called "salt of tartar," produced by burning the mineral crusts from wine barrels</note>. 

***Cerober*** is water.

**Ceruse** <note>original: *Cerusa*</note> is the rust of lead <note>White lead, or basic lead carbonate, used as a pigment</note>. It is known to everyone and retains its name in the apothecary shops. For a powder is made from either white or black lead. Others define ceruse as the rust or the ash of lead. If it is made from white lead, it is called "Spanish" and is exceptionally white. *Dioscorides* <note>A 1st-century Greek physician and botanist</note> teaches when it should be made in Book 5, Chapter 53. He also adds how it should be roasted until it becomes similar to <term>sandarac <note>Here referring to a red lead pigment or orange-red arsenic sulfide</note></term>, and how the ceruse is to be washed. The lightest of all ceruse is used for the whitening of women's skin. *Ovid, On Facial Cosmetics*. *Paracelsus* <note>original: *Para.*</note> has its composition.

***Chambar*** is Magnesia. 

***Chucef*** is a potter’s shard.

**Thunderstones** <note>original: *Ceraunia*; literally "lightning-stones," these were often prehistoric stone tools or fossilized belemnites believed to have fallen from the sky during storms</note>. They are found in Germany near rivers, such as the Elbe—*Ceraunian* gems similar to crystal, stained with a blue color and found in various shapes: sometimes like clubs, sometimes like pyramids, resembling a <term>sugar-loaf <note>original: *caput Zuccarinum*; a conical mass of refined sugar</note></term>. They are never found whole, perhaps due to the force of the river by which they are shaken out from the clouds and the impact upon the ground. They are said to induce sweet sleep, to be effective against lightning, and to be useful for winning battles and legal cases. *Solinus* <note>original: *Selinus*; a 3rd-century Roman geographer and collector of curiosities</note>, chapter 23, on *Ceraunia*.

<meta>This page continues the alphabetical "C" section of the chemical dictionary, following the discussion of ceration (waxing) and lead compounds. -># CHEMICAL DICTIONARY. 55<-

***Chalcitis*** <note>original: *Chalcitis*; a copper-bearing mineral often associated with copperas or vitriol</note> is, in one sense, a **Chalcitis stone**, which is to say a copper-bearing stone <note>original: *lapis ærarius*</note>, or **Marcasite**, or the very pyrites from which copper is smelted. *Macer* <note>Aemilius Macer, an ancient Roman poet/physician whose name was often attached to medieval herbals</note> and *Mesue* <note>Johannes Mesue, an influential 9th-century Persian physician</note> call this the "stone of the stars." Another meaning of **Chalcitis** is **capillary alum** <note>original: *alumen capillare*; a hair-like mineral formation of alum or vitriol</note> and copper-alum which grows together in pyrites. Hence, it is sometimes called "congealed alum." Thus **Dioscorides** <note>Pedanius Dioscorides (c. 40–90 AD), the preeminent Greek pharmacologist</note> says that *Chalcitis*—that is, alum—is burnt in the manner of *Chalcitis*. You may see more regarding **Chalcitis** in **Ruland** <note>Martin Ruland the Elder, author of a major alchemical dictionary published in 1612</note> on pages 141 and 142.

***Chalcute*** is burnt copper <note>original: *æs ustum*</note>. ***Chalcos*** is copper <note>original: *æs*</note>.

***Chambelech*** is the **Elixir** <note>The supreme alchemical substance capable of transmutation or healing</note>.

***Chaomantia*** <note>Chaomancy</note> is the art by which a prophecy or omen is taken from the air.

***Chelidonia*** <note>Swallow-wort or Celandine</note> is...

***Chelidonius lapis*** <note>Swallow-stone</note>, or the **Chelidonia Gem**, is found in the stomachs of swallows. These are taken from swallow chicks during the waxing moon, according to **Dioscorides**, book 2, chapter 47. Specifically, it is from those that were hatched in the first brood; when their stomachs are cut open, two small stones are found, about the size and shape of linseed, as I have seen myself. One appears black, the other reddish. Or, as **Dioscorides** would have it, one is varied in color and the other pure. **Ruland** says he has seen both black and reddish stones. The reddish or purple ones are said to be effective against insanity and epilepsy, and to win favor <note>original: *gratiam conciliare*</note>. The black ones calm anger, are effective against fevers and "bad humors" <note>An imbalance of the four bodily fluids—blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile—once thought to cause disease</note>, and are beneficial for the eyes.

***Cenigdam***, or ***cenigotam***, is a surgical instrument <note>a trepan or similar tool</note> with which the skull of the head is opened for those suffering from epilepsy. Also called ***Ceniplam***.

***Cinificatum*** means the same as **calcined** <note>Reduced to a powder or ash through intense heat</note>.

***Cerviculæ*** <note>literally "little necks"</note> is the spirit <note>the volatile essence or chemical extract</note> of the bone from a deer's heart <note>The *os de corde cervi*, a piece of cartilage found in a stag's heart that ossifies with age, highly prized in early modern medicine</note>.

***Chymus*** is dregs <note>original: *fæx*</note>. **Chymia** is dreg-like quality. ***Chymus*** also means a mass.

***Chiromantia*** <note>Palmistry or Chiromancy</note> according to **Paracelsus** <note>The controversial Swiss physician and alchemist (1493–1541)</note>, is not only considered regarding the lines of the hands, but also in other signs observed in the whole body, such as in the veins and channels,

Moreover, **Ceration** <note>original: *Ceratio*; the process of softening a substance to a wax-like consistency</note> occurs when a dry and hard body is drenched with its own moisture through repeated and frequent soaking <note>original: *imbibitio*</note>. **Arnold** <note>Arnaldus de Villa Nova (c. 1240–1311), a famous physician and alchemist</note> describes the sign of perfect ceration for us in the second book of his *Great Rosary*, chapter 27.

***Cerare*** <note>To wax</note> is to incorporate or to mix.

***Cerebrum arietis*** <note>Ram's brain; likely an alchemical code for a specific mineral or salt</note>.

***Cerober*** is water.

***Cerusa*** <note>Ceruse or white lead</note> is the rust of lead. It is known to everyone, and it retains its name in the pharmacies. For it is a powder made from lead, whether white or black. Others define it by saying that ceruse is the rust or the ash of lead. If it is made from white lead, it is called ***Spanish*** [white] and is brilliantly white. **Dioscorides** <note>A Greek physician and father of pharmacognosy</note> teaches how it ought to be made in Book 5, Chapter 53. He also adds how it may be roasted until it becomes similar to sandarac <note>original: *sandaracæ*; likely referring to red lead or realgar in this context</note>, and how the ceruse should be washed. The lightest ceruse of all is used for the "whiteness of women" <note>i.e., as a cosmetic face-whitener</note>. **Ovid**, in *On the Care of the Face*, and **Paracelsus** both provide its composition.

***Chambar*** is **Magnesia**. 

***Chucef*** is a potter's shard <note>original: *testa figuli*; a fragment of earthenware</note>.

***Ceraunia*** <note>Thunderstones; believed to fall from the sky during storms, often fossilized belemnites or ancient stone tools</note> are found in Germany near rivers, such as the Elbe. These **Ceraunite gems** are similar to crystal, but dyed with a blue color and found in diverse forms—sometimes like clubs, sometimes like pyramids, so that they represent a "sugar-loaf" <note>original: *caput Zuccarinum*</note>. They are never found whole, perhaps because of the force of the river by which they are shaken out from the clouds and their subsequent impact upon the earth. They are said to induce sweet sleep, to be effective against lightning strikes, and to be of value for winning battles and legal cases. **Selinus** <note>likely referring to the Roman geographer Solinus</note> discusses Ceraunites in Chapter 23.

<meta>This page continues the alphabetical "C" section of the chemical dictionary, following the entries for copper and swallow-stones from page 55. It focuses heavily on Paracelsian terminology, particularly the "C" terms related to hidden virtues and specific botanical-mineral correspondences.</meta>

-># CHEMICAL DICTIONARY. 56<-

<meta>Catchword: Citrinatio <note>The process of turning yellow, a stage in the alchemical Great Work</note></meta>

***Cineritium*** is a cement <note>a refining agent used in the "cementation" process</note> for gold or silver, which is called "royal" by some.

***Cherva*** is the spurge plant <note>original: *cataputia*; likely referring to *Ricinus communis* or similar purgative seeds</note>.

***Cheiri***, as used by Paracelsus, when stated plainly without any addition: if the discussion concerns minerals, it denotes **quicksilver** <note>mercury</note>; but if it concerns plants, it denotes plant flowers <note>specifically the Wallflower, *Cheiranthus cheiri*</note>. When it is found with an addition in this manner, **flower of cheiri**, if derived from silver it signifies the **white elixir**, or its **quintessence**, just as the **flower of anthos** <note>flower of rosemary</note> signifies the **red elixir** of gold.

***Cherio*** is the hidden, accidental power of external elements, and not the qualified nature of heat or cold.

***Cherionium*** is that in which nature cannot be altered; for instance, a **Crystal** hardened by nature cannot be melted like one which is manufactured by art.

***Cherubin*** is a celestial power and influence, a dominion and authority over all dominions and powers, proceeding from God onto the earth and over all men. One may read very extensively concerning this divine glory in the works of **Paracelsus**, in his expositions on the **Psalms of David**.

***Clissus*** <note>a Paracelsian term for the "vital power" or "complex of virtues" of a plant</note> is the hidden power of things that travels and returns to where it first emerged; just as the power of roots passes into the stalk and other parts that rise from them, and finally returns to the roots after autumn. There it remains hidden throughout the entire winter until the first spring and summer.

***Citrinulus*** is a pale crystal.

***Citrinulorum*** is a medicine made from **colcothar** <note>original: *colcothare*; the red iron oxide remaining after the distillation of sulfuric acid</note>, which is to say, **vitriol** <note>sulfate</note> that has been calcined <note>heated to high temperatures</note> and reduced to its alkali; this name is given by comparison to crystal, because of the pale transparency of this salt, which is very similar to clear crystal.

***Citrinula*** is **flammula** <note>Spearwort, a species of *Ranunculus*</note>, a herb very familiar to **Paracelsus**, as it is found throughout his writings.

<keywords>Paracelsus, Mercury, Elixir, Quintessence, Clissus, Colcothar, Vitriol, Alchemical Botanical terms, Mineralogy</keywords>

-># CHEMICAL DICTIONARY. 57<-

->E 3<-

***Citrinatio*** <note>Citrination</note> is a resuscitation <note>original: *resuscitatio*; in alchemy, this refers to the "yellowing" stage of the Great Work where the matter is brought back to a state of active growth or "life"</note>.

***Chybur***, or ***cibur***, is **Sulphur**. <note>See page 488 of Paracelsus's *On the Nature of Things*</note>.

***Chifir minerale*** is interpreted by some as **gold**, but I judge from what has preceded that it is any sulphur of a metallic kind.

***Cist***, or otherwise ***kist***, is a capacity of two measures of wine, or a measure and a half depending on the diversity of locations—approximately four pounds.

***Heavens of the planets***, according to **Paracelsus**, are their own proper orbits and spheres—not imaginary ones, whose movements are attributed to them by the infidels. These infidels, such as **Empedocles** and **Pliny** (the historian of natural philosophy), claimed to know the heavens as well as they could investigate "pagan conflagrations" <note>original: *Ethnicas flagrationes*; likely referring to non-Christian philosophical theories regarding elemental fire or volcanic activity</note> on earth. If they were ignorant of that which is on the earth, how could they judge that which is separated by such an immense distance, except through deceptive conjectures? Indeed, on account of their lack of faith, it was only just that they should share in no truth resulting from their speculations and imaginings. Since true visions of meditation are reserved by God for the faithful alone, false ones, by contrast, proceed from the enemy of truth and follow the speculations of the unfaithful, to whom that portion has fallen by lot.

***Cohoph***, a term very familiar to **Paracelsus**, signifies the repetition of distillations, where the liquor drawn from the dregs is returned to them repeatedly; this liquor is either the material's own natural liquid or an added vehicle <note>a process often called "cohobation" or redistillation to concentrate the essence</note>.

***Cohos*** is everything that the skin encloses in the entire human body, made as an allusion to **chaos** <note>the primordial, unformed state of the universe</note>. Now that this chaos has been divinely brought into order—and is no longer formless as it was before—it encompasses all things that were created for the benefit of the "lesser ordered chaos," or *cohos* <note>Paracelsus refers to the human body as the Microcosm, a smaller version of the ordered universe</note>.

***Chelonitos*** <note>Likely referring to a "turtle-stone" or a specific mineral formation</note>: Near the village which is now vulgarly called **Calpu**, where there is the source of a certain spring rushing into...

<meta>catchword: Albim,</meta>

<summary>This page defines alchemical stages like Citrination, clarifies Paracelsian terms for sulphur and measurements, and presents a theological argument that true scientific insight (vision) is granted only to the faithful, contrasting Paracelsus with pagan philosophers like Pliny.</summary>
<keywords>Citrination, Sulphur, Chifir minerale, Cist, Paracelsus, Empedocles, Pliny the Elder, Cohoph, Cohobation, Cohos, Microcosm, Chaos</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the alphabetical "C" section of the "Lexicon Chymicum." It moves from the previous page's philosophical discussion of planetary spheres and faith into specific mineralogical and chemical definitions, focusing on the "turtle-stone" (Chelonitis) and the fundamental definition of Chemistry according to Paracelsian thought.</meta>

-># CHEMICAL DICTIONARY. 58<-

The **Elbe**, a most pleasant and fertile place, is where the **turtle-stone** <note>original: *chelonitis*; a fossil or gemstone named for its resemblance to a tortoise shell or its legendary origin within the animal</note> is found in various species. First, there are those whose shape entirely represents sea snails; there is also another form of the turtle-stone, namely an oblong one resembling a turned water-shell, which is found here and there along rivers, such as the Elbe. These turtle-stones seem to have the power of **bone-setters** <note>original: *ostracitis*; stones thought to have healing properties for bones or resembling shells</note>. Sometimes the turtle-stone is perfect and solitary; sometimes it is barely formed; sometimes it is perfect but in such a way that it adheres in part to another stone. Occasionally two or three, perfect or imperfect, are joined together. Sometimes they are the size of a large bean; sometimes they are so small they escape the sight; sometimes they are tawny, white, and shining in the manner of an **onyx**; and sometimes they are black with remarkable lines expressed in the manner of a sea snail.

A whitish turtle-stone is also found, like an onyx with certain small drops. Others maintain that the turtle-stone is found in the head of a tortoise. But **Pliny** <note>Gaius Plinius Secundus, the Roman naturalist</note> calls that stone *Chelonia*, and says it is the eye of the **Indian tortoise**.

***Chemistry*** <note>original: *Chemia*</note> is the art of separating essences from any mixture and preparing the **magisteries** <note>original: *magisteria*; high-level alchemical preparations where the full substance is transformed rather than just extracting an essence</note> of craftsmen. It teaches one to remove that which diminishes, detains, or impedes a stage of progress; it teaches how to separate the false from the legitimate and to bring things to perfection. **Theophrastus** <note>Paracelsus</note>, in the third book of the *Paragranum*. It directs cooked and prepared things more easily than raw and imperfect ones. Some derive the name from the Greek *to pour* <note>original: χέεσθαι (*cheesthai*)</note>. Why? Because it liquefies all bodies, as is evident from what has been said.

***Christ-food*** <note>original: *Christi-pabulum*</note> is the urine of a virgin.
***Chroma*** is color. ***Chrysocalcos*** is gold-brass <note>original: *Auri chalcum*; a golden-colored copper or bronze alloy</note>.
***Chrysos***, or ***Chrysion***, is **gold**.
***Feeding*** <note>original: *Cibatio*</note> is **incorporation** <note>original: *corporatio*; the alchemical process of giving "body" to a spirit or thickening a substance</note>.
***Castor Oil*** <note>original: *Cicinum Oleum*</note> is oil of radish <note>original: *Rhaphanticum*</note>.
***Cinnabar***: The ancients called true cinnabar **dragon's blood**, and it still retains that name. **Rulandus** <note>Martin Ruland the Elder, a physician and alchemist known for his 1612 Lexicon</note>
->agrees<-

-># CHEMICAL DICTIONARY. 59<-

[The author] agrees with those who maintain that cinnabar is a juice, or rather the "tear" of some herb or tree, as indicated by its very taste and flavor. They say that there is a tall tree in **Libya** and neighboring regions from which that **dragon’s blood** <note>original: *draconis sanguis*; a red resin obtained from certain plants, often confused in antiquity with the mineral cinnabar</note> flows; just as in the **Rhaetian Alps** resin flows from sap, and among the **Germans** from the pitch-pine and fir during the springtime, which **Italian** apothecaries sell as turpentine resin, whereas true **Turpentine** is said to be similar in color to **Chian Mastic** <note>a high-quality resin from the mastic tree on the island of Chios</note>. **Platearius** <note>Johannes Platearius, a 12th-century medical writer from the School of Salerno</note> also feels that dragon's blood is the juice of a tree or herb in **India** or **Persia**.

***Metallic Cinnabar*** is ***Minium*** <note>red lead or cinnabar; the terminology for red pigments was often interchangeable in early chemistry</note>, whether ***Artificial*** or ***Native***.

**Theophrastus** <note>the Greek "father of botany" and successor to Aristotle</note> calls that true **Minium** "Cinnabar" because of the similarity in color, as do other **Greeks** (not because it is truly cinnabar, as **Dioscorides** and **Pliny** also maintain in their cited books). Finally, since native red lead is red like ***sandarac*** <note>original: *sandaracha*; here referring to red arsenic ore (realgar), not the modern wood resin</note>, note the difference between them. From red lead, whether processed or raw, **quicksilver** <note>original: *argentum vivum*; mercury</note> is often produced, which cannot be done at all from sandarac. In fact, while a vein of red lead is being washed so that it may later be ground, part of it is changed into quicksilver; and this, once dried and crushed again, returns to its original color. And just as we speak of native and artificial red lead, so by its application or use it is called ***Sinopic red earth*** <note>original: *rubrica sinopica*; a high-quality red pigment from Sinope on the Black Sea</note> or ***smith's red earth*** <note>original: *rubrica fabrilis*; common red ochre used by craftsmen</note>.

**Artificial**, which is prepared from quicksilver and sulfur by **Chemists**; among **Germans**, this artificial variety is called common **Cinnabar**. There is also another adulterated red lead, other than that which they call cinnabar, which today alone retains the name of "minium" and is made from lead. There is also a third adulterated kind, found in silver and lead ores, produced from a certain burnt stone found in the veins.

***Incineration*** <note>original: *cinefactio*; the chemical process of reducing a substance to ash</note> is the process by which ashes are made, although sometimes—

<meta>This page continues the alphabetical "C" section of the "Lexicon Chymicum." It picks up from the previous discussion of red pigments and minerals (cinnabar, minium), shifting now into the definitions of "Ashes" (Cinis) and the alchemical process of "Circulation" (Circulatio).</meta>

->### CHEMICAL DICTIONARY. 60<-

...just as both metallic soot and Alkali are called "ashes," yet the term properly applies to things that are combustible or flammable. The remains of these burnt things are properly called ashes.

*Ivy ash* <note>original: *Cinis hederæ*</note> is **Potash** <note>original: *Cinis Clavellatus*; literally "ash of small nails," referring to the crude potassium carbonate obtained from wood ashes</note>. *Potash* is Alkali.

*Ash from ash* is an extracted water. *Ash of ash* is a congealed coagulant.

*Vine-shoot ash* <note>original: *Cinis sarmentitius*</note> is that ash useful not only to Physicians—called the "Leaven of leaven"—and to Surgeons, but also to Chemists. It contains the power of salt but in a milder form. It corrodes fleshy growths, etc.

The *vein of ash* belongs to the Ash tree <note>Latin: *Fraxinus*</note>.

**Circulation** <note>original: *Circulatio*</note> is the exaltation of a pure liquid through circular solution and coagulation in a **Pelican** <note>a specialized alchemical flask with two side-arms that feed vapors back into the body of the vessel, allowing for continuous distillation</note> by the action of heat. This applies only to liquid things that can be thinned or resolved into "air" [vapor] and return from there in turn to a liquid state; it primarily follows the extractions by which essences are made. **For example:** Much labor and study is spent on the true extraction of **spirit of wine** <note>ethanol/alcohol</note> so that it can be obtained without any **phlegm** <note>original: *phlegmate*; the watery, inactive residue left after distillation</note>. For as often as it is raised up, it always carries some phlegm with it. Therefore, we shall demonstrate the method of circulation using this as an example.

The most noble wine that can be found is taken and poured into a spacious circulatory vessel. With the opening sealed, it is placed in a **bain-marie** <note>original: *balneo maris*; a water bath used to provide gentle, uniform heat</note> for 14 days, so that the water boils continuously. Afterward, it is removed and poured into a vial. While it is still warm, if it is wintertime, it is placed in cold snow-water with an **alembic** <note>the cap or "head" of a distillation apparatus</note> and receiver attached and sealed with paste; in summer, however, it is placed in a cellar in a vessel containing ice shells. The pure spirit, free from all phlegm, will transcend the alembic and slip down into the receiver. When nothing further as-

<meta>This page continues the "C" section of the "Lexicon Chymicum." It concludes the discussion of the distillation of spirit of wine (alcohol) from the previous page and proceeds through definitions of chemical vessels and processes including comminution, melting, and the etymology of "Chemistry" itself.</meta>

->### CHEMICAL DICTIONARY.<-
->61<-

...ascends <note>completing the word from the previous page</note>, the receiving vessel is removed, and the vial is taken out with the wine which contains no true spirit, but rather **burnt wine** <note>original: *vinum adustum*; an early term for brandy or distilled spirits</note> (as *Theophrastus* <note>Theophrastus Bombastus von Hohenheim, better known as Paracelsus, a foundational figure in Swiss-German alchemy and medicine</note> calls it). This **spirit of wine** <note>ethanol</note>, still placed in the circulatory vessel, is rotated until it can no longer be poured from glass to glass, but must be moved forward through distillation or "moist elevation" <note>vaporization</note>. However, when the material upon which it is meant to act is placed in the receiver, it easily embraces it and abandons its flight <note>meaning the spirit ceases to be volatile once it reacts with or dissolves the target substance</note>.

*Circulatorium* (**Circulatory vessel**), is a glass vessel where the infused liquid, by ascending and descending, is rotated as if in a circle. There are various types of these vessels, but two are of the greatest importance and use: specifically the **Pelican** <note>a flask with arms that return vapors to the base</note> and the **Diota** <note>a two-handled vessel resembling an ancient Greek vase</note>.

*Circulus* (**Circle**), is a round instrument made of iron for cutting glass vessels. They are broken in this way: the red-hot iron circle is applied to the glass and grips it until it grows hot; then, while it is still glowing, a drop of cold water is applied, or a blast of cold air, and the glass cracks and breaks apart.

*Comminutio* (**Comminution**), is when we reduce a thing into very tiny parts by striking it against something <note>pulverization</note>. Drying, roasting, and sifting are sometimes useful for this. Since not all things can be thinned to a very fine powder at once, the fine parts must be shaken through a narrow sieve, and the work repeated with the coarser bits.

*Colliquatio*, *colliquefactio* (**Colliquation**), is the joining of several meltable things in the fire into a single compound through fiery melting.

*Colophonia* (**Colophony**), is "Greek pitch," the gum of the pine or resin.

*Clareta* (**Clareta**), is egg white <note>original: *Albumen ovorum*</note>, also called the white of the egg, *leucon* of the egg, or *Leucoma*.

*Chymia* (**Chemistry**), comes from the Greek *apo to chyo* <note>original: $A\pi\grave{o}$ $\tau\tilde{\omega}$ $\chi\acute{\upsilon}\omega$</note>, meaning "to pour" or "to melt." Among the ancient chemists, **coagulation** is also understood to be included under the term **solution**; whence...
