<meta>This page continues the definitions under the letter "C" in the Lexicon Chymicum. The text begins mid-sentence, concluding a definition (likely of Alchemy or Chemistry) before moving through terms like Clepsydria, Clyssus, and celestial virtues.</meta>

->### CHEMICAL DICTIONARY.<-
->62<-

...that common saying: **Dissolve and coagulate** <note>original: *Solve & coagula*; the central maxim of alchemy, referring to the process of breaking down a substance to its essence and then reforming it</note>. The **Art of Chemistry** <note>original: *Ars Chymia*</note> is the physical art of separating the pure from the impure to prepare medicines, adapted both for healing human bodies and for bringing metallic bodies to their highest perfection.

*Clepsydria* <note>from the Greek for 'water-thief,' traditionally a water clock</note>, is the drawing off of a liquid through the "eye" <note>opening</note> of a solid vessel perforated at the bottom. This process was formerly used in time-keeping machines, from which it takes its name; although not only water is drawn off, but also oil and the melts of minerals. Metallurgists call the hole through which the material is drawn out of their furnace the "eye," which is why this term is appropriate, although in its place they may use small tubes, spigots, notches, channels, etc.

*Clarum* [Clear], is that which is made from Crystal.

*Climia*, *Ereps*, is the **calamine** <note>original: *Cathmia*; likely refers to the dross or oxide formed in the furnace</note> of **orpiment** <note>original: *auripigmenti*; arsenic trisulfide, used as a yellow pigment and in alchemical transformations</note>.

*Clyssus*, is a composite species made from various forms of the same substance processed separately.

*Clyssus* can encompass the entire essence of a thing, since, after the impurities and dregs are cast away, whatever is essential in it is reduced to a single compound. Or, it is the extraction of the subtle parts of all segments of a plant coming together into one common existence. It can be joined in two ways: either so that all essences extracted from different parts are joined and incorporated; or so that oil is in one **cucurbit** <note>a gourd-shaped glass flask used in distillation</note>, salt in a second, and liquid in a third, and thus they are distilled through a common channel using a single **alembic** <note>the 'cap' or head of a still</note> and thereby united. But this does not succeed so easily. For waters and salts do not ascend <note>vaporize</note> in the same way. Therefore, this might be done with different oils and waters.

*Celestial bodies of the Spagyrists* <note>original: *Corpora cæleſtia Spagyrorum*; Spagyry is the Paracelsian term for the alchemical process of separating and recombining</note>, are the astral virtues <note>powers or influences</note> within their own matter.

*Chrysei*, are stars which emit bright yellow rays.

*Chrysite* <note>original: *Chriſites*</note>, is a precious gemstone mentioned by **Pliny** <note>Pliny the Elder, Roman naturalist</note>, in *Book 37, Chapter 10*.

->### CHEMICAL DICTIONARY.<-
->63<-

**Chrysites** is the scale of gold, or gold itself, which comes from tested and melted lead.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;**Chrysite** is a golden earth in which small particles of gold are mixed with small parts of the earth.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;**Chrysocolla** <note>from the Greek for "gold-glue," historically referring to various green copper minerals like malachite used in soldering gold</note> is a green earth. To the Greeks and Latins it is *Chrysocolla*, as if it were the glue of gold. It is of two types: Natural and Artificial. **Dioscorides** <note>a Greek physician and botanist of the 1st century AD</note>, in Book 5, Chapter 54, dealing with the natural variety, gives first place to the **Armenian**, second to the **Macedonian**, and third to the **Cyprian**. Excellent varieties are found in many places in **Dacia** <note>modern-day Romania</note> and **Germany**. It is mined in **Hungary**, **Burgundy**, and at **Goldberg** in **Silesia**, and from here gold is smelted. Some call this natural kind **borax**, though it is not the "borax stone" found in toads <note>original: *lapis borax de bufonibus*; the mythical "toadstone" believed to be found in the heads of old toads and used as an antidote to poison</note>. Chrysocolla could be called "green fissile" <note>original: *viride scissile*; a green stone that can be split</note>, just as **verdigris** <note>original: *aerugo aeris*</note> is called "scraped copper-green." **Pliny** <note>the Roman author of Natural History</note>, in Book 33, Chapter 5, defines natural Chrysocolla as a liquid found in wells flowing through a vein of gold; it thickens from slime during winter frosts until it hardens to the consistency of pumice. The most praised comes from copper mines, the next from silver mines, the third from gold, and the fourth from lead. **Dioscorides** lists the "artificial" kind among the simple rusts of copper, which goldsmiths use and which physicians used in former times. This natural kind is formed from the urine of infants, and this is called **tincar** <note>an old term for crude borax</note> and **borax**. Thus these metals are related: natural copper rust, which is called "scraped copper-green," and Chrysocolla, which is also called "scraped copper-green." Natural Chrysocolla has the power of dispersing and eroding [flesh]. Excellent gold is smelted from natural Chrysocolla and blue pigment; note that in that stone where there is green earth, there is also **kyanon** <note>original: *κύανον*; a deep blue mineral, likely azurite or lapis lazuli</note>. A third substance, white and shining in the manner of sparks, is what they call **mica**, or **cats' silver** <note>original: *felium argentum*; likely muscovite or weathered mica, which glitters like silver but is worthless</note> among miners. It has the color of silver, but it is not silver. **Serapio** <note>likely Serapion the Younger, a 12th-century Arabic medical writer</note> says that natural Chrysocolla, which is found on the shores of the sea, is a species of "hot salt" and is dry in the fourth degree.

->### CHEMICAL DICTIONARY.<-
->64<-

It is called Azoth, Rebis, sulfur water, spiritual wine <note>original: *vinum animeum*; likely referring to a highly distilled or "ennobled" spirit</note>, Flower, etc. **Ruland** <note>Martin Ruland the Elder, author of a prominent 1612 alchemical lexicon</note> on page 148 lists six species of **Chrysocolla** <note>literally "gold-glue"; used historically to describe various green copper minerals like malachite or borax used in soldering</note>.

**Chrysocolla** is poisonous if taken in more than the proper dose, according to **Dioscorides** <note>an ancient Greek physician and pharmacologist</note>; but physicians use it cautiously to facilitate childbirth.

**Chloretes**, a gemstone: According to **Pliny** <note>the Roman author of Natural History</note>, it is a precious stone of a green color, like grass.

**Coaspites**, a gemstone: It takes its name from the **Choaspes** river, according to **Pliny**, Book 37, Chapter 10. It is a precious gemstone.

**Concha** <note>shell</note>, or glass **Alveolus** <note>small basin</note>, is a vessel, usually made of glass, shaped like a belly <note>original: *alvus*; referring to the rounded body of a flask</note>, with a mouth and a handle.

**Ethiopia** produces the **Chrysolite** <note>literally "gold-stone"; in the ancient world, this often referred to yellow topaz or peridot</note> gemstone together with the **Hyacinth** <note>a term used for various blue or reddish stones, often zircon or sapphire</note>, etc. It is translucent with a golden color, from which it takes its name. **Pliny** says it is of a pale color by day, and fiery at night. **Albertus** <note>Albertus Magnus, the 13th-century philosopher and theologian</note> says that the golden-colored Chrysolite appears most beautiful in the morning hours; at other hours it is not so beautiful and unlike itself. It is corrupted and driven away by fire, because it is ignited by it. He also considers a species of it to be the golden **Marcasite**, which he also calls another stone of blue and red color.

The power of **Chrysolite** is to stir up good spirits, to oppose melancholy and nocturnal spirits <note>original: *lemuribus*; referring to ghosts or night-terrors</note>, to cure scabies and ulcers, and to soothe the heat <note>original: *colorem*; literally "color," but here referring to the flush or intensity</note> of fever.

**Chlorosis**, a disease, which among us is called the "virgin's disease," or **The green sickness** <note>a historical diagnosis for a type of anemia that caused a greenish tint to the skin</note>.

**Cheras**, also known as **struma**, is a swelling in which small glands arise. It is also called **scrofula** <note>a tubercular infection of the lymph nodes in the neck</note>.

**Coloritium** is a gradation of color achieved through a sharp paste <note>likely a corrosive or acidic mixture used in metallurgy</note>, which they also call *coleritium*. Thus, it is a certain method of dyeing primarily intended for metals. It is only effective on the surface, for it cannot penetrate deeply.

<meta>This page continues the entries for the letter 'C', specifically concluding a discussion from the previous page before detailing "Colcothar," its alchemical preparation, and a satirical critique of medical fraudsters. It then moves to "Colic."</meta>

->### CHEMICAL DICTIONARY.<-
->65<-

...it cannot [be used] on account of its thickness, unless it is diluted with a copious liquid feeding, and thus changed into the form of a lye <note>original: *lixivium*; a solution obtained by leaching wood ashes, used in chemistry and soap making</note>.

**Colcothar** <note>the reddish-brown residue, primarily iron oxide, left after the distillation of green vitriol (ferrous sulfate)</note>, according to **Paracelsus** <note>Theophrastus von Hohenheim, the 16th-century Swiss-German physician and alchemist</note>, is properly understood as vitriol and the serpent, or the green lizard that has devoured its own tail. This is because it is fixed by its own water in this manner, namely: all its water is drawn off from it through distillation; this water is then returned to its own ground "dead head" <note>original: *caput mortuum*; the solid remains of a substance after all volatile parts have been removed by heat</note> once it has been crushed, and is poured back over it. It is drawn off as before and poured back again, repeating this process of <term>cohobation</term> <note>the process of repeatedly distilling a liquid from its solid residue to concentrate its "essence"</note> so many times until it reaches the point where it yields no more water, even if it is pushed by a more severe fire. 

More commonly, it is understood as the **dead head** <note>original: *caput mortuum*</note> that has been turned red from vitriol in a simple way without cohobation; this is also called "red ink" by some. And it is ironically called by **Paracelsus** "**Red Henry**" <note>original: *Henricus rubeus*</note>, in mockery of certain fraudulent surgeons and traveling charlatans <note>original: *circulatores*; literally "those who travel in circles," referring to peddlers of fake medicine</note> who attempted to cure all ulcers and wounds with this medication alone. They leaned on this single remedy as if it were a "sacred anchor" <note>a Latin idiom for a last resort or ultimate hope</note>, losing their patients rather than saving them. 

Nor are there lacking even now those who, in this time, put burnt vitriol, sulfur, and alum up for sale at a high price throughout almost all the markets—things they bought for a pittance. They preach it as a "holy stone" brought from distant and overseas regions, when it actually comes from the nearest apothecary. With it, all ulcers and abscesses are supposedly cured; especially the "ulcer" in the merchant's purse swells up again into an "abscess" filled with the peasants' small coins. Thus the world desires to be eternally deceived, so that, warned by no example or danger, it may never return to its senses. Let it therefore have what it seeks.

**Colic** is a dissolved **tartar** <note>in the medical system of Paracelsus, "tartar" refers to stony or hardened metabolic wastes that cause various diseases</note>, a disease of the intestines fixed in the neck of the intestine.

->**Colic, a disposition.**<- <note>original: *diathesis*; a physical condition or constitution</note>

**Colcritium** is a liquid composed of corrosive me- <note>The entry cuts off here and continues on the next page.</note>

<summary>This page defines Colcothar and describes the alchemical process of cohobation, while also delivering a scathing critique of medical charlatans who overcharge for common chemical residues. It concludes with a brief Paracelsian definition of Colic as a metabolic "tartar."</summary>
<keywords>Colcothar, Paracelsus, vitriol, cohobation, caput mortuum, Red Henry, medical fraud, Colic, tartar</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the alphabetical entries for the letter 'C' in this chemical dictionary, beginning with synonyms for a substance (likely Chrysocolla or a form of Mercury) and proceeding through various gemstones and medical conditions before describing a method for coloring metals.</meta>

->### CHEMICAL DICTIONARY.<-
->64<-

It is called **Azoth** <note>the "universal medicine" or "universal solvent" sought by alchemists, often symbolized by mercury</note>, **Rebis** <note>literally "the double thing"; an alchemical symbol representing the union of opposites, often depicted as a hermaphroditic figure</note>, **Water of Sulfur**, **Wine of the Soul** <note>original: *vinum animatum*; a term for a potent alchemical spirit or solvent</note>, **Flower**, etc. **Ruland** <note>Martin Ruland the Elder (1532–1602), a German physician and author of a famous alchemical lexicon</note> on page 148 lists six species of **Chrysocolla** <note>literally "gold-glue"; historically used to refer to borax or green copper minerals like malachite used in soldering</note>.

**Chrysocolla** is poisonous if taken in more than a proper dose according to **Dioscorides** <note>a 1st-century Greek physician and botanist whose *De Materia Medica* was a standard medical text for centuries</note>; however, doctors use it cautiously to facilitate childbirth.

***Cloretes***, a gemstone; according to **Pliny** <note>Pliny the Elder, a Roman naturalist whose 1st-century encyclopedia described various minerals</note>, it is a precious gem of green color, like grass.

***Coaspites***, a gemstone; it received its name from the River Choaspes <note>a river in modern-day Iran</note> according to **Pliny**, Book 37, Chapter 10. A precious gem.

***Concha*** <note>literally "shell"</note>, or a glass trough, is a vessel usually made of glass, shaped in the manner of a belly <note>original: *alvis*; likely meaning a rounded basin or vat</note>, with a mouth and a handle.

The gemstone ***Chrysolite*** <note>from the Greek for "golden stone"; in antiquity, this often referred to yellow gems like topaz, though today it refers to peridot</note> is sent from Ethiopia along with the hyacinth <note>likely zircon or sapphire in historical contexts</note>, etc.; it is translucent with a golden color, from which it takes its name. **Pliny** says it is of a pale color by day and fiery by night. **Albertus** <note>Albertus Magnus (c. 1200–1280), a medieval scholar known for his writings on minerals</note> says that the golden-colored chrysolite appears most beautiful in the morning hours, while at other hours it is not so fair and unlike itself; he also says it is destroyed and driven away by fire, because it is inflamed by it. He also considers a species of it to be the golden **Marcasite** <note>an iron sulfide mineral with a metallic luster</note>, which he also calls another stone of blue and red color.

The power of **Chrysolite** is to excite good spirits, to oppose melancholy and nighttime ghosts <note>original: *lemuribus*; restless spirits or specters</note>, to cure scabies and ulcers, and to mitigate the heat of fever.

***Chlorosis***, a disease, which among us is called the "virgin's disease," or **the green sickness** <note>a historical diagnosis for a form of anemia in young women, named for the pale or greenish tint of the skin</note>.

***Cheras***, also called ***struma***, is a tumor in which glands arise. It is also called **scrofula** <note>a form of tuberculosis affecting the lymph nodes of the neck</note>.

***Coloritium*** is a gradation of color achieved through a sharp paste <note>original: *pastam acutam*; an acidic or corrosive mixture used to treat metal surfaces</note>, which they also call *coleritium*. Therefore, it is a certain method of dyeing primarily intended for metals. It is only effective on the surface, for it cannot penetrate deep...

<meta>This page finishes a sentence regarding the dilution of a substance (likely the gemstone Chrysolite mentioned on the previous page) and begins the entries for "Colcothar" and "Colic." It contains a notable critique of medical fraud and the use of alchemical symbolism.</meta>

->### CHEMICAL DICTIONARY.<-
->65<-

...can, because of its thickness <note>original: *corpulentiam*; referring to its density or lack of fluidity</note>, unless it is diluted with a copious liquid food, and thus changed into the form of a lye <note>a liquid solution of salts, often caustic</note>.

**Colcothar** <term>the metallic residue—usually iron oxide—left after the distillation of vitriol</term>, according to **Paracelsus** <note>Philippus Aureolus Theophrastus Bombastus von Hohenheim (1493–1541), a Swiss physician who revolutionized medicine by using chemistry</note>, is properly understood as **vitriol** <note>a sulfate mineral</note> and the **serpent**, or green lizard, which has devoured its own tail. This signifies a substance that has been "fixed" by its own water in the following way: its entire water is drawn off through distillation and then returned to its ground-up **caput mortuum** <term>literally "dead head"; the dregs or solid residue left after a chemical process</term> and poured back on. It is distilled off and poured back again and again through **cohobation** <term>the process of repeatedly redistilling a liquid over its own residue to concentrate its essence</term>, until it reaches a point where it yields no more water, no matter how strongly it is urged by the fire.

More commonly, it is also understood as the *caput mortuum* made red from vitriol in a simple way without cohobation, which some also call "red ink." And it is ironically called by **Paracelsus** "**Red Henry**," in mockery of certain fraudulent surgeons and **charlatans** <note>original: *circulatorum*; itinerant healers or traveling peddlers of medicines</note> who attempted to cure all ulcers and wounds with this medicine alone. They relied on this single remedy as if it were a "sacred anchor" <note>an ancient Greek and Roman metaphor for the largest anchor on a ship, used only as a last resort in a storm</note>, losing their patients rather than saving them.

Nor are there lacking even now those who, in these times, put out for sale at a high price burnt vitriol, sulfur, and alum at almost every fair, though they bought them for a pittance. They proclaim it a "**Holy Stone**" brought from distant and overseas lands—or rather, from the nearest apothecary shop—by which all ulcers and abscesses are cured. In truth, the merchant’s own "ulcer" in his coin-purse swells again into an "abscess" filled with the small coins of peasants. Thus the world desires to be deceived for all time, so that it never recovers its senses, warned by no example or danger. Let it have, therefore, what it seeks <note>original: *Mundus vult decipi, ergo decipiatur*; a Latin proverb meaning "The world wants to be deceived, so let it be deceived"</note>.

**Colic** is dissolved **tartar** <note>in Paracelsian medicine, "tartar" refers to stony or crystalline deposits in the body, like kidney stones or gouty crystals, that cause disease</note>, a disease of the intestines fixed in the neck of the intestine. *Colic diathesis* <note>a physical predisposition or constitutional state leading to colic</note>.

**Colcritium** is a liquid composed of corrosive metals...

<meta>This page continues the dictionary entries from the previous page, completing a description of gold-testing materials and proceeding through terms beginning with 'C', including Complexion and Strengthening Medicines.</meta>

->### CHEMICAL DICTIONARY.<-
->66<-

...of metals with the most hostile materials, by which gold is tested by rubbing it against a **touchstone** <note>original: *lydium lapidem*; a dark stone used to assay the purity of precious metals</note>, and which no metal except gold alone can resist. From this, it is immediately recognized if any other metal is mixed with the gold; all such mixed material soon vanishes, changing color in that part where the coloring liquid has been applied, while the other parts not moistened preserve the color of the rubbed gold. However, that which is pure and clean is changed not at all by that liquor.

**Cometz** is half a drop.

**Complexion** is the nature of the father. In another sense, it is the quality of heat or cold: the former has a moist nature innate to it, while the latter has a dry nature. Therefore, there are two complexions, and not four <note>This challenges the traditional Galenic view of four humors/complexions—sanguine, choleric, melancholic, and phlegmatic—reducing them to a binary system</note>. For heat, by its own simple and unmixed nature, is always dry; and if it sometimes admits moisture, it is borrowed from cold—just as the latter receives dryness from the former, which it occasionally takes on accidentally.

**Firmament** <note>original: *Confirmamentum*</note> is the body of the star in man, or the sidereal part in man, or the astral body <note>A Paracelsian concept suggesting that humans possess an internal "heaven" or "star" that connects them to the cosmos</note>.

**Strengthening medicines** <note>original: *Confortativa* or *confortantia*</note> are medications that corroborate the hearts and the nature of men, so that their expulsive power might be strong enough to exclude any harmful symptoms. This is the most powerful method of all medicine, upon which the whole of **spagyric labor** <term>the alchemical process of "separating and recombining" substances to create potent medicines</term> most especially exerts itself. For nature ought to be its own physician; man, to whom the name of physician is attributed, is only the minister of nature and the servant of the true physician. If he presumes anything beyond this, it should be known that he is a reckless persecutor of nature—indeed, a destroyer of men—and this truth ought not to be concealed.

**Congelatives** are medicines that stop all flows, being compressing and drying in nature.

**Congluten**, or the conglutinated, is that which is turned into a glutinous matter through putrefaction.

**Heart** <note>original: *Cor*</note> among minerals refers to gold. It also signifies fire, or the greatest heat.

<meta>This page continues the alphabetical dictionary entries from page 66, moving through the 'C' section. It transitions from general medical terms to specific chemical substances and philosophical concepts, heavily influenced by Paracelsian thought.</meta>

->### CHEMICAL DICTIONARY.<-
->67<-

**Corbatum** is copper.

***Cornu cervi*** <note>literally "Hartshorn"</note>, in chemistry, is the beak or spout of an alembic <note>the pointed tube of a distillation vessel through which vapors flow</note>. In medicinal contexts according to Paracelsus, it is a wound-healing herb, so named because its leaves naturally resemble such horns; it is "marked by art" <note>original: *ex arte signata*; a reference to the "Doctrine of Signatures," the belief that a plant's physical appearance reveals its healing properties</note>.

**Body** <note>original: *Corpus*</note> is the subject or substance in which the internal powers <note>virtues</note> of things lie hidden.

**Supercelestial bodies** are those things which are known only through the mind and imagination, and not through physical eyes. For alchemists <note>original: *Spagyrorum*</note>, these are the subjects of miraculous works.

**Confusion**, which is a "mingling" <note>original: *synchesis*</note>, is the actual mixing of liquids that are able to flow on their own. Thus, the consistency—both of the individual parts and the whole—is watery or fluid. For those things that are first melted and afterward mixed together, such as pitch, wax, or resin, etc., they are primarily the result of melting <note>original: *colliquationis*</note>, although "confusion" serves them incidentally, since they could have been joined even without such mixing.

**Chybur** is sulfur. **Chybur** is prepared in such a way that nothing is more effective for the lungs. Let *Chybur* be mixed with mineral calxes <note>metallic powders produced by heating</note>, and then be sublimated <note>purified by turning into vapor and back to solid</note> three times. Give this **Chybur** to the sick person, for it wards off all defects of the lungs and keeps them healthy and free from all diseases. It is a balm for the lungs and chest; no similar secret remedy <note>original: *arcanum*</note> among the powers of things should be sought. For there is no "external lung" more perfectly suited to the body, nor does a greater secret for the lung lie hidden anywhere, except in **Chybur** alone. *Paracelsus, On the Nature of Man, page 488.*

**Marriage** <note>original: *Conjugium*</note> or **Copulation**. <note>In alchemy, these terms refer to the chemical union of two substances, often described as a "chemical wedding"</note>

**Conglutination** is a composition made by means of a "glue," wherein the nature of the joined parts remains the same. It is a reduction, or a restoration to a whole state. The "glue" varies according to the nature of each substance; here, it refers to anything that joins one thing to another, or even binds them together, through a tenacious stickiness.

<vocab>Corbatum, Cornu cervi, Corpus, Corpora supercaelestia, Spagyrorum, Confusio, Chybur, Conjugium, Conglutination, Paracelsus</vocab>

<meta>This page continues the alphabetical "C" entries of the chemical dictionary, following the discussion of sulfur (Chybur) from page 67. It focuses on mineral and semi-precious substances, specifically Carnelian and Coral, blending natural history with medicinal applications.</meta>

->### CHEMICAL DICTIONARY.<-
->68<-

**Conterfehe**, the liquid residue that sweats out of the walls of a furnace <note>often referring to a zinc-like byproduct or furnace calamine</note>.

**Contus**, an *Agage* <note>a technical term for a long stirring rod</note> or long pole. *Iron pole, Digger's pole, Third pole.*

**Cornelius**, or **Corneolus** <note>Carnelian</note>, more properly a gemstone or stone. One variety is distinguished by its redness; another has the lighter color of flesh; another is marked with white flecks <note>original: *pinnulis*, literally "little wings"</note>; another with white lines; another is entirely dark in its redness; and another shines brightly, like minium <note>red lead or cinnabar</note>. --- *The power of the Carnelian is to bind blood, and to stop menstruation and hemorrhoids. It is said to soothe anger, and it cleanses the teeth when used in a dentifrice. --- Furthermore, seals for rings are frequently made from Carnelians.*

**Corecrum** is a ferment <note>a leavening agent or substance that initiates a chemical transformation</note>.

**Corallus**, **Corallium** <note>Coral</note>, also called *Bassad, Beseth, or Bassath*, is nothing other than a "sea brother" <note>a metaphorical alchemical name</note> which, when pulled from the deep, becomes hard; it emerges and immediately solidifies when exposed to the air. In the same way, the lynx-stone <note>original: *lapis lyncurinus*; a legendary gemstone believed to be formed from the solidified urine of a lynx</note> is frozen from lynx urine by the air, becoming a coral-like gem. According to Pliny <note>the Roman naturalist</note> and Solinus <note>the Roman geographer</note>, pearls <note>original: *uniones*</note> are soft while in the water, but once removed, they immediately harden in the air. Pontanus <note>Giovanni Pontano, a Renaissance scholar</note> says the opposite occurs with alders and ferns in his *Meteorics*, chapter 44, regarding bitter springs.

---

> --------- *You might see the alder turn to stone*
> *Beneath the blue spring of the Sarnus, and bundles of fern*
> *And stalks twisted with chaff and tough osier.*

For just as the coral plant becomes stone when frozen by the air, so too the alder and the fern herb are turned to stone in the water of the Sarnus spring; you may see more on this in Ruland <note>Martin Ruland the Elder, whose 1612 *Lexicon Alchemiae* is a primary source for these definitions</note> from pages 171 to 173. **Coral** of a black color is rare and held in great value; it is a most effective remedy against the disease of frenzy <note>original: *phrenesim*; a state of delirium or mental inflammation</note>. Without a doubt, it is formed from the viscosity of the earth, hardened by its own heat and water. Art, by imitating nature, also makes corals which very successfully mimic the natural ones. Consult certain chemists who know well how to compose them.

->### CHEMICAL DICTIONARY.<-
->69<-

**Bark of copper** <note>original: *Cortex æris*</note>, is *bracitum* <note>likely copper scale or dross</note>, or the "flower of copper" <note>the fine flakes or oxidation that form on copper during heating</note>.

**Small touchstone** <note>original: *Coticula*</note>, used for testing gold.

**A grate** <note>original: *Craticula*</note>, is an instrument made of square iron rods about a finger’s breadth in thickness, placed close to one another in a sharpened manner so that a half-finger’s space remains between them; it is constructed for building hearths and serves to support the coals.

**Black chalk** <note>original: *Creta nigra*</note>, is black sulfur.

1. **Silvery chalk** <note>original: *Creta argentaria*</note>, in which flakes of silver shine through.
2. **Rare soft** [chalk].
3. **Hard** [chalk].
4. **Soft yellow Waldenburg chalk**, which draws lines very easily.
5. **Tufa-like** [chalk] <note>a porous stone or earth</note>.
6. **Hard violet-grey** [chalk].
7. **Blue chalk**, which is dug in Thuringia <note>a region in central Germany</note> near the town of Mogella <note>likely Muhlberg or a similar locality</note>, which craftsmen use for painting walls.
8. **Hard green** [chalk].
9. **Greenish** [chalk].
10. **Soft black** [chalk].
11. **Soft black [chalk] shining with silvery spots**.
12. **Hard black** [chalk].
13. **White builder's chalk**, from which the Catalans at the Matrona <note>likely the Marne River in France</note> build walls and fortifications, and from which the port of Algiers was constructed.

**Crocus**, Brass <note>original: *Aurichalcum*</note>.

**Crocus**, a Flower <note>in alchemy, "crocus" often refers to a fine metallic powder, named for its resemblance to saffron</note>.

**A crucible** <note>original: *Crucibulum*</note>, is a melting vessel made of earth that is most stubborn against fire <note>refractory clay</note>, with a narrower base and expanding upward into a larger capacity in a triangular or round shape, formed for the melting and refining of minerals and metals. There is also a type of crucible commonly called a **testa** <note>literally "shell"; a cupel or roasting dish</note>, suited for melting metals.

<meta>This page continues the alphabetical "C" entries of the chemical dictionary from page 69. The previous page ended with an incomplete entry for Crocus; this page shifts to Crystal (Chrystallus), chemical freezing (Congelatio), and various laboratory vessels and Paracelsian terms.</meta>

->### CHEMICAL DICTIONARY.<-
->70<-

**Crystal stone** <note>original: *Chrystallus lapis*</note> is a substance hardened by a more vehement frost from snow and ice; it is found nowhere except where the greatest winter snows prevail. That it is a form of ice is certain, because it is unable to withstand heat. And just like **Amber** <note>original: *Electra*</note>, crystals are prepared and polished by art. Crystals are afflicted by many defects, such as roughness, rust, spotted clouds, hidden cavities <note>original: *vomica*</note>, a very hard and brittle center, salt-like inclusions, and hair-like cracks. Crystal has a medical use in burning bodies <note>original: *in cremandis corporibus*; referring to the use of crystal spheres as "burning glasses" to focus sunlight for cauterizing wounds</note>. Placed under the tongue, it is said to quench thirst; ground and mixed with honey, it increases milk in women. The ancient Astrologers dedicated crystal to the **Sun**. In chemical terms, crystal is a small stone created through congelation <note>the process of thickening or freezing</note>.

**Congelation** <note>original: *Congelatio*</note> of this kind occurs when the watery moisture departs and the "congealable juice" remains, setting into the form of a small stone; this can happen in a cellar without obvious heat. However, if part of the wateriness is scattered by evaporation <note>original: *divaporando*</note>, the work proceeds more quickly. Therefore, lye solutions of ice are usually boiled down to a soapy thickness, or the watery part that floats on top is poured off once a sediment has formed. The remainder congeals more easily.

**Crystal**, is a white stone, transparent, and very similar in color to ice: it consists of fire and coldness. **Paracelsus**, page 545, *Book of Vexations* <note>original: *lib. vexationum*</note>.

**Cucurbit** <note>original: *Curbita*</note>, is a vessel usually shaped like a top, swelling in its belly into the form of a gourd or a pear. One type of cucurbit is provided with a rounded bottom, another with a flat one <note>the cucurbit was the lower part of a still, used to hold the liquid being heated</note>.

**Blind cucurbit** <note>original: *Curbita cæca*</note>, is a vessel in which the dissolutions of salts and other things are performed <note>a "blind" vessel has no outlet at the top, used for digestion rather than distillation</note>.

**Cubil**, is red earth.

**Culatum**, means calcined <note>reduced to a powder by heat</note>.

**Inverted Crepinum** <note>original: *Crepinum introversum*</note>, is corrected **Tartar** <note>potassium bitartrate purified for medicinal use</note>. Paracelsus, in the *Great Surgery* <note>original: *Chyrur. magna*</note>, page 459, folio.

**Crepinus**, is the **French disease** <note>original: *morbus Gallicus*; an early name for Syphilis</note>. Paracelsus, in the *Great Surgery*, folio 463, page 2.

<meta>catchword: Copper (Cupri)</meta>

<meta>This page continues the "C" section of the chemical dictionary. The previous page (70) discussed crystals and the process of congelation (thickening or freezing). This page moves into entries for copper (Cuprum), various salts and alums, laboratory vessels, and the technical definition of "corrosion" as an alchemical process.</meta>

->### CHEMICAL DICTIONARY.<-
->71<-

<margin>Page 416.</margin>
When **vitriol of copper** <note>original: *Cupri vitriolum*; vitriol usually refers to sulfate salts</note> is made; see Book 5 of *On the Nature of Things*.
How **balsam of copper** <note>original: *Cupri balsamum*</note> is made; in the same place, page 418.
**Sublimation of copper** <note>the process of heating a substance until it turns to vapor and then solidifies again</note>; in the *Great Surgery*, page 62.

In **Coagulation**, many things are "fixed" <note>made stable and non-volatile</note> during the process of distillation, especially if the substances to be fixed contain water within themselves, such as vitriol. If this is fixed, it is called **Colcothar** <note>original: *Colchotar*; the brownish-red residue left after the distillation of sulfuric acid from iron or copper sulfates</note>. **Alum**, if it is fixed in its own water, is called **sugar-alum** <note>original: *alumen sacchari*</note>; this is also resolved into a liquor which, if allowed to putrefy for a month, produces a water with the sweetness of sugar. This water is of great power and is an outstanding **secret** <note>original: *arcanum*; a specialized medicinal remedy</note> in medicine for quenching the **microcosmic fire** <note>a Paracelsian term for internal inflammation or feverish heat within the human "microcosm"</note> in "metallic men" <note>people whose constitutions are dominated by certain mineral qualities or who work in metallurgy</note>.

**Cuporosum** is burnt copper <note>original: *aes ustum*</note>.

**Cuperum** is Copper <note>original: *Aes*</note>. Also known as **Venus** or **Cancer** <note>alchemical/astrological associations for the metal copper</note>.

**Curcuma** or *Cursuma* is the **lesser celandine** <note>original: *Chelidonia minor*</note>.

**Cynnia**, *Cymia*, or *Carora* is a vessel similar to a urinal.

**Cyraemia** or *Crocomma* is the dregs of oil, and of saffron <note>original: *Croco*</note>.

**Corrosion** is a form of **Calcination** <note>reducing a substance to powder by heat or chemicals</note> that reduces coagulated things into a "lime" <note>original: *calcem*; a fine powder</note> through the use of corrosive spirits. It is either vaporous or immersive <note>achieved through acidic vapors or by dipping the material into liquid</note>.

**Corrosion by stratification** occurs during calcination when the material to be corroded is spread layer by layer in a vessel along with corrosive powders. The material should be either hammered into thin plates and broken, or crushed into suitable small particles, such as filings, etc.

**Corrosion by paste** <note>original: *per pastam*</note> occurs when plates of metal or stone are coated with a colored paste and, as it were, plastered over. The coated plate is set aside somewhere until it is corroded. The paste must consist of "separating agents" such as salts, vitriol <note>original: *calchantho*</note>, or vinegar.

The **invisible body** is the soul; indeed, it holds the middle position between the visible body (for which reason it is called "body" in that respect) and the invisible (because it participates with the invisible spirit...

<meta>The catchword at the bottom of the page "ſibili" completes the word "invisibili" (invisible) on the following page.</meta>

<summary>This page defines various copper-based substances and chemical processes, specifically detailing the methods of alchemical corrosion (stratification and paste) and the medical uses of sugar-alum. It also touches on the philosophical definition of the "invisible body" or soul as a bridge between the physical and spiritual.</summary>

->### CHEMICAL DICTIONARY.<-
->72<-

...visible. It should be noted, meanwhile, that these definitions are physical and not theological, lest anyone find an occasion for slander.

**Physical body** <note>original: *Corpus physicum*</note> is the subject of nature.

**Consolidatives** <note>original: *Consolidativa*</note> are external surgical medications that fill wounds and ulcers with new flesh and cause a scar to form.

**Consolida**, which is elsewhere called the **Golden Sophia** <note>original: *Aurea Sophia*</note> by Paracelsus, is a certain herb with round flowers of a lemon-yellow color like a small flame; its leaves are not unlike hyssop. It is very common in Carinthia <note>a mountainous region in southern Austria where Paracelsus spent time</note>, but in other regions it is not so frequent or well-known.

**Constellation** is the name for the impression and operation of the powers and virtues of the celestial stars or bodies upon the things below, by which they come together into a certain natural harmony. Concerning this, there exists a physical art and science more excellent than all others, which is properly called the **Signated Physiognomy of Natural Things** <note>original: *Physionomia signata*; the study of the "signatures" or outward marks that reveal the inner nature and celestial sympathies of plants and minerals</note>, or the sympathy of higher things for the lower. This science is not currently available, but was suppressed along with most of the other more excellent writings of Paracelsus. If it were brought to light, then finally those envious persecutors of nature would recognize what kind of philosopher and physician the "German Apollo," Paracelsus, really was, and by how great a distance he left all the Greeks and infidel philosophers behind him.

**Constrictives** <note>original: *Constrictiva*</note> are styptic surgical medications that compress the edges <note>literally "lips"</note> of wounds by their own power. Concerning these, read above in the second treatise on "specifics."

**Contorsion** <note>original: *Contorsio*</note> is a torment of the intestines, otherwise known as the *Miserere mei* <note>literally "Have mercy on me," a traditional name for a severe intestinal obstruction or bowel blockage, named for the patient's cries for mercy</note>.

**Contraction** and **contracture** <note>original: *contractio* and *contractura*</note> of the limbs is a lack of power and a loss of their natural strength.

**Corrosives** are surgical medications that eat away superfluous and unhealthy flesh. These are also called **catheretics** <note>Greek-derived term for substances that wear down tissue</note>, **nomai** <note>original: *nemomena*; referring to substances that treat spreading sores</note>, or **septics**.

*   **Preservatives** <note>original: *Conservativa*</note>, especially those used in physics <note>here meaning natural philosophy and medicine</note>, are medications that preserve life and the body from decay. Such things are primarily to be sought from those substances which have received from nature a longer life and a more balanced temperament. Among plants, these include any that remain green in both winter and summer and endure for a long time. Among animals, they are those that live longer and more healthily. Among minerals, they are those that possess more "mummy essence" <note>original: *mumialis essentiæ*; a reference to "mumia," a vitalizing balsam or life-force believed to reside in certain substances</note> or liquid. Among metals, they are those less prone to rust <note>original: *ærugini*</note>. Common preserves of roses, violets, and the like are not such things, for they require preservation themselves and borrow it from sugar. It is a shame even to recount the inventions of the ancients.*

*   **Coostrum**, the middle part of the diaphragm.*
*   **Cotoronium**, is a liquid.*
*   **Coruscus**, is the herb mouse-ear <note>original: *auricula muris*; likely *Pilosella officinarum*, used in traditional medicine for respiratory issues</note>.*
*   **Bark of the sea** <note>original: *Cortex maris*</note>, is the **Vinegar of the Philosophers** <note>a specialized alchemical solvent, not common vinegar</note>.*
*   **Blood of salt** <note>original: *Cruor salis*</note>, is salt separated from the "first salt" by a defect in the second digestion <note>an alchemical term for the refinement process</note>.*
*   **Cycima**, is litharge <note>original: *lytargirium*; lead oxide produced during the separation of silver from lead</note>.*
*   **Cydar**, is Jupiter <note>the alchemical name for the metal **tin**</note>.*

*   **Diasatyrion** <note>original: *Diasatyrion*</note> is a confection that arouses lust <note>original: *venerem excitans*; Satyrion was a type of orchid traditionally used as an aphrodisiac</note>.

*   **Dach** is a type of stone.

*   **Daib, Deheb, Deab** is gold <note>derived from the Arabic *dhahab*</note>.

*   **Damsir, Densir** is sand.

*   **Dehene** is blood. Also called **Dihin**.
*   **Dehenes, Duenes** is ink <note>original: *atramentum*; could refer to black pigment or vitriol-based ink</note>.
*   **Dehenez, Duenec, Duhenec** is Roman vitriol <note>iron or copper sulfate</note>.

**Deliquium** <note>deliquescence</note> is a "cold descent." This occurs when coagulated bodies are exposed to the air of a cellar, a well, or another cold and damp place—placed upon a slab of marble, a glass plate, or in a "Hippocratic sleeve" <note>original: *manica Hippoc.*; a conical cloth filter bag used for straining</note>. With the aid of external moisture from the air joining itself to these bodies, they resolve into a liquid, which then flows down and drips into a vessel placed beneath. Mostly salts, calcined powders <note>powders reduced to ash by heat</note>, and other bodies of a salty nature resolve in the cold; however, everything that is resolved by cold is coagulated again by heat.

**Deliquium in the air** is the liquefaction of a solid <note>original: *concreti*</note> that occurs as external moisture works its way in, resolving the dryness so that it may flow. That which is resolved in this way is called a **liquor** and has a watery consistency. For although lime stones <note>original: *lapides calcarii*</note> may be broken down by atmospheric or watery moisture, this is neither a liquefaction, nor is the resulting substance called a "liquor." **Deliquium** is of two kinds: namely, **Vaporous** and **Embaptic** <note>from the Greek *embaptein*, meaning to dip or soak</note>.

**Vaporous deliquium** occurs when a substance [is reduced] into a fine powder...

-># CHEMICAL DICTIONARY. 75<-

...reduced to [powder], it is exposed to moist air and, by becoming damp, is dissolved into a liquid. Indeed, that moist air is sometimes warmer and sometimes colder, such as the air found in damp underground places. Unless the substance to be dissolved is naturally suited for this, it is prepared—brought primarily to the nature of a salt or something similar—which is achieved mostly by **calcination** <note>the process of heating a substance to high temperatures to remove volatile components or reduce it to a friable powder</note>, or by reducing it to such a state of dryness that it greedily drinks in external humidity and readily flows. For it is necessary that the tenacious structure of the substance be broken and a certain salt-like brittleness be induced.

**Embaptic Deliquescence** <note>original: *Deliquium Embapticum*; from the Greek *embaptein*, meaning "to dip or immerse"</note> occurs when a substance is submerged in a liquid and kept there until it has melted away. This happens in two ways: either the substance is contained in a small vessel and immersed, or the liquid substance itself does not touch it directly, but rather a very thin "sweat" permeates through—as when it is placed in a specific vessel or a bull’s bladder, and once it has dissolved, it is extracted. If the liquid used is hot (which is usually the case), this is a likeness of a bath <note>original: *simulacrum balnei*; a reference to a water bath or *balneum Mariae*</note>. Alternatively, the substance is allowed to spread into the liquid itself, either directly or with a linen cloth placed in between to simultaneously filter out impurities. In this case, the liquid may be cold—by which means gums and thickened juices, such as sugar, etc., can be dissolved—or it may require heating, as with **manna** <note>a sweet secretion from the flowering ash tree, used as a mild laxative and chemical base</note> and similar things that have a greasy or tenacious juice. That liquid which serves as the medium for the solution must be of such a nature that if we wish to set it aside, it can easily be separated again; nor should it introduce any foreign quality into the dissolved substance, unless we are intentionally seeking an alteration of that kind.

**Denequat** is Borax.
**Denodatio** <note>literally "unknotting"</note> is dissolution.
**Derquet** is varnish <note>original: *vernix*</note>.

**Distillation by inclination** is that which is performed with the vessel containing the material tilted to the side, with the beak pointing downwards. Because that vessel is usually a **retort** <note>a glass vessel with a long, downward-pointing neck used for distilling substances</note>, the process is also called **distillation by retort**. <meta>This page continues the definitions of chemical processes, moving from the conclusion of "deliquescence" (deliquium) on the previous page into the various methods of distillation, specifically focusing on distillation by descent.</meta>

-># CHEMICAL DICTIONARY. 76<-

What is called **distillation by descent** <note>original: *distillatio per descensum*</note> is so named because there is very little elevation of the vapors, and from there a significant downward reflection occurs; or it is because the spirits are pulled downward and descend, coagulating in a curved pipe without the use of an **alembic** <note>the cap or "head" of a still that redirects vapors downward</note>.

**Hot descent** (commonly called distillation by descent) is the distillation of liquid dissolved from bodies within an inverted vessel. Primarily, all types of wood, bone, and other materials whose liquid cannot rise due to its weight are dissolved by this method. It is performed in this way: I take an earthen **cucurbit** <note>a gourd-shaped chemical vessel used for boiling or distilling</note> and place fragments of the materials to be dissolved inside it. To prevent them from falling out when the cucurbit is turned upside down, I cover them with several larger fragments or sticks placed crosswise next to one another. Once this is done, I join another cucurbit—containing a little water—to this inverted one, so that the upper vessel aligns exactly with the opening of the lower one. However, it is necessary to place a strong iron plate between them, concave like a dish and perforated, to hold back the falling fragments so they do not slip into the vessel placed below. Finally, I dry the seals, which have been reinforced with the best **lute** <note>original: *luto*; a specialized clay or paste used to make apparatus airtight</note>, and place the vessel in its furnace, and the receiving vessel in sand or ashes, so that it might more easily receive the forced-down moisture while protected from the violence of the fire.

**Cold descent** occurs when the dissolved liquid descends in the cold. This is either **deliquescence** <note>original: *deliquium*; the process of a substance melting into a liquid by absorbing atmospheric moisture</note> or filtration.

**Distillation** <note>original: *distillatio*</note> is a drawing-forth by which the essence is extracted in the form of a liquid and, once condensed, is carried down drop by drop, transferred from the vessel containing the material into a receiver placed below. Whatever is to be distilled in this manner must be capable of being resolved into a moist consistency, or must be accompanied by moisture, whether that moisture is inherent to the substance or added from the outside. Such substances are primarily vaporous and possess oily spirits. Therefore, if the materials to be distilled are not of such a nature, they must be brought to that state by artifice. Distillation was invented principally for the sake of extracting the essence, although it also serves...

<meta>catchword: serves</meta>

-># CHEMICAL DICTIONARY. 77<-

...it may serve for abstractions, purifications, and the like.

*Distillation by ascent* <note>original: *Distillatio per ascensum*</note> is when, before the extract distills, it is raised up in an airy form. This process ought to be continuous from the start to the end, since if the work is interrupted, that which remains can barely be made to rise.

*Distillation by descent* <note>original: *Distillatio per descensum*</note> is when the moisture, drawn forth without being lifted upward, distills downward.

*Diathesis* is nature, disposition, or a state of being.

*Dihalegi, dicalegi, ditalem* is **tin** <note>original: *Stannum*</note>.

*Dionysius stone* <note>original: *Dionysius lapis*</note>, or gem, takes its name from Bacchus <note>Dionysus, the Greek god of wine and revelry</note>. It is said to resist drunkenness because it dissolves and expels the vapors from which intoxication arises. It is a black or dark brown gemstone, hard, mixed or sprinkled with reddish spots, droplets, or marks; when ground in water, it produces the taste and scent of wine.

*Diaphryges* <note>a parched or scorched mineral substance, usually a byproduct of copper smelting</note>; there are three kinds, as you may see in **Ruland** <note>Martin Ruland the Elder (1532–1602), author of a famous 1612 chemical dictionary</note>, pages 184 and 185. It is so named because it is burned, scorched, and dried by the sun and by twigs. The natural kind consists of a material similar to **vitriol** <note>original: *calcanthi*; specifically green or blue vitriol (copper/iron sulfates)</note>; for the liquid is like mud slipping through veins, belching forth from the earth itself. It is collected from twigs and scorched by the sun; hence it is called "twice-cooked." Some surgeons use this natural kind.

The first man-made species of *Diaphrygis* is the dregs of refined copper that settle, and it is formed along with the **flower of copper** <note>a fine powder or scale produced during the smelting of copper</note>; after the copper has been removed by the pouring on of cold water, it is found clinging to the bottom part of the furnaces, having the astringency and taste of copper. The other man-made kind is simple: it is when **pyrites** <note>sulfide minerals, likely copper pyrites here</note> are burned in a furnace like lime, until they become as red as **ruddle** <note>original: *rubrica*; a red earthy pigment or ochre</note>. This species is often adulterated with burnt ochre, but they can be distinguished by taste: the species of *Diaphrygis* burned from the pyrite itself or its material has an astringent taste of copper and **verdigris** <note>original: *æruginis*; the green pigment or "rust" that forms on copper</note> and dries out the tongue. Burnt ochre, however, has neither such a taste nor such power.

# CHEMICAL DICTIONARY. 76

...which is called distillation by descent <note>original: *distillatio per descensum*</note>, because there is little elevation, and from that point, the greatest downward reflection occurs; or because the spirits are drawn downward as much as possible and descend without an **alembic** <note>the "cap" or head of a still used to cool and direct vapors</note>, coagulating within the curvature.

*Hot descent* <note>original: *Descentio calida*</note>, (commonly known as distillation by descent) is the distillation of a liquid dissolved from bodies within an inverted vessel. Primarily dissolved by this method of descent are all kinds of woods, bones, and other materials whose liquid cannot ascend because of its weight. It is performed in this manner: I take an earthen **cucurbit** <note>a gourd-shaped chemical vessel used as a boiling flask</note> and place fragments of the materials to be dissolved into it. To prevent them from falling out when the cucurbit is turned upside down, I cover them with several larger fragments or small sticks placed crosswise next to one another. Once this is finished, I attach another cucurbit containing a little water to this inverted one, so that the upper opening fits exactly with the lower opening. However, it is proper that a sufficiently strong iron plate—concave like a basin and perforated—be placed between the two; this holds back the falling fragments so they do not slip down into the vessel placed below. Finally, I dry the seals, which have been strengthened with the best **lute** <note>original: *luto*; a paste or cement used to make chemical apparatus airtight</note>, and place the vessel in its **furnace** <note>original: *fornace*</note>. I then position the receiving vessel in sand or ashes so that, protected from the violence of the fire, it may more easily receive the moisture pressed down into it.

*Cold descent* <note>original: *Descensio frigida*</note> occurs when a dissolved liquid descends in the cold. This is either **deliquescence** <note>original: *deliquium*; the process where a substance absorbs moisture from the air until it dissolves into a liquid</note> or filtration.

*Distillation* <note>original: *Distillatio*</note> is an enticement or drawing forth by which the essence is extracted in the form of a liquid; once coagulated, it is carried down by dripping, transferred from the vessel containing the material into a **receiver** <note>original: *excipulum*</note> located below. That which is to be distilled in this way must necessarily be capable of being resolved into a moist consistency, or be present with moisture, whether that moisture is innate or added from the outside. Such substances are mostly vaporous and possess oily spirits. Therefore, if the things to be distilled are not naturally of such a kind, they must be brought to that state by art. Distillation was primarily invented for the sake of extracting the essence, although...

# CHEMICAL DICTIONARY. 78

It binds, purges, wipes clean, dries, consumes growths, and brings ulcers to scarring.

*Digestion* <note>original: *Digestio*</note> takes place in an *athanor* <term>athanor: a self-feeding furnace designed to maintain a steady, prolonged heat, often called a "palingenesis furnace" by alchemists</term> when, having filled the tower of the athanor with coals to the very top, we light a fire in the hearth. Using an iron spatula or a register <note>a sliding plate or damper used to control airflow</note>, we can widen or narrow the small door that transmits heat to the chambers according to our judgment. Some, seeking to emulate the operation of nature (as they say), contend that a continuous and identical heat is required from the beginning of the action to the end. Others divide the heat into degrees: for instance, the first degree is applied at the start until the "whiteness" <note>original: *albedinem*; a specific stage in the alchemical process</note> is reached; the second from there until the "yellowness" <note>original: *flavedinem*</note>; and for the final stage, which is the richest "redness" <note>original: *rubedinis*</note>, the third degree is used. *Ruland* <note>Martin Ruland the Elder, a famous physician and alchemist</note> supports this opinion.

*Separation* <note>original: *Discessus*</note> occurs when things that were deeply united are separated and depart, primarily through the power of a dissolving heat. This is accomplished by the "breathing out" <note>original: *exspiratione*</note> and the breaking down <note>original: *resolutione*</note> of the mixed components.

*Separation by expiration* <note>original: *Discessus per exspirationem*</note> occurs when a part thinned into a spirit departs. It is primarily called "expiration" when it is raised into the open air and released. However, when it is captured, it is called distillation or sublimation—operations which sometimes also serve the purpose of expiration. Moreover, expiration occurs in moist and vaporous substances.

*Distillation* <note>original: *Distillatio*</note>, or "upright moist elevation," is the process by which the finer moist parts are raised into the air and, clinging to the cold chambers of the *alembic* <term>alembic: the cooling cap or "head" of a still, used to condense vapors into liquid</term>, are condensed into liquid. They then drip through its channel, drop by drop, and flow down into a vessel placed for that purpose. It is done in this way: The material to be distilled is placed in a *cucurbit* <term>cucurbit: the gourd-shaped bottom vessel of a still</term>, which should not be too tall but sufficiently wide (for the height of the vessel hinders the craftsman, while width aids him). A large alembic is placed on top to more easily capture the rising spirits and resolve them into liquid. Afterward, depending on the nature of the substance to be raised, it is set at an appropriate heat, with a receiver attached...

# CHEMICAL DICTIONARY. 79

<note>The text begins mid-sentence, continuing from the previous page's description of a distillation setup.</note> ...with the receiver taking the dripping liquid, and all joints being properly sealed, a fire is lit and applied in degrees until all moisture has descended into the alembic <term>alembic: the cap or head of a distilling apparatus that cools and condenses vapors</term>. We shall provide an example from the vegetable kingdom: I fill a gourd-shaped flask <note>original: *cucurbitam*</note> to the third part with fresh roses, and I place it in a water bath <note>original: *balneo maris*; literally "bath of the sea," a vessel of hot water used to heat another container gently</note> while the water is still cold. Afterwards, I fill the alembic with the petals of red roses from which the yellow spikes <note>original: *aculei lutei*; likely referring to the stamens or the yellow base of the petals</note> have been plucked out, and I place it upon the gourd-shaped flask. After attaching the receiver <note>original: *receptaculo*</note> and sealing the joints with clay, I light the fire. Then the rising moisture occupies the alembic and extracts the tincture from the roses; once condensed into water, it drips into the receiver as a rosy liquid. It must be noted, however, that if the spikes are not plucked out, the water cannot extract a purely red tincture. I continue with the heat until no more water passes over and the roses in the alembic turn white. I have presented this for the sake of the technique of extracting the tincture from roses using their own water. When we draw something out through a copper vessel <note>original: *vesicam*; literally "bladder," but in alchemy, it refers to a specific type of boiling vessel</note>, it is when we elevate something through an alembic attached to a gourd-shaped flask.

*Distillation and moist elevation by an angle* <note>original: *obliqua*</note> occurs when the moisture is raised while the vessel is lying on its side. This is done either through the side or through a retort <term>retort: a glass vessel with a long, downward-pointing neck used for distilling substances by heating them and condensing the vapor</term>.

*Distraction* <note>original: *Distractio*</note> is that by which parts are pulled apart, each remaining in its own homogeneous state. Thus, it is a kind of tearing apart of different things that were previously joined, or of things once united, regarding the extraction of one thing existing or contained within another.

*Distraction* is of two kinds: separation or calcination <term>calcination: the process of heating a substance to high temperatures in air or oxygen to change its state, often to reduce it to a powder</term>.

*Divaporation* <note>original: *Divaporatio*</note> is the exhaling of dry-moist or breathable substances. For Chemists <note>original: *Chymici*</note> possess both a moist spirit and a dry one. It is performed in some vessel suitable to the condition of the matter—a pot, a pan, a gourd-shaped flask, etc.—and is done with various degrees of heat.

<meta>catchword: Dual,</meta>

<summary>This page describes practical alchemical techniques for distilling rose tincture and defines the terms "distraction" (the separation of components) and "divaporation" (the evaporation of spirits).</summary>

### CHEMICAL DICTIONARY. 80

<term>Doal</term>, or <term>Elemptis</term>, is <term>Gold</term> <note>original: *Sol*; alchemists used the names of planets to represent metals—the Sun for gold</note>.
<term>Dolet</term>, is red vitriol <note>iron or copper sulfates</note>, or red ink.
<term>Domicile</term> <note>original: *Domicilium*</note>, the vessel or place in which salt is cooked.
<term>Dracatium</term>, is <term>Lead</term> <note>original: *Saturnus*; Saturn was the planetary symbol for lead</note>.
<term>Dragon</term> <note>original: *Draco*</note>, is <term>Mercury</term> <note>also called quicksilver</note>.
<term>Dragon-stone</term> <note>original: *Draconites*</note>, or *drachates*, is a gem that is formed from the brain of dragons; but it never turns into a gemstone unless it is cut away while the creature is still alive, due to the envy of the animal when it feels itself dying. Therefore, the head is cut off while the dragons are sleeping, and thus the stone is removed. For the action of the vital spirit <note>original: *animae*</note> contributes to many things which the corruption of death alters in the bodily fluids <note>original: *humoribus*</note>. It is white in color. It drives away all poisons and cures venomous bites. Our own water snakes and marsh-vipers <note>original: *hydri & chelydri*</note> also sometimes carry gems in their heads, which you might call dragon-stones by similarity. The <term>slough</term> <note>original: *Syphar*; the skin which an adder casteth</note> of our marsh-vipers also has its own virtues in medicine; for its smoke wonderfully heals a prolapsed anus or womb.
The dung of a <term>Peacock</term> <note>original: *Pavonis*</note> is beneficial for those with the "comitial disease" <note>original: *comitialibus*; an old term for epilepsy</note>.
<term>Dragantum</term>, is vitriol.
<term>Daumir</term>, is a species of serpent that goes into the composition of theriac <note>a famous ancient medicinal "universal" antidote</note>.
<term>Darchem</term>, is select, pure Cinnamon.
<term>Diuretica</term>, medicines that provoke the flow of urine.
<term>Diaceltatesson of Paracelsus</term>, is the common quicksilver <note>mercury</note>, coagulated in <term>Alkahest</term> <note>the legendary universal solvent</note>, and tinted with the water of eggs; it becomes a coralline secret <note>original: *arcanum*</note> of the essence and condition of "goldness," because it is also "horizontal" <note>Van Helmont used this term to describe substances that act on the boundary between the material and the spiritual</note>. *Van Helmont*.
<term>Duumvirate</term> <note>original: *Duumviratus*</note>, makes a single marriage and one bed out of the stomach and the spleen. In this, he <note>Van Helmont</note> assigns to the spleen the role of the husband for the first movements, and to the stomach the role of the first sense; thus the stomach is the completion of the spleen, and the spleen the completion of the stomach. Under the single bed of both is the sovereignty of a single Duumvirate. *Van Helmont*.

# Two Brothers
<note>In alchemical symbolism, "Two Brothers" refers to pairs of related substances or metals</note>
| **Two Brothers** | *Saturn and Jupiter* <note>Lead and Tin</note> |
| :--- | :--- |
| | *Venus and Mars* <note>Copper and Iron</note> |
| | *Gold and Silver* |

*Two Brothers, <term>Edir</term> and <term>Michati</term>. Edir* is *iron or steel.*

*<term>Dyota</term>,* is a *circulatory vessel named for having two "ears" <note>handles</note>, or one arm attached to both sides. Its lower part is shaped like a gourd <note>original: cucurbitæ</note>, upon which is placed an alembic <note>a distilling cap</note>. At the very top, it has a channel used for pouring in liquid, and in the appropriate place, it is equipped with two curved beaks which carry the condensed moisture from the cap back down into the gourd.*

*<term>Dyoptra</term>,* is a *small perforated board, or a wooden tablet with a handle, split wide near the middle. By holding this in front of the face, we can observe the condition of the fire and the progress of the work without injury to the eyes.*

*<term>Digestion</term> <note>original: Digestio</note>,* is a *chemical operation, or a spagyric <note>alchemical</note> stage mimicking the digestion of the human stomach, in which and through which matter is cooked down to separate the pure from the impure. It is sometimes used to refer to the furnace or the glass vessel in which this mastery is fulfilled.*

*<term>Disk of the Sun</term> <note>original: Discus solis</note>,* is *quicksilver <note>mercury</note> extracted from gold.*

*<term>Divination</term> <note>original: Divinatio</note>,* is a *certain uncertain premonition drawn from thoughts out of one's own inner light, such as when it is said "my mind or heart foretold this to me," or "my spirit-angel indicated it," and similar common sayings.*

*<term>Divertaliū</term>, or <term>divertallum</term>,* is a *generation <note>a life form</note> that arises from the elements.*

*<term>Dubelec</term>,* is the *hollow space of abscesses in general, involving a rupture of continuous tissue.*

*<term>Dubelcolep</term>,* is a *composition made from coral and amber <note>original: carabe</note>.*

*<term>Duelech</term>,* is a *type of tartar <note>stony deposit</note> in the human body; it is a very dangerous, spongy stone.*

# E.

*<term>Edelphus</term>*, is the name given to anyone who predicts the future based on the nature of the elements.

*<term>Edir</term>*, is steel or iron.

*<term>Electrum</term>*, according to Theophrastus <note>Paracelsus</note>, is a mixture of a composite metal made from all the metals melted and fused together into a single metallic body according to observed planetary conjunctions. A small bell belonging to Virgil in the time of King Arthur was made of a similar substance; by its ringing sound alone, it cast those tainted by adultery—both men and women—off a bridge and into the water. This freed the King from the suspicion he had wrongly conceived regarding his wife. It is also said (and this is established by the experiments of several <term>spagyrists</term> <note>alchemists</note>) that a tasting cup fashioned from a similar, rightly fused composite metal will not admit any venom or poison. If such a thing were accidentally cast into it, whatever liquid is contained within would boil over with a loud, noticeable noise, just as if saltpeter were thrown onto burning coals.

Paracelsus tells another story in his treatise on the composition of such electrum: he saw a certain Spaniard who possessed a bell of this kind, weighing barely a pound and a half. By means of certain inscribed characters and words, he could summon, display, and drive away as many ghosts and portents as he wished, according to his will. He tried to persuade Paracelsus that the entire force and energy of the bell lay in the words

<meta>The text cuts off here with the catchword "and" (Latin: &), leading into the next page where the Spaniard's explanation continues.</meta>

--- Page 123 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on "Electrum" from page 82, specifically focusing on Paracelsus's discovery that the power of this magical alloy resides in the metal itself rather than just inscribed symbols. It then moves into definitions for the letter "E," covering the terms "Essila" and "Element."</meta>

->### CHEMICAL DICTIONARY. 83<-

...and that it consisted more in characters <note>inscribed symbols</note> than in the sound or the metal. But Paracelsus discovered that the matter was not as [others] asserted; although various wonders might be produced through variations in the writing, he found the greatest power to be situated in the metal itself. This is mentioned only so that one may know what **Electrum** signifies to Paracelsus: it is not always the amber <note>original: *carabem*</note> that attracts straw, nor is it Cyprian copper tinted with calamine stone, known by the another name as latten <note>original: *laton*; a brass-like alloy</note>.

***Essila*** is a tincture produced by the heat of the sun, appearing either on the face or on any other part of the skin of the body <note>likely referring to freckles or sun-darkened skin treated as an alchemical signature</note>.

***Element***, according to Paracelsus, is the corruptible and transitory essence of the world and of all things that undergo change, distress, and necessity until the day of the Final Judgment and the destruction of this lower world. It is also the essence of the spirits dwelling and hiding within the elements, and their astral governing principle <note>original: *astrum*</note>.

***Elements*** are the matrices <note>wombs or formative environments</note> of things, in and from which they are generated. These are subordinate to Paracelsus’s [primary] Elements, as each of the latter contains those four and is more general. One must not overlook here what is most worthy of note in Physics, and has been less observed until now: the simpler something is in hidden <note>original: *occulta*</note> nature, the more general it is found to be by <term>spagyrists</term> <note>alchemists who separate and recombine substances</note> and adept philosophers. Conversely, the more specialized a thing is, the more composite and material it is—to use the language of the art. For example, the more the "species" is removed from the essence of individuals, the closer their matter approaches the simple elements; likewise, their form approaches the simplest, yet most general, universal influence <note>original: *influens universale*</note>. This is not the case in manifest <note>visible or physical</note> nature, where the most specialized parts—even the inferior and more degenerate ones—exist as they recede further from their whole. This occurs because the principles of natural things contain all possibilities within themselves. Therefore, the spagyrist’s view on the difference between hidden and manifest nature, and vice versa, is most true.

<summary>This page defines alchemical terms, notably distinguishing Paracelsus’s magical metal alloy "Electrum" from amber and describing "Elements" as both spiritual matrices and corruptible worldly essences. It highlights a core alchemical theory: that in the hidden world, simplicity leads to universal power, while in the visible world, complexity leads to material decay.</summary>

->### CHEMICAL DICTIONARY. 84###<-

Paracelsus says that we have seen rings of **Electrum** <note>a magical alloy of seven metals</note> commonly worn on the fingers; whoever wore them would never be seized by spasms or by toothache <note>original: *odentalgia*</note>, nor would they fall into **Epilepsy**. Indeed, as soon as such a ring is applied to the finger they call the "ring finger" <note>the fourth finger</note> during a most violent fit, the seizure is suddenly driven away and vanishes as if wiped from the fingers, and the sick person immediately rises from their fall. We have also observed and experienced that if one of these rings is worn on the ring finger and some hidden disease is about to break out, you will see that ring sweat continually, and you will see it almost change color and alter before your eyes through a remarkable **sympathy** <note>the alchemical belief that objects are naturally linked by hidden resonances</note>. Furthermore, it is worth noting that our Electrum is a most excellent amulet against hostile spirits and harmful Genies <note>entities or spirits believed to influence human fate</note>. For there is a certain celestial operation within Electrum, and the influence of all the Planets acts within it, along with a certain harmony of all things superior and inferior. But the Electrum that is thickened by nature in metallic veins from gold and silver, as *Mathasius* <note>likely Johannes Mathesius, a 16th-century mineralogist</note> wrote, does not possess such numerous virtues as that which is fashioned by the industry of Artists from the seven metals in the fire, as *Geber, Raymond Lull, Theophrastus Paracelsus*, and other masters of this art testify. The ancients held this kind of Metal in such great value that they forged various household items for themselves from it, and they preserved vessels made of **Electrum** among their rarer furnishings, even above those of gold or Emerald.

**Illness**, is twofold: Future and Accidental.

Regarding **future** diseases, these include all fevers except the daily fever <note>original: *quotidianam*</note>.

Regarding **accidental** [diseases], these are species of **dropsy** <note>original: *hydropisis*; swelling caused by fluid</note>, **jaundice** <note>original: *icteritia*</note>, and **colic**, as in *Paracelsus on Degrees.*

Take Copper and Steel, **five parts of each**.

We call it "refining" when, after removing the more ignoble parts of the matter—which either cling to it incidentally or provide a support for the essence in some way—we draw out the most excellent part. Thus, the segregation of the impure from the pure most strongly prevails here; it is also a Solution or a Union.
