--- Page 154 ---
<meta>This page (110) precedes the page provided in the continuity notes (111). It concludes a definition of a "philosophical operation" (likely circulation or distillation) before providing a sequence of alphabetical definitions from "Immersiva corrosio" to "Indicum spuma."</meta>

-># CHEMICAL DICTIONARY #<-

### 110 ###

...joined, it is raised, and finding no exit, it falls back into the body, washing it through frequent moistenings until, purely coagulated with the body, it can no longer ascend but remains entirely fixed. This is clearly a philosophical operation <note>original: "philosophica est operatio"; in this context, "philosophical" refers to the higher art of alchemy as practiced by the "philosophers" or adepts, rather than common chemistry</note>, and it does not lower itself to common methods.

**Immersive corrosion** is when substances, when dipped into other materials, are reduced to a powder <term>calx: the crumbly, oxidized residue left after a metal has been heated or corroded</term>. It can be either a wet or a dry process.

**Inceration** is the mixing of a liquid with a dry substance through a slow "drinking in" until it reaches the consistency of softened wax. For this reason, it is called **enkerosis** <note>original Greek: ἐγκήρωσις, from *keros*, meaning wax</note>. In other contexts, it is also known as **imbibition**, which occurs through irrigation when the dry substance absorbs the moisture. Physicians use a similar method of "nourishing" materials, by which **sarcocolla** <term>a tree resin traditionally used to "glue" or heal wounds</term>, **litharge** <term>lead monoxide, often used as a pigment or in medicine</term>, and other substances are gradually soaked in a specific juice until they become suitable for use.

**Incineration** is the process of ignition that converts substances into ashes using a more powerful fire.

Note that plants and animal parts are "incinerated," but minerals (speaking in the chemical manner) are not properly said to be turned to ash, but rather are "reduced to lime" <note>original: "calcem redigi"</note>.

**Incorporation** is a blending by which moist and dry things are immediately combined into a single body in the form of a mass. In this case, there is no slow "nourishing"; instead, as much liquid is added at once as is required for the consistency of the mixed body, resulting in something like a dough. Because of this, it can also be called **impastation**, or in some cases, **subaction** <note>the process of kneading or working a substance into a uniform state</note>. Incorporated materials should be left in a heat suitable for digestion so that, through mutual action and reaction, they may attain a shared temperament <note>original: "crasis," a Greek term referring to the balanced mixture of the four qualities—hot, cold, wet, and dry</note>.

**Incuba** is the bride of the Sun <note>In alchemical allegory, this usually refers to the "Lunar" principle or Silver, which acts as the female partner to the "Solar" Gold in the chemical wedding</note>.

**Indian Salt** is Rock Salt <note>original: "Sal gemmae," literally "gem salt," referring to pure, transparent crystalline halite</note>.

**Indian Foam** is of two varieties. One is a native Indian foam that forms spontaneously on certain reeds in India. To painters, it is called **la-**

<meta>catchword: pis (completing the word "lapis," or stone)</meta>

<summary>This page defines several key alchemical processes for altering the consistency and state of matter, such as inceration, incineration, and incorporation. It also introduces the "bride of the sun" and the origins of Indian salt and indigo foam.</summary>

<keywords>corrosion, inceration, incineration, incorporation, Incuba, Indian salt, Indigo, calx, sarcocolla, litharge</keywords>

--- Page 155 ---
<meta>This page continues the alphabetical entries of the "Chemical Dictionary," specifically concluding the entry for "Indicum" (Indigo) and moving through "Infundibulum" (Funnel-mold) to "Inhumatio" (Inhumation). It focuses on the Paracelsian theory of the Three Principles.</meta>

-># CHEMICAL DICTIONARY #<-

### 111 ###

...Indian Stone <note>original: "Lapis Indicus"; referring to Indigo</note> is improperly named, for it is not truly a stone. The substance **adarces** <note>original: "adarces"; a salty efflorescence or crust found on reeds in marshy areas</note> is almost similar to this, which also clings to reeds in the same way. But while **adarces** is formed by accumulation, Indian stone grows naturally on reeds; it is also partly manufactured in the workshops of dyers. It is a purple foam, born upon the dyeing vats. That which is manufactured is best when it presents the appearance of sky-blue, for it possesses a blue color within the black.

It is lightly astringent, purging, and suppresses ulcers. Others say it is simply the juice of a herb with which cloths are now dyed, which they call **Indicum** [Indigo], that is, **glastum** <note>original: "glastum"; the Latin term for woad (*Isatis tinctoria*), which produces a blue dye similar to indigo</note>. One variety is domestic and known to the Thuringians, which dyers use most frequently. From this, the manufactured Indigo is properly made; the other is the wild woad. Concerning these, see *Dioscorides, book 2 on woad*.

*Infundibulum* [Funnel-mold] is an elongated metallic vessel with a handle, hollowed out on the inside, which receives molten metals and shapes them into rods or bars.

*Inhumation, and To Inhume.* <note>In alchemy, "inhumation" or burial refers to the process of placing a sealed vessel in a "grave" of warm earth or horse manure to provide a steady, gentle heat for digestion or fermentation.</note>
In fire, the seven Planets.
In water, the seven metals.
In earth, the seven herbs.
In air, the seven **tereniabin** <term>tereniabin: a term derived from Arabic for "manna," specifically a sweet, dew-like substance gathered from plants</term>.
In animals, there are seven principal members.

<margin>Paracelsus, volume 1, page 872.</margin>
For there is nothing in the whole nature of things that does not consist of these three: Mercury, Salt, and Sulphur; and these are the three principles of **Theophrastus** <note>Theophrastus Bombastus von Hohenheim, better known as Paracelsus (1493–1541)</note>, which are uncovered through spagyric analysis <term>spagyric: the alchemical art of separating a substance into its core components and then recombining them in a purified state</term>. We shall find nothing besides these three, which exist individually in each of the elements. **Jean Fernel** <note>Joannes Fernelius (1497–1558), a famous French physician who sought to reconcile traditional medicine with new observations</note> also observed these three in his medical teachings regarding "innate moisture." The ancients even handed these down in the past, such that **Theophrastus** was not the first to discover them, but rather he called them back into the light after they had been buried in darkness.

*A metallic instrument indicating the parts of the world.*
->The Compass.<-

<summary>This page defines Indigo and its relation to woad, describes a metal ingot mold (infundibulum), and outlines the Paracelsian theory that all nature is composed of three primary principles: Mercury, Salt, and Sulphur.</summary>
<keywords>Indigo, woad, adarces, ingot mold, inhumation, Paracelsus, Three Principles, Mercury, Salt, Sulphur, Jean Fernel, compass</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the "I" entries of the Chemical Dictionary, transitioning from the previous page's discussion on "Inhumation" to mineralogical descriptions of the Iris stone and moving into the complex Paracelsian terminology of "Iliaster."</meta>

-># CHEMICAL DICTIONARY #<-

### 112 ###

*Iris is a gemstone similar to crystal; according to some, it is the "root" of crystal. It is frequently found in a hexagonal shape in Arabia and on a certain island in the Red Sea. It is an extremely dry stone, a quality indicated by its great refinement. It is thought to be born from a watery juice that flows from the matter of stone which is generated in red mud. It is called "Iris" because, in its center, it represents the rainbow <note>original: "Irim meteoron"; the meteorological phenomenon of the rainbow</note> through distinct small veins; or because, when the stone is struck by the sun's light under a roof, it casts the appearances and colors of the heavenly bow onto the nearby walls, as witnessed by* Pliny. *He adds that some have rough sides and unequal angles, which, when cast into the open sun, cause shimmering rays to flicker within themselves, and some even illuminate their surroundings with a brightness cast out before them. He adds another Iris similar to wax, which is very hard and is born in Persia, and a gemstone similar to this in appearance, but not of the same effect.*

*Ios is poison <note>original: "Ios"; from the Greek word for poison or the rust/patina of metals</note>.*
*Iota or Iora is green copper-rust <note>original: "viride rama"; likely a corruption of "viride aeris" or verdigris, the green pigment formed on copper</note>.*
*Ipacedes is goat's beard <note>original: "barba hircina"; likely referring to the plant *Tragopogon*, used for its medicinal roots</note>.*
*Arthritic Iva, or Musk-scented Iva <note>original: "Iva artetica, vel Muscata"; likely Ground Pine (*Ajuga chamaepitys*), used for joint pain</note>, is a medicine for contracted limbs.*
*Drinkable Iva.*
*The Metallic Judge <note>original: "Iudea metallicus"; translated from the German "Berg Richter". This was an official who managed mining laws and disputes in mining districts</note>.*
*Iumnisum or Iumnizum is Ferment.*
*Jupiter is Tin <note>In alchemical symbolism, the planet Jupiter represents the metal tin</note>.*
*Iussa is a gypsum stone.*
*The first or implanted Iliaster <note>The "Iliaster" is a term coined by Paracelsus to describe the principle of life or the "star" within a substance. It refers to the vital force that governs growth and duration</note> is the limit of life, or rather life itself, or the balsam of life in man.*
*The second or prepared Iliaster is the limit of the balsam, and the life which we have from the elements and from things composed of the elements.*
*The third Iliaster is the prepared limit of the balsam, or the life which we possess from the quintessence <note>The "fifth essence," the purest part of a substance</note>.*
*The fourth Great Iliaster is the rapture of the mind or soul.*

<summary>This page contains a detailed mineralogical entry for the "Iris" stone (likely quartz or iridescent crystal) and a series of alchemical definitions, concluding with Paracelsus's metaphysical hierarchy of the "Iliaster."</summary>
<keywords>Iris stone, Pliny, Ios, verdigris, Iva artetica, Jupiter, Tin, Iliaster, Paracelsus, balsam of life, alchemy dictionary</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the alphabetical entries for "I," specifically concluding a definition related to spiritual or alchemical "rapture" and moving through definitions of Iliaster, imagination, and celestial influences.</meta>

-># CHEMICAL DICTIONARY #<-

### 113 ###

...taken into another world, such as the rapture of Enoch and Elijah <note>original: "raptus Enoch & Eliæ"; referring to the biblical figures taken directly to heaven without dying</note>.

*Iliaster* <note>original: "Iliafter"; a Paracelsian term derived from "Hyle" (matter) and "Aster" (star)</note> however, in a general sense, is called the hidden power of nature from which all things have their growth, are nourished, multiply, and thrive. This can be read about more extensively in Paracelsus's book on meteorological generations.

*Images*, are metallic figures or effigies in which heavenly powers and virtues operate <note>This refers to the creation of talismans or "sigils" intended to capture planetary influences</note>.

*Imagination*, is the "star" within a human being, a heavenly and super-heavenly body <note>In alchemical philosophy, imagination was not just "make-believe" but a creative, formative force that connected the human mind to the cosmos</note>.

*Impressions*, are the invisible fruits of the stars within things of the lower world.

*Impure alkali*, is the foam or scum which is removed during the purification process after extraction. *Impure tartar*, refers to the tartar of the alkali itself.

*The Inanimate*, are the pygmies <note>In Paracelsian cosmology, "pygmies" were earth elementals (Gnomes) who were considered "inanimate" because they were thought to lack an eternal soul like humans</note>.

*Inclination*, is a power of nature manifested in a human being, showing the direction toward which their life is most prone, as demonstrated by their actions and works.

*Incarnatives*, are the medicines used by surgeons to cause skin to grow over wounds or to close the flesh.

*Inclusives*, see above, regarding what "pertaining to the sword" <note>original: "gladialis"; likely referring to the "weapon salve" or *unguentum armarium*, a medicine applied to the weapon rather than the wound</note> may be.

*Incubus*, is a nocturnal spirit that mocks and deceives women in their sleep, as if they were performing a sexual act with it.

*Influence* is defined as the attraction of the virtues and nature of the stars and higher planets into our minds through mental imaginations. Once these are swallowed and received, we retain them so they remain with us. This influence is twofold: one is derived to us through a created medium, such as the influence of heaven through the firmament; the other is given without any medium by the "Giver of Lights" <note>original: "datore luminum"; a reference to God, from the Epistle of James 1:17</note>. This latter type is properly called "supernatural," whereas the former is natural, or artificial and moral.

*Natural Influence*, however, is that which the higher stars of the firmament pour in, according to a natural decree into...

<meta>catchword: these (hæc)</meta>

<summary>This page defines key Paracelsian concepts including Iliaster (the vital force), the creative power of the Imagination, and the various ways celestial "influences" affect the human mind and the physical world.</summary>
<keywords>Iliaster, Paracelsus, Imagination, Talismans, Alchemy, Pygmies, Elemental Spirits, Medical Surgery, Celestial Influence, Incubus</keywords>

<meta>This page continues from the previous page's discussion on "Inclinations," concluding the explanation of how celestial forces influence both sentient beings and inanimate matter, before moving into definitions for the letter K.</meta>

-># CHEMICAL DICTIONARY #<-

### 114 ###

...these lower things, through which they govern and rule by means of inclinations in humans, beasts, and others, and through powers and efficacies in inanimate things. From this, it is to be concluded that "inclination" is a certain animalistic force and a magnetic attraction, which we can easily oppose and resist through moral influence (provided we are supported by divine inspiration; otherwise, it is extremely difficult).

*Unnatural* or *supernatural bodies* are those which are conceived by the mind alone. This is why someone might occasionally say, "I seem to see a man before my eyes," even though that person is more than two hundred miles away, or perhaps passed away twenty years ago.

---

## K.

*Kachimia* <note>original: "Kachimia, vel kakimia"</note> is the name given to the immature ore of a metal, or a semi-perfect metal not yet finished by nature. It still hides in its "first being" <note>original: "primo suo ente"; a Paracelsian term for the purest, initial state of a substance before it takes final form</note>, like an infant in its mother's womb. Thirty species of such *Kachimias* have been identified so far, but listing them here would be tedious and of little use.

*Karlina* is wild dill <note>original: "anethum silvestre"</note>.

*Kibrith* is sulfur.

*Elevated Kymit* <note>original: "Kymit elevatum"</note> is white cinnabar that has been sublimated <note>Sublimation is the alchemical process of heating a substance into vapor so it purifies as it resets into solid form</note>.

*Kist* (or *Cist*) is a measure-and-a-half, or a weight of four pounds of any liquid.

*Kobolt* <note>original: "Kobolt, vel cobaltum, sive cobletum"</note> is a metallic material darker than lead and iron. It lacks a metallic shine, though it can be melted and hammered. It is not "fixed" <note>In alchemy, a "fixed" substance is stable and does not evaporate or transform when exposed to high heat</note>, but instead carries off better metals with it through its vapors. It is also called the "Cadmia stone" <note>original: "Lapis Cadmiæ"; a historical term for ores used in brass-making or associated with cobalt and zinc</note>.

*Kal* is "Salt of the stream" <note>original: "Sal de torrente"</note>.

<meta>catchword: Kaldi</meta>

**Kali** is potash <note>original: "cinis clavellatus"; literally "wedged ashes," referring to the potassium carbonate obtained from wood ashes</note>.
**Kalnos** is smoke.
**Kamar**, or **Camar**, is Silver.
**Kamir** is Leaven <note>original: "fermentum"; in alchemy, the substance added to a base metal to initiate its transformation into gold</note>.
**Kanfor** is Tin. **Kazdir** is also Tin.
**Kaprili** and **Kibrith** are Sulfur.
**Kar** is a gemstone shining like fire <note>likely referring to a carbuncle or ruby</note>.
**Kalam** is iron.
**Katimia**, **Kadmia**, or Calamine stone; **Tutia** <note>Tutty or zinc oxide, often found as a crust in smelting furnaces</note>.

**Kaysir** is sea-foam, specifically pumice.

**Kyram** is snow.
**Kiva** is Opoponax <note>a scented vegetable resin used in perfumery and medicine</note>.

# L.

**Labor of Wisdom** <note>original: "Labor Sophiæ"</note> is called Paradise, or another World.
***Virgin’s milk*** <note>original: "lac virginis"</note> is Mercurial water. Also, ***Virgin’s milk*** is the Mercury of the Philosophers, the juice of the Sun-plant and Moon-plant <note>original: "succus Solariæ & Lunariæ"</note>.
***Milk of poppy*** is Opium.
***Lacerta*** <note>literally "lizard"</note> is a cavity of the flesh and body; also, flesh enclosed in muscles without bones.
***Red lizard*** <note>original: "lacerta rubra"</note> is Colcothar <note>a red oxide of iron remaining after the distillation of sulfuric acid</note> or Vitriol.

# 116 #

**Levigation**, or **Alcoholization** <note>original: "Alcolismus"</note>, is a form of Calcination that brings a substance into a fine powder <note>original: "Alcool"; in early chemistry, this referred to any finely divided powder, not just liquid spirits</note> by grinding. In this context, it also refers to the reduction of certain substances by bonding together <note>original: "glutinationem"</note>, and others by melting, or by explosive purification <note>original: "fulminationem"</note>.

**Lacune** is Sealed Earth <note>original: "Terra Sigillata"; a famous medicinal clay stamped with an official seal to guarantee its origin and quality</note>.

**Lamac** is gum Arabic.

**Lamina** is Orpiment <note>original: "Auripigmentum"; a deep yellow arsenic sulfide mineral used as a pigment and in alchemy</note>.

**Lans** is dead silver <note>likely referring to silver that has lost its "life" or reactivity through chemical treatment</note>. **Lanx** is the bitter almond.

**Laos** is Tin.

**Stone in Chemistry** <note>original: "Lapis in Chymia"</note> refers to any "fixed" substance—that is, a substance that does not evaporate or fly away when exposed to fire.

**Adiz Stone** <note>original: "Lapis Adiz"</note> is Sal Ammoniac <note>ammonium chloride</note>.

**Stone not a Stone** <note>original: "Lapis non Lapis"</note> is a Stone by virtue of its power and effectiveness, but not a stone in its physical substance. Avicenna and Kodar <note>likely referencing early Arabic alchemical authorities</note> assert that the "stone not a stone" is the Elixir <note>the legendary substance capable of transmuting metals or prolonging life</note>.

**Animal stone** <note>original: "Lapis animalis"</note> is human blood; likewise, it refers to the tortoise <note>original: "curenta"</note> that carries a shield on its back.

**Mortification of stones** is their Calcination <note>reducing them to a powdery oxide through heating</note>.

**Arabian Stone** <note>original: "Lapis Arabicus"</note> is similar to sturdy ivory; it is used in tooth-powders and for drying out hemorrhoids. **Arabian Stone** is nothing other than *lapis specularis* <note>mirror stone</note>, or Moon-stone, or Mary’s glass <note>original: "glacies Mariae"; a transparent variety of gypsum known today as selenite</note>, from which windows were prepared in olden times—using smoothed wood in place of lead frames—and from which the best gypsum is made. **Rulandus** <note>Martin Ruland the Elder, a famous alchemical lexicographer</note> lists five species on pages 274 and 275. However, mirror stone does not possess the "oiliness" <note>original: "pinguedinem"</note> of orpiment; indeed, there is no similarity between orpiment and mirror stone except for their flaky layers. Mirror stone is a remarkable stone and a moisture, a sort of congealed earth, which does not dissolve; and because it is the moisture of the earth frozen like a crystal and hardened into a stone, they call it Mary’s glass. Some say that it is a celestial thing placed toward the brightness of the Moon, coagulated and constricted into the appearance of a stone, especially that which is

<meta>catchword: is</meta>

-># CHEMICAL DICTIONARY #<-

# 117 #

...is very white, and is called <term>Aproselinon</term> <note>original: "Aproselinon"; a Greek-derived name for moonstone or selenite, literally meaning "away from the moon"</note>. And although <term>Mirror Stone</term> <note>original: "Lapis specularis"; a transparent stone, often mica or selenite, used in antiquity for windows</note> has a kinship with gypsum, such that gypsum is its source, its use in medicine is nevertheless excellent. This is in contrast to gypsum, whose use is uncertain and poisonous, as it causes choking <note>original: "strangulat"</note>.

**Stone from the crab**. There are found within the crabs themselves—especially in the females when they have cast off and shed their outer shell <note>original: "syphar"; the molted exoskeleton</note>—white and round stones, commonly called <term>crabs' eyes</term> <note>original: "oculi cancrorum"; these are gastroliths, calcium deposits that crabs store to help harden their new shells</note>, because they have the appearance of an eye. But they are incorrectly called eyes, for they do not perform the function of eyes, nor do they protrude from the head, nor do they last the whole year. Furthermore, these stones are not an essence extracted from the whole shell of the creature, as can be discovered through many dissections of crabs. *Van Helmont, page 139.* <note>Jan Baptista van Helmont (1580–1644) was a pioneering chemist and physician who bridges the gap between alchemy and modern chemistry.</note>

Their power is to break up stones in the bladder, which is why they are collected. But nothing is more effective than these stones for "breaking the stone" in those who have suffered a heavy fall <note>original: "de alto deturbantur"; likely referring to internal bruising or "stones" formed by trauma</note>. *Van Helmont, page 139,* asserts that a very rare diuretic is gathered from the crab stone, which is also a healer of wounds and a banisher of fevers <note>original: "vulnerarium & febrifugum"</note>, provided it is dissolved into the form of its original "milk." And nothing is more suitable for the wounded or for mothers after childbirth than a remedy made from these small stones. The most famous of these is <term>Gascony Powder</term> <note>original: "Pulveris Gasconi"; a famous 17th-century medicinal compound used as a tonic, often containing pearls, coral, and crab stones</note>.

**Magnet Stone** <note>original: "Lapis Magnetis"; Lodestone</note>. *Ruland, pages 275 and 276,* <note>Martin Ruland the Elder was a famous physician and author of a major alchemical dictionary</note> lists 19 species of Magnet; it is thick and has the color of iron.

**Wheel-stones and Column-stones** <note>original: "Lapides, Trochitæ & Entrochi"</note>. *Ruland, page 277,* lists nine species. <note>These are fossilized segments of crinoids, ancient sea creatures, which look like small wheels or gears</note>.

**Colored stones**. He lists 8 species on page 277, but notes nothing of their medicinal virtues.

**Assian Stone** <note>original: "Lapis Asius"</note> is the best kind, according to *Dioscorides, Book 5, Chapter 88* <note>Dioscorides was a 1st-century Greek physician and the father of pharmacology</note>. It has the color of pumice and is light, spongy, and crumbly, having yellow-colored fissile veins running through to the bottom. It is called a stone only because of its color and substance; in terms of its hardness, it is not truly a stone, because it is light and spongy. It is called...

<meta>catchword: called</meta>

--- Page 162 ---
<meta>This page continues the alphabetical dictionary of chemical and alchemical terms, following the previous page's entry on minerals. It completes the description of the "Asian stone" and moves on to the Phrygian and Thracian stones.</meta>

-># CHEMICAL DICTIONARY #<-

# 118 #

...is called the <term>Asian Stone</term> <note>original: "Lapis Asius"</note>, because it originates on Mount <term>Assos</term> <note>an ancient Greek city in the Troad region of modern-day Turkey</note> in the Troad. Its power is astringent, and it has the property of moderately eroding and lightly corroding superfluous flesh. For this reason, it is called <term>Sarcophagus</term> <note>from the Greek "sarx" (flesh) and "phagein" (to eat); this is the origin of the word for stone coffins, as they were believed to consume the bodies within</note>. <term>Pliny</term> discusses this in Book 36, Chapter 17. He states that through a certain property, the Asian stone, which is salty to the taste, eases gout, and that in those very quarries all defects of the legs are healed, whereas in all other metal mines the legs are usually damaged. He affirms that the bodies of the deceased placed within it are consumed within 40 days, except for the teeth. They also have this stone in <term>Germany</term>, and on its surface. Around it and beneath it, a "flower" grows, a salty down with the appearance of nitre <note>potassium nitrate or similar efflorescent salts</note>, which is called the <term>flower of the Asian rock</term> <note>original: "flos Asiæ petræ"</note>. When applied to the tongue, it bites somewhat; when this "flower" is dried and washed, it has various uses in medicines. Some maintain that the flower of the Asian rock is born from sea-dew when rain falls upon it.

<term>Phrygian Stone</term> <note>original: "Lapis Phrygius"</note>: the best is pale and moderately heavy, not of a solid consistency but with white segments interspersed, much like <term>Calamine</term> <note>original: "Cadmia"; a zinc ore</note>. <term>Pliny</term> says that the Phrygian stone takes its name from the nation where it is found, and that it is a pumice-like clod, useful only for dyeing clothes.

<term>Thracian Stone</term> <note>original: "Lapis Thracius"</note>: this is born in a river of <term>Scythia</term> <note>an ancient region in Eurasia</note> called the Pontus. It has the power of <term>jet</term> <note>original: "gagatis"; a type of lignite coal used as a gemstone</note>; it is ignited by water and extinguished by oil, just as happens with <term>bitumen</term>.

This stone is nothing other than another black species of jet. It is formed from black <term>naphtha</term> <note>a flammable liquid hydrocarbon</note> or black liquid bitumen, just as yellow jet is formed from <term>petroleum</term>—that is, white liquid naphtha. When it drips or flows into the sea, it is hardened into stone by heat, both its own internal heat and that of the water. That the Thracian stone is made of bitumen is testified by its odor, its bituminous substance, and its black color, and the fact that it is a rough, crusty, and light stone, as described by <term>Dioscorides</term> <note>a famous Greek physician and botanist (c. 40–90 AD)</note>. If it is burned, it gives off a sulfurous odor, is ignited by water, and is extinguished by oil; in every respect, it corresponds to our black jet.

<summary>This page describes various types of "stones" (minerals) used in ancient and early modern medicine and chemistry, specifically focusing on the Asian, Phrygian, and Thracian stones. It details their geological origins, their physical properties like "eating flesh," and their chemical behaviors such as reacting to water and oil.</summary>

<keywords>Lapis Asius, Sarcophagus, Pliny the Elder, Lapis Phrygius, Lapis Thracius, Jet, Bitumen, Naphtha, Dioscorides, Mineralogy</keywords>

### 119 ###

I 2
<meta>catchword: ani-</meta>

*Bezoar Stone* <note>original: "Lapis Bezar"</note> signifies every medicine suited to counteracting a poison, and it is formed in this way: In the springtime, the **Stag** tracks down the dens of serpents and, with the breath of its nostrils, draws out those that resist; it eats them so that it may purge the weariness of the year. Soon after, it seeks out waters and rivers in which it plunges itself up to the head, staying there until it feels the power of the venom has been overcome. Meanwhile, through the force of the venom, it sheds tears from its eyes which coagulate into the **bezoar**. It is effective against all poisons, benefits those with heart ailments <note>original: "cardiacis"</note>, aids against defects of the mind <note>original: "defectus animę"</note>, and heals all venomous bites. The bone from the heart of the Stag <note>original: "os de corde Cervi"</note> possesses the same power, as hunters know by its shape—much like the crosses found in the head of the **pike** <note>original: "lupi aquatici," literally "water wolf," a common term for the predatory pike fish</note>, except that these are bones.

Bones from the heart of the Stag or the young hind protect the birth and are wonderfully beneficial to those who are pregnant. The stone found in the stomach and intestines of the Hind has the same power. Its marrow is useful for all inflammations, especially of the womb. Its dried, fibrous genitals, when taken in a drink, soothe the pain of colic. Shavings of its private parts incite lust; its blood stays the bowels and benefits those suffering from stones <note>kidney or bladder stones</note>. Its horn, if acquired during the **Feasts of Mary** <note>likely referring to the Assumption (August 15) or the Nativity of Mary (September 8), traditional times for gathering medicinal materials</note>—especially the tips of the branches—is wonderfully helpful for those with the "falling sickness" <term>epilepsy</term> <note>original: "comitialibus"</note> and works against swallowed poison. Nothing is more useful to Physicians than its lung.

The *Bezoar Stone* is also of two other types: **Oriental** and **Occidental**.

1. The **Oriental** is from an animal of **Persia** and **India**, which is part **Stag** and part **Goat**. In this animal, the stone is found (mostly in the forestomach, but also in the stomach and other internal cavities) in varying shapes—oval, round, easily split, and hollow inside (containing straw, hair, or something similar in the center). The surface is smooth and shining, and the substance is compacted of layers, sitting one upon the other like the layers of an onion.

The *Occidental Bezoar Stone* is a stone found in those same places—

<meta>This page continues the detailed entry for the Bezoar stone from page 119, concluding its medicinal applications and physical composition before moving into descriptions of other mineral and biological "stones" used in early modern pharmacology, such as the Quiris stone, the Perch stone, and the Jewish stone.</meta>

### 120 ###

...in living creatures, as mentioned previously, it is formed in layers <note>original: "tunicosim concretus," meaning formed in concentric layers like an onion</note> around a foreign object contained in a central cavity. The **Oriental** stones are for the most part larger, with a rough surface, and an ashen, white, black, or dark green color—the dark green being considered the best.

The **Oriental** stones are held to be superior: they are given in a dose of three to ten grains. The **Western** stones are given from six to twenty grains. They are useful in conditions of the heart, poisons, fevers, melancholy, worms, and epilepsy <term>Epilepsia: historically called the "falling sickness," often treated with cooling or absorbent minerals in this period</term>.

**Bezoar stones**, crab stones <note>gastroliths or "crab's eyes" found in the stomachs of crayfish</note>, and those from pearls are less hard and are not of a truly rocky nature. Instead, they are formed from a milky juice that is half-curdled and half-petrified. They possess a nature somewhere between cartilage and stone, much like the stony pits found in medlars, peaches, and similar fruits. They occupy a middle ground between wood and tuff <note>original: "lapidem tophicum," a porous, light rock often formed from calcium carbonate</note>. Although the solid matter of the powder from the **Bezoar stone**, crab stones, and so on is not digested by the human stomach, there is nonetheless a milky and mucilaginous juice within them of great power—though small in quantity—similar to that which can be extracted by boiling the filings of a stag’s horn. *Van Helmont, page 115.* <note>Johannes Baptista van Helmont (1580–1644) was a Brussels-born chemist and physician who famously bridged the gap between alchemy and modern science.</note>

*Quiris Stone* <note>original: "Lapis Quiris"</note>. In the nest of the **hoopoe** <note>original: "upupæ"</note> is found a stone named *Quiris*, which is said to be a betrayer of secrets and a provoker of various images in dreams.

*Famous Stone*, or precious stone, or even the salt of urine.

*Stone from the Perch*. The **Perch** <note>original: "Perca"</note> is a fish of firm flesh: there are two types, larger and smaller. In its head, two very white, bony gems are found, about the size of linseed, though proportional to the size of the fish. Nothing is more effective than this gem in breaking apart a stone in the bladder; for this reason, it is carefully and rightly collected by those who know its use. The head of a **gudgeon** <note>original: "funduli," identified in the text as a Gudgeon</note>, when gutted, is said to have the same power; it is said to resemble the adorned face of a maiden.

*Jewish Stone* <note>original: "Lapis Iudaicus"; these were actually fossilized spines of sea urchins, commonly used to treat kidney stones</note>. There are two kinds, the larger and the smaller.

The larger is similar to an acorn, marked with lines drawn as if by a lathe, just as it is excellently described by **Dioscorides** <note>Pedanius Dioscorides, a 1st-century Greek physician whose "De Materia Medica" was the primary pharmacological text for 1,500 years.</note>.

### 128 ###

It is also called **Thecolithos** <note>original: "Thecolithos," from the Greek for "stone-dissolver"</note>, because it is similar to the pit of an olive, and because it breaks and expels the stones <note>kidney or bladder stones</note> of those who lick it. The smaller variety is similar in shape to a cylinder and shines when broken. Similar to an olive pit and striated; these stones are said to be called "stony olives" in Palestine to this day. 2. Similar to an olive, but lacking the striations.

*Stone of Rebis* <note>original: "Lapis Rebis," referring to the alchemical 'Two-fold Matter'</note>, or *Stone of the Mountains*, is the Tortoise, or the Rebis.

*Stone of Lime* <note>original: "Lapis Calcis"</note> is the dross or the filings of copper.

*Ruland enumerates various kinds of stones on pages 284 to 292* (and also on page 301). These include those from aquatic environments; those embodied in wood; and those resembling animals, birds, bones, and shells.

*Martin Ruland treats the Philosophers' Stone from page 293 to page 300.*

*Later lictro* <note>A corrupt or specialized term</note> is quicksilver <note>mercury</note>.

*Laton* is brass <note>original: "aurichalcum," a golden-colored alloy of copper and zinc</note>.

*Lempinas* is sealed earth <term>Terra sigillata: a medicinal clay, often stamped with a seal to guarantee its origin and purity</term>. *Lempinas of Lime* is the filings of copper.

*Lempinas*, or *Lempina*, is orpiment <note>arsenic trisulfide, a yellow mineral</note>.

*The Green Lion* <note>original: "Leo viridis"</note> is, in the tradition of Hermes, the Mercury of Gold, or Duenech, or Antimony.

*The Green Lion* is, in the opinion of some, gold.

*The Green Lion* is vitriol <note>iron or copper sulfate</note>.

*The Red Lion* <note>original: "Leo Rubeus"</note> is the Blood of the Lion.

*The Foliated Yellow Lion* is orpiment.

*The Lion* is gold.

*Iron scales* <note>original: "Lepi ferri"</note> are the flakes of iron.

*Lepos Calcis* <note>likely "Scale of Lime"</note> is copper powder.

### 122 ###

*Leffas*, is the watery part of the earth which, by the power of seeds, is hardened <note>original: "induratur," matching the previous page's catchword</note> into wood, herbs, etc.; in this way, water is petrified by the power of a seed. *Van Helmont, page 45.*

*Leffas of Paracelsus*, is the sap of the earth, recently drawn into the root as if into the "kitchen" of the plant. *Van Helmont*, see more under the title *Leffas*.

*Leta*, is a glowing red heat.

*Leucasia*, is quicklime <note>original: "calx viva," unslaked lime</note>.

*Leux*, is an Almond.

*Libra*, is a hanging scale <note>a balance for weighing materials</note>.

*Wood turned into Stone* <note>original: "Ligna in Saxa"</note>, 1. Branches, leaves, bark, wood, coals, bones, and shells which have been incorporated into stones by a petrifying juice; [found] in a spring not far from *Frankfurt*, and also in *England*, near a town called *Knaresborough* <note>original: "Gnasborough"</note> in the province of *Yorkshire* <note>original: "Eboracensi"</note>. *Ruland* <note>Martin Ruland the Elder, a famous alchemist and physician</note> lists nine species of these on page 304.

*Limpid*, means pure and cleansed.

*Lily* <note>original: "Lillium"</note>, is *Mercury*, from which the flowers of the "Pelican" <note>an alchemical circulatory vessel</note> of the *Lily* are almost always produced in the tincture of the *Philosophers*: it is the quintessence of sulfur. Fixed sulfur.

*Liquation*, is when that which has grown together into one is dissolved by melting so that it can flow.

*Liquated* is that which has taken on a flowing consistency, resembling waves.

*Liquefaction*, is the dissolution of a mineral body by the force of a greater fire. It is of two kinds:

1. Simple *Liquefaction*, when a body melts for no other purpose than to be fused.

2. Probatory *Liquefaction* <note>Liquefaction for testing</note> is when a melted body is tested, and the imperfect part is separated from the perfect; this is done either through the *cupel* <note>original: "cineritium," a vessel made of bone ash used to refine precious metals</note> or through *antimony*.

*Liquation*, is fusion, or deliquescence <note>the process of a solid turning to liquid by absorbing moisture</note>.

*Lithanthrax*, is stone-coal. They are dug up near *Zwickau* <note>original: "Cygneam"</note>: it is a species of jet. It is nothing other than

*Stone* <note>original: "Lapis"</note>, is a term used for any "fixed" <note>resistant to fire</note> substance.

It is called the Elixir and the universal medicine.

*Demotine Fall* <note>original: "Lapsus demotinus"</note>, is a sudden seizure resulting in death, more dangerous than apoplexy.

*Laterium*, is lye <note>original: "lixivium"</note>, or "capitellum" <note>the strongest part of the lye</note>.

*Lato*, is copper dyed a golden color by calamine stone <note>zinc ore</note>, which is called "electrum" <note>historically an alloy of gold and silver</note> by ignorant physicians; it is composed of a single metal, whereas true electrum is made from several. Calamine is not a metal, but a mineral substance that has not attained perfection; it exists as a "bad-mineral" <note>original: "cacominerale"</note> body, destroying metal rather than [perfecting it...]

However, true **electrum** <note>a natural alloy of gold and silver</note> composed by nature alone is found made of gold and silver; it is of priceless value because of the admirable virtues within it.

*Laudina*, is **angelica** <note>the herb *Angelica archangelica*, highly valued in Renaissance medicine</note>.

*Laxa chimolea*, is a salt growing upon stones; see above regarding *anathrone* <note>soda or natron</note>, and below regarding *usnea lapidea* <note>stone-moss or lichen used in medicine</note>.

*Laxatives*, are medicines that purge both upward and downward <note>inducing both vomiting and bowel movements to clear the body's humors</note>.

*Lemures*, are spirits of the element of air, although the Pagans <note>original: "Ethnici"</note> interpreted them as shadows and ghosts of the dead.

*Litharge* <note>original: "Lithargyrium"</note> according to Paracelsus, is the **froth of Mercury**, but commonly it is called the froth of silver; however, it is rather the froth of lead while it is refining silver. A "golden" variety of the same kind is also held to exist; both are prepared by the same craft during the cleansing of the aforementioned metals.

*Lithargius* is a seeker of minerals, who likewise finds them. *Ruland* <note>Martin Ruland the Elder, a famous alchemical lexicographer</note> lists seven species of litharge on page 306.

*Limbus* <note>the "border" or reservoir of matter</note> according to Paracelsus, is the great and universal world, the seed and the primary matter of man. It is also heaven and earth, the upper and lower spheres with the four elements, and whatever is contained within them.

*Aquilegian liquor*, is **distilled wine** <note>likely a high-proof alcohol or brandy</note>.

*Essential liquor*, is that which is drawn from the internal organs and transmuted into flesh and blood.

*Liquor of Mercury*, is the **balsam of things**, in which lies and consists all the power of healing and the virtue of curing. This kind of mercury is most powerful in *tereniabin* <note>a type of edible manna found on plants</note> and in *nostoch* <note>a jelly-like algae believed by alchemists to have fallen from the stars</note>.

<meta>This page continues the "L" section of the chemical dictionary, following the entry for Litharge (the froth of silver or mercury). It continues the alphabetical sequence of medicinal liquids, botanical terms, and Paracelsian preparations.</meta>

->### 123 ###<-

*Liquor of mummy* <note>original: "Liquor mumiæ"</note>, is what human fat is called.

*Liquor*, when the name of a specific substance is added to it, refers to its oil; for example, the *liquor of turpentine* is its oil.

*Liquor of mummy from gum*, is the oil of gum.

*A liquid from a dissolved substance*, is that which is liquid by its very nature.

*Liquor of herbs*, is made from crushed herbs including their whole substance, sealed in glass and cooked in a water bath <note>original: "balneo maris"; a vessel of water used to heat another container slowly</note> for several days; the juice is afterwards strained through a cloth, and by the heat of ashes it acquires the thickness of honey.

*Liquor of salt*, is the balsam of nature, by which the body is sustained so that it does not rot <note>meaning it prevents decay or gangrene</note>.

*Locusts* <note>original: "Locustæ"</note>, are the tips of branches on trees that are still fresh and tender; Paracelsus writes that Saint John used these for food, and not those poisonous winged insects as theologians have interpreted until now. Certainly, it would seem strange if someone were so austere as to eat a living little animal with honey in the desert, where such a great abundance of trees would provide far healthier meals with wild honey.

*Lorindt*, is a commotion of waters, accompanied by a resonating sound; it is a sign of change, just as a comet is a sign of alteration in the heavens <note>original: "firmamento"</note>.

*Theophrastus’s Laudanum* <note>named for Theophrastus Paracelsus</note>, is neither labdanum <note>a resin from the rockrose plant</note>, nor is it opium. For its effects have shown it to be something far different; he did not kill men with this "greatest secret" <term>arcanum: a powerful, hidden remedy in alchemy</term>, but restored them to life as if from the dead. Our friend Oporinus <note>Johannes Oporinus, Paracelsus's secretary and later a famous printer</note> confessed to me that Paracelsus himself performed miracles with this medicine. He said he even administered it to many in *Theophrastus’s* name; some of these people were completely restored to life, while others who used it too late were at least restored to their senses for several days, especially during the plague. He also told me that he once took a "precipitate" <note>likely a chemical preparation of mercury</note> himself, and when his wife had provoked him to anger, which caused him to become overheated, he hurried to a spring...

--- Page 170 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of Paracelsus's "Laudanum" from page 125. The text opens mid-sentence, concluding a story about a man (likely Oporinus or an associate) who suffered a severe reaction after drinking cold water while treated with mercury.</meta>

-># CHEMICAL DICTIONARY #<-

->### 126 ###<-

...condition, and having drunk cold water quite freely, the poison of Mercury <note>original: "venenum Mercurii"; referring to the toxic effects of medicinal mercury common in that era</note> was so stirred up that his whole body swelled. The physicians, in the presence of his wife, gave him up for dead. When he heard this from those standing by—his sight having already failed—he soon placed a little bit of the laudanum, which he still had with him from what **Theophrastus** <note>Theophrastus Paracelsus, the famous Swiss-German physician and alchemist</note> had given him, into his mouth. From this, he fell into a most sweet sleep for half an hour or a little longer; when he woke, the swelling and the entire disease had vanished—which is the peculiar nature of this medicine—to the amazement of his wife and the others present.

I received these details from the mouth of **Oporinus** <note>Johannes Oporinus, Paracelsus’s secretary and later a famous printer</note> himself. **Andreas Jociscus** recited many things more freely in his oration; indeed, he said that Oporinus repented of the letter he wrote to **Dr. Weyer** <note>Johann Weyer, a physician known for his work on magic and medicine</note> concerning Theophrastus. He said at that same time, while walking with me in a meadow by the **Rhine**, that the letter had been begged out of him, and he would not have written it if he had known it would be published to the public in such a way. 

Although it is better that he wrote it, for there are more things in it that pertain to the praise of **Theophrastus** than to his blame; and the things he criticizes there are to be understood quite differently than how either Oporinus or others interpreted them. 

But **Paracelsus’s Laudanum** is a most highly praised medicine, which is made not from cheap things, nor poisonous things, nor opiate things <note>The author argues here that Paracelsus's original recipe did not contain opium, contrary to later versions of laudanum</note>, but from the most excellent of all things in the world that preserve life. It is effective in all diseases except for Leprosy <note>original: "Lepra"; in this period, "leprosy" often referred to any incurable or systemic skin decay</note>. It was so named by Theophrastus because it was "most worthy of all praise" <note>original: "omni laude dignissima"; a play on the word Laudanum and the Latin 'lauda' (praise)</note>. There are at least two things of which it consists, and greater things than these cannot be found in the world. I know that what some have published against this Laudanum is false and lying. 

There lives a reliable witness, a venerable old man named **Georgius Veterus**, a preacher at **Eberbach** in the domain of the Counts of **Erpach**. At that time, he lived with Theophrastus in **Basel**, when he cured a noble Canon and many others; and he administered the medicines in the name of Theophrastus.

<summary>This page defends the reputation of Paracelsus's "Laudanum," arguing it is a life-preserving medicine free of opium and mercury, and provides biographical anecdotes about Paracelsus's secretary Oporinus.</summary>

<page-num>127</page-num>

-># CHEMICAL DICTIONARY #<-

*Pure laudanum* <note>original: "Laudanum purum"; here referring to a specific Paracelsian remedy rather than simple opium tincture</note> is said to be that which is not prepared with other ingredients, but which is distilled into a liquid when dissolved: **Michael Toxites**, M.D., and **Johannes Fischart**, M.D., report these things concerning **Theophrastus** <note>Theophrastus Paracelsus</note>.

*Prepared laudanum*.

*Magra mud*, *Magra* is Carnelian <note>original: "Carneolus vel Carniolus"; a semi-precious reddish-orange gemstone</note>.

*Star-mud* <note>original: "Lutum stellæ"</note> is Gypsum.

*Leseolus disease* is jaundice <note>original: "icteritia"</note>. For *Leseolus* <note>a Paracelsian term for a specific salt-based remedy or essence</note> cures jaundice and no other disease: and this is proven by the fact that in *Leseolus* there is found the greatest transparency of salt, which tints and fixes more than melted topaz. Jaundice is also a salt, transparent in a yellow color, which ought to be driven out by its own likeness <note>A reference to the homeopathic principle of "like cures like," central to Paracelsian medicine</note>. **Theophrastus**, chapter 7, *On the Three Principles*.

*Lorcha* is said to be the entire sweetness of lizards, reaching to the very center of their substance.

*Ludus* is a bladder stone cut from the body, which is ultimately a remedy for the Stone <note>original: "Calculi"; the practice of using a processed gallstone or bladder stone to treat the same ailment</note>.

*Nitrous earthworms* <note>original: "Lumbrici nitri"</note> are worms found in dung or in the earth, so named for their slipperiness <note>original: "lubricitate"</note>.

*Compact Moon* <note>original: "Luna compacta"</note> is fixed Silver, or white Gold.

*Lustum* is the fat of milk, Cream, or the "flower of milk."

## M.

*Macha* is a small flying worm. *Machal* is something fixed.

*Metaphysical magic* is an art permitted to Christians; it is not, as many who are ignorant of the good arts imagine, poisonous or superstitious, but supernatural. Through it, any hidden secrets are revealed, just as manual work is required for mechanical industry.

*Magic in general*, which in Latin means "wisdom" <note>original: "sapientia"</note>, is of two kinds. One is natural and lawful, the mother of true medicine; it is the hidden wisdom of nature, lying deep and concealed within the center.

<meta>catchword: concealed</meta>

*Magisterium* <term>Mastery; a concentrated medicinal preparation</term> *for Paracelsus*, is a secret essence <term>arcanum</term> extracted from natural things without any elemental separation (by which other substances are usually prepared), but only by the addition of other things from which that which has been extracted is then separated. You will find more on this above in the second treatise, and likewise in the *Archidoxes* of **Paracelsus**.

*Magnetinus tartarus* is a stone in man [a bladder or kidney stone] that is very hard, like a magnet, but spongy in texture.
*Mandella* is the seed of black hellebore <note>original: "ellebori nigri"; a plant used in ancient medicine, often for madness or purging</note>.
*Mangoraria* is the art applied to weights, by which any heavy object is lifted with little labor or difficulty.
*Mandibularum liquor* <note>liquid of the jaws</note> is oil extracted from jawbones <note>likely referring to the jawbones of pike or other fish, used in early modern pharmacology</note>.

For those shells at a certain time of year, when the generative hour of the year stimulates them, open themselves with a kind of yawn; they thirst for dew as if for a husband, they gape with desire for it, and wish to be filled with the dewy conception. Soon, when the lunar sprays are melted, they drink in the dew—the moisture they longed for—through the matrix as if it were gaping, and they become pregnant. Then they bring forth pearls according to the quality of the dew conceived. If they conceive pure dew, the pearl appears with a marvelous whiteness; but if they conceive turbid dew, the offspring becomes dirty. Pearls grow pale when the sky threatens storms; if they were conceived [then], or if the shells are made to gape at the proper time, the pearls grow large. If it lightens at the time of mating, the shells are squeezed shut, or they bring forth pearls according to the measure of their fasting <note>meaning the pearl is stunted or small due to the shell closing prematurely</note>. ...the shells are pressed, and then they produce "abortions," which they call *physemata* <note>original: "physemata"; refers to hollow, bubble-like pearls that are considered incomplete or failed births of the shell</note>. They say if frequent thunder is heard after their conception, the shells spit the pearls into the rivers; thus they are often found in rivers, such as the Moselle, among the sands. A pearl becomes clearer if the shells receive the "seed of the morning air"—that is, the dew—rather than the evening dew. There is such a great affinity between the sky and the shell in their union with the dew, which is, as it were, the seed of the air. Because of this miraculous union, Pearls are dedicated to Venus <note>the Roman goddess of love, often associated with the sea and beauty</note>. It is called *unio* <note>original: "unio"; a term for a large, high-quality pearl found alone</note> because no two are ever found together. Its value lies in its whiteness, size, weight, roundness, and smoothness. While in the water, every pearl is soft like coral; once removed to the air, it immediately hardens and becomes stone. They are beneficial against fainting and heart palpitations, they comfort the spirits, are effective against the flux of blood, lientery <term>a type of diarrhea where food is passed undigested</term>, and diarrhea; they aid the vision, regulate menstruation, clean the teeth, and clear the blood.

*Magnesia is the female.*
*Mars* <note>the planetary name for the metal</note> *is iron.*

*Masculine and feminine seed refers to the substance of Mercury and Sulfur.*
*Massalis, masserium, mossel, and mother* <note>original: "Massalis, masserium, mossel, mater"</note> *all refer to Mercury.*
*Metallic matter, likewise, is a mixture of copper, lead, and silver.*

1. **Prime matter.**
2. **The Philosophical Stone.**

This list illustrates the highly metaphorical and guarded language used in the chemical arts to describe primary substances.</summary>

36. White Magnesia, The Magnet <note>original: "Magnesia alba, Magnes"; in alchemy, "Magnesia" refers to a substance capable of "attracting" the celestial spirit like a magnet</note>.

*Maturation* is an exaltation <note>a process of refining or raising a substance to a higher state</note> from raw crudeness to a mature and perfect state.

*Maturation* is fourfold: Digestion and Circulation, both of which are carried out by a gentle heat of the first degree <note>the lowest alchemical heat, roughly equal to the warmth of a nesting bird</note>, or thereabouts, in dung, a water-bath, olive-oil dregs <note>original: "amurca"</note>, chopped straw, a dry vapor-bath, hay, moisture, grape-seeds in the sun, etc. Then follows Fermentation and Projection <term>the final step where the alchemical "Medicine" is thrown upon base metals to transmute them into gold or silver</term>.

*Spot* <note>original: "Macula"</note> is a blurring of the eye, a cataract.
*Madic* is the milk left after butter <note>buttermilk</note>.
*Magister* (Master) is one who prepares salt.
*Magister* (Master) is one who smelts iron.
*Magistery* <note>original: "Magisterium"</note> is a chemical species elaborated and exalted from the whole substance, without extraction, with only the external impurities removed. In this process, all natural and homogeneous parts of the solid are preserved. But they are so exalted that they nearly reach the dignity of essences; for this reason, almost the same quantity or mass remains as nature gave it, although it cannot always be prevented...

# CHEMICAL DICTIONARY
<page-num>133</page-num>

<header>CHEMICAL DICTIONARY</header>

<note>This sentence continues from the previous page's discussion on maturation and heat.</note> ...but rather that it departs somewhat along with foreign elements and things clinging to it from the outside; and sometimes it is neglected on purpose for a specific end and use. From these factors, that which Physicians <note>original: "Physici"; referring to natural philosophers or doctors of the time</note> call **alteration** is understood to be most effective here.

*Magistery of Consistency* <note>original: "Magisterium Consistentiæ"; a Magistery is a "masterly" alchemical preparation that changes a substance's form without losing its essence</note> is when, while the essence is preserved, the consistency is changed to a more excellent state. This is done in several ways, not so much so that any nobility is added to the thing itself, but so that it becomes more suitable for various uses.

*Magistery of Fixed Things* is when volatile and spirituous bodies are fixed into a state of constancy. "Fixed" is the opposite of "inconstant" and "volatile"; both are properly judged by their reaction to fire. That which withstands the force of fire is "fixed"; that which does not is "volatile," since it flees from the heat and flies away like a spirit or a spark. It is also the process by which that which is soft is hardened.

*Magistery of Scent* is when a thing is exalted <note>refined or raised to a higher state</note> by its smell. Alchemists especially strive for this in works where some unpleasant odor occurs during the processing. This occurs in the burnt smells <term>empyreumata: the "burnt" or "smoky" odor resulting from the overheating of organic substances in a still</term> of oils, waters, and the like; and also in those things which otherwise by nature have a bad, troublesome, or even no odor, yet must be improved for the sake of the thing's pleasing use.

*Magistery of Principles* is when a composite structure is resolved back into its primary principles <note>the basic building blocks, like the Tria Prima (Salt, Sulfur, and Mercury)</note> once again.

*Magistery of Quality* is when a thing is exalted through the specific forms into which it has been worked. This happens in two ways: according to "hidden" <note>occult</note> or "manifest" qualities.

*Magistery of Hidden Quality* is when a perfect exaltation occurs in those things pertaining to the "thickness" of the whole substance, which are only evident through experience by their effects. If that quality is harmful for use, it is abolished entirely, sometimes with some loss of the substance; in such cases, the "perfection" looked for is not in the thing itself, but in the usefulness of the thing. In medicinal substances, both the substance and its use are observed. If it cannot be abolished entirely,

<summary>This page defines various types of alchemical "Magisteries," which are master-level processes used to refine a substance's physical state, scent, and internal qualities for practical use. It focuses on how substances are "fixed" against fire and how their "hidden" qualities are perfected or purified.</summary>

--- Page 182 ---
<meta>This page continues the definitions of various "Magisteries" (masterly chemical operations) from page 133, focusing on how alchemists refine the physical properties of substances perceived by the senses.</meta>

# CHEMICAL DICTIONARY
<page-num>134</page-num>

<header>CHEMICAL DICTIONARY</header>

...is suppressed, so that it can be used without harm. <note>This continues the final sentence from the previous page regarding the moderation of a substance's intensity.</note>

*Magistery of Obvious Quality* <note>original: "Magisterium qualitatis manifestæ"; a Magistery is a "masterly" alchemical preparation that changes a substance's form without losing its essence</note> is when a substance is refined into forms that can be perceived by the senses. This is threefold, corresponding to the various sensory qualities the Chemist <note>original: "Chymicus"; referring to an alchemist or chemical practitioner</note> works to elevate in substances. Some of these qualities relate more closely to the physical body of the substance, while others are more essential or "formal."

*Magistery of Flavor* is when an elevation occurs in the taste of a substance; thus, the pharmacy, the kitchen, and the wine cellar can all be supplied from here. The Chemist uses this most especially in correcting sharpness <note>acrimony</note> and removing tastes related to the <term>empyreuma: the burnt or smoky flavor and smell resulting from the overheating of organic matter in a vessel</term>.

*Magistery of Sensible Properties* occurs when substances are elevated by their own specific sensory qualities. This happens either through the primary sense, which is necessary for animal life, or through those qualities that provide a more pleasing grace. The primary sense is touch; therefore, the magistery of the primary tactile qualities—heat, cold, moisture, and dryness—arises from this.

*Magistery of Sound* is the process by which a sound is corrected by the Chemist. This is done most especially with minerals, where some sign of excellence is understood from the sound—as in the case of metals—or in those substances which threaten danger in the fire because of violent "spirits" breaking out with a noise and exploding everything in their way. <note>This refers to the trapped gases or "spirits" within minerals that can cause a crucible to shatter if heated too quickly.</note>

*Magistery of Powders* refers to a substance of continuous consistency being reduced to a powder. This is achieved primarily through the methods of <term>calcination: the process of heating a substance to high temperatures to drive off volatile aspects and reduce it to a fine, dry powder</term>. Because of this, the result is not infrequently called by the name "lime" <note>original: "calx"</note>, or commonly <term>alcholia: an archaic alchemical term for an extremely fine, impalpable powder</term>. The main purpose of this is to break the substance's toughness so that it can be easily crumbled.

*Magistery of Volatiles* is when a fixed substance is made "volatile" <note>turned into a gas or vapor that can rise</note>. Closely related to this is "softening," where a substance is made manageable, ductile, or even fluid from a state that was rigid, hard, and unmanageable. (Note that the term "soft" is understood somewhat more broadly here than by the natural philosophers). The techniques by which this is accomplished are extractions, macerations, <term>incerations: the process of gradually adding liquid to a dry substance while heating it until it reaches a wax-like consistency</term>, and sublimations.

<meta>catchword: solution</meta>

<summary>This page defines various categories of alchemical "Magisteries," which are processes used to refine the sensory and physical qualities of substances, such as flavor, sound, and consistency. It highlights how alchemists used these methods to transform hard materials into fine powders or volatile vapors for medicinal and practical use.</summary>

<keywords>magistery, flavor, empyreuma, sensory qualities, mineral sound, calcination, alcholia, volatile, inceration, sublimation</keywords>

# CHEMICAL DICTIONARY
<page-num>135</page-num>

<header>CHEMICAL DICTIONARY</header>

<sig>K 2</sig>

...solutions and similar processes. For in these cases, we generally seek the restoration of consumed or deficient moisture, or the removal of an inherent harshness, etc. In volatile substances, however, [the goal is] that they might take on the nature of spirits through the thinning <note>original: "attenuatione"; the process of making a substance more subtle or vapor-like</note> of the essence, and so on.

*Magistery of weight* <note>original: "Magisterium ponderis"</note> occurs when a substance is elevated by its weight. This is especially required for gold and silver and is one of the marks of perfection, since gold is the heaviest among metals; for this reason, weight is also added to the external value of coins. If it is lacking, it is compensated for by art. A mass of gold or silver that is too light is boiled in urine with a coralline precipitate <note>likely a red mercury oxide</note>, or mercury is rubbed onto the gold or silver until it is compatible and becomes the same color; otherwise, they are boiled in a lixivium <note>a strong alkaline solution or lye</note> prepared from sulfur and quicklime, or they are "cemented" <note>a process of heating a metal surrounded by powdered reagents in a sealed vessel</note> with sulfur or a fixed precipitate, or by this method: *Take* half an ounce of powdered salt, the same amount of crushed brick, and a quarter-measure of <term>colcothar: the red-brown iron oxide residue remaining after the distillation of vitriol</term>, and mix them. Moisten thin plates of gold with egg white and sprinkle them with <term>calx of the Moon: silver that has been reduced to a fine powder through calcination</term>, then dry it with the previously mentioned powder. Arrange them layer upon layer <note>original: "stratum super stratum"</note> and cement them either in a box or between tiles. Gold usually turns white from this process; therefore, apply a coloring agent, and holding the metal with tongs, keep it in the fire until it glows white-hot; afterwards, quench it in urine.

*Magistery of color* <note>original: "Magisterium coloris"</note> occurs when the color of any substance is produced to its best and highest degree within its specific kind. It is not merely that a present color is elevated from a lower to a higher degree, but that which is in potential, or hidden, is called forth into action or into the manifest. Sometimes even a foreign color is introduced, following the example of whatever is most excellent in its kind. If the color is simultaneously fixed and constant, the magistery is considered more noble.

*Metaphysical Magic* <note>original: "Magia Metaphysica"</note> is an art permitted to Christians, being neither sorcerous <note>original: "venefica"; literally "poison-making" or "witchcraft"</note> nor superstitious, but supernatural, through which any hidden secrets are revealed, just as manual labor requires mechanical industry.

<page-num>114</page-num>

...[is] restrained, so that it can be used without harm <note>original: "citra noxam"; likely referring to the removal of toxicity from a medicinal substance</note>.

*Magistery of manifest quality* <note>original: "Magisterium qualitatis manifestæ"</note> occurs when a substance is refined into forms perceived by the senses. This is threefold, corresponding to the types of sensory qualities that the chemist is occupied with exalting in things. Some of these more closely concern the physical body of nature, while others are more formal <note>original: "formaliora"; referring to the essential qualities or "form" of a substance in the Aristotelian sense</note>.

*Magistery of flavor* is when there is an elevation in taste. Thus, the apothecary’s shop, the kitchen, and the wine cellar can all be supplied from this art. The chemist uses this most especially for correcting sharp acidity <note>original: "acrimonia"</note> and for removing tastes associated with <term>empyreuma: a scorched or burnt smell and taste that occurs when organic matter is overheated in a closed vessel</term>.

*Magistery of the senses proper* occurs when things are exalted by their own sensory qualities. This happens either in the primary sense, which is necessary for the life of animals, or in those qualities intended for a better state. The primary sense is touch; therefore, the magistery of the primary tactile qualities—which are heat, cold, moisture, and dryness—arises from this.

*Magistery of sound* is that by which sound is corrected by the chemist. This is done most especially with minerals, where some mark of excellence is gathered from the sound—as with metals—or with substances that threaten danger in the fire because of violent spirits that burst out with a noise and explode everything in their path.

*Magistery of powders*—that is, a solid consistency brought into the state of a powder—is primarily achieved through methods of <term>calcination: the process of heating a substance to high temperatures to drive off volatile aspects, often reducing it to a fine ash</term>. Because of this, it is not uncommonly called by the name "lime," or commonly <term>alcholia: an alchemical term for an extremely fine, impalpable powder</term>. The main goal here is to break the substance's internal toughness so that it may be easily crumbled <note>original: "friari"</note>.

*Magistery of volatiles* occurs when a volatile substance <note>one that easily evaporates or turns to gas</note> is created from a fixed one. Closely related to this is "softening," in which a substance is made manageable, ductile, or even fluid—for "soft" is understood somewhat more broadly here than among the natural philosophers—from something rigid, hard, and unmanageable. The techniques by which this is accomplished are extractions, macerations, <term>incerations: the process of slowly adding liquid to a dry substance until it reaches a waxy consistency</term>, and <term>sublimations: heating a solid until it becomes a vapor and then solidifies again, used for purification</term>.

...solutions and similar things. For here, what is almost always sought is the restoration of consumed or deficient moisture, or the removal of overcome harshness <note>original: "asperitatis convictæ"</note>, etc. In volatile substances, however, [it is sought] that they might take on the nature of spirits through the thinning of their essence, and so forth.

*Magistery of weight* <note>original: "Magisterium ponderis"</note> occurs when a thing is exalted by its weight. This is especially required in gold and silver, and it is one of the marks of perfection; since gold is the heaviest among metals, weight is also added to its external value in coinage. If this weight is lacking, it is compensated for by art.

A mass of gold or silver that is lighter than it ought to be is boiled in urine with <term>coralline precipitate: a red form of mercuric oxide used to add weight or color</term>, or <term>hydrargyrus: mercury</term> is rubbed onto the gold or silver until it is compatible and made the same color. Alternatively, they are boiled in a lye prepared from sulfur and <term>quicklime: calcium oxide</term>, or they are <term>cemented: a process of heating a metal in contact with a powdered reagent to change its properties</term> with sulfur or a "fixed precipitate," or by this method: 

Take half an ounce of pulverized salt, the same amount of crushed brick, and a quarter [measure] of <term>colcothar: the red oxide of iron remaining after the distillation of vitriol</term>; mix them. Sprinkle gold plates with egg white and <term>calx of the Moon: silver that has been calcined or reduced to a powder</term>, then dry them with the previous powder. Arrange them layer upon layer and cement them either in a box or between tiles. The gold usually turns white from this; therefore, apply a coloring agent, and holding the metal with tongs, keep it in the fire until it glows white-hot, then quench it in urine.

*Magistery of color* <note>original: "Magisterium coloris"</note> is when the color of any thing is brought to the best and highest degree within its sought-after kind. Nor is the present color merely raised from a lower degree to a higher one, but that which is "in potential"—or hidden—is called forth into action or into the manifest. Sometimes even a foreign color is introduced, following the example of what is most excellent in its kind. If the color is both fixed and constant, the magistery is more noble.

*Metaphysical Magic* is an art permitted to Christians, neither sorcerous nor superstitious, but supernatural, through which any hidden secrets are revealed, just as mechanical industry is a work of the hands.

*Magnet* <note>original: "Magnes"</note>, is called the Herculean stone; and *Syderitis* <note>original: "Syderitis," from the Greek word for iron</note>, because it attracts iron; for among the Greeks, *sideros* <note>original: "σίδηρος"</note> signifies iron. It always turns itself toward the north. It is a stone with a color verging toward blue (the blueish one is always the best), dense, not particularly heavy, attracting iron by a certain natural power <note>original: "dynami"</note>. And iron, though nothing is more stubborn in its hardness, yields to the magnet and is acted upon by it: and iron touched by a magnet draws other iron. But if the magnet is smeared with the juice of garlic or onions, it will not attract iron at all <note>This was a common, though incorrect, belief in ancient and medieval science regarding the "antipathy" between garlic and magnets</note>. The magnet is found in veins of iron; a magnet burned either by the heat of the earth or by artificial fire becomes <term>hematite: a reddish-black mineral of iron ore</term>, and is sold as such. *Pliny* <note>Gaius Plinius Secundus, the Roman natural historian</note> lists five types of Magnets, and *Ruland* <note>Martin Ruland the Elder, author of a famous alchemical dictionary</note> does so on page 315.

*Magnesia*, is the shell <note>original: "testudo"</note> or Sulfur.
*Magnesia*, is the woman <note>In alchemy, "magnesia" often refers to the feminine or receptive principle of a substance</note>.
*Maltheorum*, is <term>rock salt: naturally occurring sodium chloride</term>.
*Manbruck*, is silver.
*Mamheb*, is <term>scoria: the dross or slag left after smelting metal</term>.
*Marched*, is <term>litharge: a lead oxide mineral</term>.
*Markasita*, is <term>Calcitheos: an old term for marcasite or copper ores</term>.
*Maruch oil*, is the same metal.
*Marble*: *Ruland* lists various types of marbles from page 320 to 321.
*Morcellus*, is a large hammer.
*Martegon*, is <term>Silphium: an ancient plant, likely a species of giant fennel, prized in medicine</term>.
*Marfarea*, is <term>mouse-ear: a type of hawkweed (Hieracium pilosella)</term>.
*Masellum, Mosel*, is <term>Jupiter: the alchemical name for the metal Tin</term>.
*Medea’s gem*, is brought from the region of the Medes. It is black, having veins of a golden color, yielding a saffron-colored juice and the taste of wine; it has an immense effect against drunkenness and for winning love, as the Magi relate.
*The Mid-heaven of the Moon*, is Margesia.
*White, fluid marrow of the Torgensian stones.*
*Ruland* enumerates 14 species on page 326.

*Melanteria* <note>original: "Melanteria," from the Greek for "blackening"</note>, *a metallic form of chalcantum* <note>original: "calchanti," usually referring to copperas or vitriol</note>, *that is, shoemaker’s blacking, which is a species of vitriol of a dark blue color. *Otherwise called Melanterion, it is the scrapings of old iron nails, or shoemaker's ink, similar to what is commonly made by our own shoemakers today. The fossil variety is superior, specifically that which takes on the color of sulfur, and which is light, pure, and uniform, and which turns black immediately when touched by water (or even just the finger, as the Arabs claim).*

*Melisea or Melision, is understood as Manna obtained from the more noble herbs by means of a* <term>magistery: a master-process or expert chemical preparation</term>.

*Michae, is Venus* <note>In alchemical symbolism, "Venus" represents the metal copper</note>.

*Michach, is Copper.*

*Mifros, is Asphalt.*

*Mineral vessel, is a vessel made from mineral matter, whether metallic or earthen.*

*Misserassi, is of gypsum.*

*Mixture* <note>original: "Mistio"</note>, *is a composition.*
