***Melian Earth*** <note>original: *Terra Melia*</note>, was once brought from the Island of *Melos*. In color it is similar to ashen-gray *Eretria* earth <note>a type of medicinal earth from Euboea</note>, rough to the touch, and crackling when bitten in the manner of pumice; it has the properties of alum. *Dioscorides* <note>Pedanius Dioscorides (c. 40–90 AD), a Greek physician and botanist whose "De Materia Medica" was the primary pharmacological text for 1,500 years</note>. It is a stone with which barbers commonly wipe their basins. It is pale, ranging between gray and yellow, and it cleans mirrors and everything that ought to be kept clean. Every earth should be chosen that is fresh, soft, not stony, and friable <note>easily crumbled</note>, which is easily dissolved.
***Potter's Earth*** <note>original: *Terra figula*</note>, is clay or chalk.
***Ampelitis Earth*** <note>original: *Terra ampelitis*; from the Greek *ampelos*, meaning "vine-earth"</note>, used for vines or as a medicine, is found in Seleucia in Syria; it is especially praiseworthy, black, very similar to bitumen <note>a naturally occurring petroleum product</note>, splintering into flakes, resembling coals with a uniform shine, and it dissolves in oil. It has the power of dispersing and cooling.
***Earths*** <note>original: *Terræ*</note>, are found in mines, which are similar to clay in color, but are distinguished only by their dryness, from which they are formed. Some are white, some black (which is *nigitis*), some yellow (which is ochre), some pumice-like, some purple or red (which is *sandaracha* <note>realgar or red arsenic, often used as a pigment</note>), some are green (which is *chrysocolla* <note>literally "gold-glue," usually referring to malachite or copper silicate</note>), some are ashen, and some are blue, called *hyanon* by the Greeks and *caeruleum* by the Latins.
***Lemnian Earth*** <note>original: *Terra Lemnia*</note>, so called because it was brought from Lemnos. *Avicenna* <note>Ibn Sina (c. 980–1037), a Persian polymath and one of the most influential physicians of the Islamic Golden Age</note> calls it "sealed earth" <note>original: *terra sigillata*</note> and "sealed clay," because it was marked by the Priests of Diana with an image

...of Diana, under whose protection it was, or of the Goat, which was mixed with goat's blood; from which it is named *sphragis argos* <note>original: *sphragida argos*; from the Greek for "white seal" or "shining seal," though the text interprets it as "seal of the goat"</note> (that is) the seal of the Goat. In its place today, some use stamped potter's clay, as Platearius <note>Mattheus Platearius, a 12th-century physician from the school of Salerno</note> advises. God created humans from red earth; on the other hand, man is dissolved back into earth, and earth serves as an antidote for him against poisons and the most serious diseases. It is held that this very earth, especially Lemnian earth, is most conformed to the temperament of human nature, which is well established.

There is a **sealed earth** <term>Terra sigillata: "sealed earth," medicinal clay pressed into small cakes and stamped with an official seal to guarantee its authenticity and origin</term> belonging to his Imperial Majesty <note>Likely referring to the Holy Roman Emperor Rudolf II, who was famously obsessed with alchemy and medicine</note>, both white and red, marked with a scorpion; which, prepared by the Emperor himself, is not at all inferior in power to the **Turkish Lemnian earth**.

***Eretrian Earth*** <note>original: *Terra Eretriades*; a medicinal earth from Eretria on the island of Euboea</note>. Dioscorides <note>An ancient Greek physician and the father of pharmacology</note> posits two species of this earth: the white and the ash-colored. Since it is soft, when it is drawn across copper tools, it leaves a violet line; and then it is at its best. He teaches that it has the power of binding <note>astringency</note>, cooling, softening, filling up hollows <note>such as wounds or ulcers</note>, and knitting together bloody things. Pliny <note>Roman naturalist and author of *Natural History*</note> mentions in Book 35, Chapter 16, that it is used in a loosened and prepared state.

***Chian Earth*** <note>original: *Terra Chia*; from the island of Chios</note>. White and flaky, it is similar to Samian earth in both form and virtue. It is especially recommended for improving the color of the body; in baths, it cleanses the skin of women better than soda <note>original: *nitro*; referring to natron or mineral soda</note>. This earth is found in many places in **Saxony**.

***Samian Earth*** <note>original: *Terra Samia*; from the island of Samos</note>, according to Dioscorides, is of two types:
1. White and light, sticking to the tongue like glue when touched, soft, moist, and friable <note>easily crumbled</note>; this is the kind which others call *collyrion* <note>a term for medicinal cakes or eye-salves</note>.
2. The other is flaky and very dense, like a whetstone. They are burned and washed like the Eretrian earths, and are similar to them in power. They stop the vomiting of blood and discharges of the womb, and are beneficial for inflammations of the testicles and breasts, as well as for poisons and the bites of serpents.

***Selinusian Earth*** <note>original: *Terra Selinusia*; from Selinus in Sicily</note> has the same nature as Chian and Samian earth, especially the white variety that glitters. Or

**Cimolian Earth** <term>Terra Cimolia: a white or grayish clay from the island of Cimolus, used by the ancients for both cleaning cloth and as a medicinal cooling agent</term>. Regarding this earth, **Dioscorides** <note>An ancient Greek physician and pharmacologist</note> identifies two types. One is white, and the other inclines toward a purplish color. The best is that which has a natural oiliness and feels cold to the touch. Its power, when diluted, is for cleansing, drawing out, and cooling.

**Tin** or **tisatum** is Sulfur.

**Tiletten** is a thin plate of iron.

**Tapsaria** is barley water. A ptisan <note>a medicinal infusion or tea</note>.

**Tincture** is a specific secret <term>arcanum: a specialized medicinal or alchemical preparation believed to contain the essential "spirit" or power of a substance</term> containing the essence and formal qualities, including the color of a thing, so that it can transform <note>original: *tingere*; literally "to dye" or "to tinge," but in alchemy, it implies a fundamental transformation of nature</note> another substance into a nature similar to itself. If this is perfectly prepared, it appears as a very thin, transparent substance, as if it were pure and clear air, yet colored in a certain way, with the color not blocking its transparency. Therefore, it can be kept for a long time without any sediment. Nor is it sought so much for the sake of the work of preparation, as for its...

**Tincture is twofold:** { **Open**, and
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;**Produced**.

* **Open Tincture** is that which, by the impulse of nature, offers itself to be seen upon the surface: such as is frequently found in minerals.*

* **Produced Tincture** is that which is brought forth and extracted from the center to the surface. For in most other things, the heat and virtue reside in a hidden recess—what others call being "in potential" <note>original: *in potentia*</note>—whence it must be brought forth through modes of exaltation <term>Exaltation: an alchemical process intended to raise a substance to a higher state of purity or potency</term> before it is extracted. The most noble of these are the tinctures of metals, though some are also found in the category of vegetables.*

* **Titanos** is lime made from gypsum.*

* **Topazion** <note>the gemstone Topaz</note> is named after a misty island in the Red Sea; and from this island, **Pliny** <note>Roman naturalist, *Natural History* Book 37, Ch. 8</note> relates that this gem was first brought to Queen Berenice of Egypt. **Albertus** <note>Albertus Magnus, 13th-century scholar</note>, **Evax** <note>a legendary king associated with early lapidaries</note>, and others say one species of it is like gold, while another surpasses gold in its saffron-yellow color. They say a power resides in them against lusts, and that they make men chaste and courageous.*

* **Trapha** is a Box.*

* **Tricor** is gold.*

* **Trigias** is Tartar <note>the potassium bitartrate crust found in wine barrels</note>; *trigia* means dregs or lees.*

* **Tenacity of glue** <note>original: *Tenacitas glutinis*</note> is mineral resin, just as pine resin comes from the fir tree, or turpentine from the larch.*

* **Earth of gold** is golden litharge <term>Litharge: a lead oxide mineral produced during the refining of silver or gold</term>.*

* **Earth of silver** is silver litharge.*

* **Faithful earth** is silver, which in other contexts is called "celestial earth,"*

# CHEMICAL DICTIONARY.

->O 4<-

...lestial, and "terrestrial heaven," if silver is taken to be the Moon; by which same reasoning, silver could be called "celestial Moon."

***Terra hispanica*** <note>Spanish Earth</note> is vitriol <term>Vitriol: a sulfate mineral, often used in making acids</term>.
***Tinkar*** is borax. It is also called the "shackle of gold" <note>original: *Capistrum auri*; borax was used as a flux for soldering gold</note>.
***Tincture*** <note>original: *Tinctura*</note> is so called because it penetrates and dyes other bodies with its own color, much like saffron cast into water.
***Thamatica*** <note>Thaumatics</note> is the name of the art through which mechanical operations moved by air or water were invented.
***Thapneus*** is a medicine that is clean and repurified.
***Thisma*** is a duct of minerals or subterranean veins.
***Toruscula*** is the name for a drop, such as a "drop of pine" <note>original: *toruscula pini*</note> for a drop of resin from a pine or fir tree, using the whole to represent the part.
***Turba magna*** <note>The Great Crowd</note> is the innumerable multitude of stars in the firmament of heaven. It is also an omen or prophecy drawn from everything contained within the fourfold lower world of the elements.
***Turbit minerale*** <note>Mineral Turbith</note> is mercury precipitated into a sweet state without the use of any corrosive agent.
***Trachsat*** is metal still existing within its own mineral ore.
***Trarames*** are the invisible actions of shadows and specters, which are nonetheless heard.
***Trifertes*** are said to be spirits inhabiting fire.
***Trigonum*** <note>The Trigon</note> is a fourfold transmutation of the spirits of the stars, according to the number of the four elements. Each one rules and lasts for two hundred years. For example, when the Fiery Trigon begins, the superior planets always have their conjunction in a fiery sign for as long as this trigon lasts, and so on for the others.
***Tronus*** and ***Tronossa*** is the name given to celestial dew born from the air, the sweetest species of manna; it is thick, tenacious, and whiter than all others, [formed] from the mercury of the middle region...

<summary>This page provides dictionary definitions for various alchemical substances, subterranean phenomena, and astrological concepts, including definitions of borax, mineral veins, and the "Trigon" cycles of the stars.</summary>
<keywords>vitriol, borax, tincture, mechanics, mineral veins, Turba Magna, Mineral Turbith, specters, fire spirits, astrological trigons, manna, celestial dew</keywords>

<page-num>204</page-num>

# CHEMICAL DICTIONARY.

->V.<-

...generated in the middle, and entirely separated from all sulfur and salt; it falls in the spring and summer, and is found in the morning adhering to the leaves of trees like other manna.

***Truphat*** is the hidden power of minerals, which compels and advances each metal into its predestined state of being.

***Transudation*** <note>original: *Transudatio*</note> occurs when, during "distillation by descent," the enticed essence sweats through and is carried drop by drop into the receiving vessel by heat applied from above. For the hearth is located above, around the work-chamber, which is why it is said to be driven by a "circular fire" <note>An alchemical technique where the heat source is placed around or above the vessel to force vapors downward</note>. This is performed in a furnace of descent, a bath of descent, a sand-bath of descent, or by similar arts.

***Iron tripod*** <note>original: *Tripes ferreus*</note> is a vessel—or rather an iron support—designed to receive and hold another vessel containing the material to be heated.

***Tritorium*** (commonly called a ***funnel*** <note>original: *infundibulum*</note>) is a vessel for transferring liquids, wide at the top and gradually tapering to a point, formed with an attached handle.

***Tumbil*** is earth.

***Tumbaba*** or ***Timpabar*** is "living sulfur" <note>original: *sulphur vivum*; naturally occurring native sulfur</note>.

***Turpeth*** <note>original: *Turpethum*</note> is a specific, fixed coagulate. For this reason, even when impurities are removed and wild volatility is separated out, it remains at the bottom of the vessels. It is prepared chiefly from minerals whose essence and specific power reside primarily in their stable part—or at least it is reduced to this form so it may be suited for practical use. The word *Turpeth* crept into chemistry from the Arabic language, where they use it to designate either the bark or the root of a fennel-like plant (though it was later adapted to a medicine made from a similar type of spurge <term>Spurge: plants of the genus *Euphorbia*, often used as purgatives</term>). This was perhaps due to a similar effect observed in Mercury when it is reduced by certain methods into a secret remedy <term>Arcanum: a deep secret or a powerful medicine in alchemy</term>. Therefore, lest anyone should mistake it for the vegetable *Turbith*, they added the word "Mineral."

<meta>catchword: Vegetable-</meta>

## V.

**V**egetables <note>original: *Vegetabilia*</note> are green things having their roots fixed in the earth and their trunks and branches in the air, such as herbs, plants, and trees.

*Verto* is the fourth part of a pound.

*Philosophers' Glass* <note>original: *Vitrum Philosophorum*</note> is an alembic <note>A double-vessel apparatus used for distillation</note>; however, when the term is used on its own, it refers to a woolen sleeve in the manner of a "Hippocratic sleeve" <note>A conical fabric filter bag used for straining liquids</note>, or like a small tongue through which liquids are clarified.

*Corrected wine* <note>original: *Vinum correctum*</note> is alcohol of wine <note>Rectified or highly distilled spirits</note>.

*Extracted wine* <note>original: *Vinum effatum*</note> is that which has drawn out the virtues of herbs or of other things over which it was poured <note>An infusion or tincture</note>.

*Greenness of salt* <note>original: *Viriditas salis*</note> is its liquid or its green oil.

*Viscaleus* is a branched, sticky growth originating on trees, such as the mistletoe of the oak, of apple trees, etc.

*Viscus* properly is:
- Pitch boiled from ashes, or
- From the pods <note>original: *Locustis*; likely referring to carob pods or seed pods</note> of trees.

*Viscus of the second generation* is the gore <note>original: *cruor*</note> which descends from salts.

*Vision* <note>original: *Visio*</note> is said to be a supernatural appearance of spirits, visible in some way to human eyes.

*Liquefied or vitriolated vitriol* <note>original: *Vitriolum liquefactum, aut vitriolatum*</note> is vitriol extracted from the mine and in liquid form, which can no longer be coagulated; or it is *calcanthum* <note>A historical name for copperas or blue vitriol (copper sulfate)</note> extracted from a copper mine.

*New vitriol* <note>original: *Vitriolum novum*</note> is white vitriol <note>Zinc sulfate</note>.

*Roman vitriol* <note>original: *Vitriolum Romanum*</note> is that which is most green, like green ink <note>Ferrous sulfate</note>.

*Volans* and *Vnquas* are terms for quicksilver <note>Mercury</note>.

*Valrat* and *Vlcac* are terms for a leaf.

<header>CHEMICAL DICTIONARY</header>

*Vapor*, smoke.

*Crude vessel* <note>original: *Vas crudum*</note> is a new vessel.

*Small vessel* <note>original: *Vas minus*</note>, by which mineral substances are extracted, is called a *Robel*.

*Metallic vessel*, made from metal, serves for refinement <note>original: *subtiliationi*</note> or melting.

Serving for refinement, it is either:
- A Copper Kettle <note>original: *Ahenum*</note>, or
- A Bladder <note>original: *Vesica*; a distillation vessel named for its shape</note>.

*Melting vessels* <note>original: *Vasa fusoria*</note> are those containing the material itself, by whose aid minerals are melted, such as an ash-cup <note>original: *catillus cinereus*; a cupel used in assaying</note> and a crucible.

*Vehicle* <note>original: *Vehiculum*</note> is a liquid or spirit in which oils and chemical secrets <note>original: *arcana*</note> are provided to patients for a more pleasant taste or easier administration.

*Vein of Venus* <note>original: *Vena Veneris*</note> is vervain <note>An herb traditionally associated with the planet Venus</note>.

*To strip the veins of their skin* and *to open the vein*. <note>Metaphorical mining terms for preparing a mineral for extraction</note>

*To smear the vein* with mud where it is rich in metal, or to cover it with earth, rocks, a plank, or a stake.

*Vener* is Mercury.

*Poison* <note>original: *Venenum*, also German *Gifft*</note> is the Stone of Mercury.

*Venus* or *Murpur* is Copper.

*Horse's belly* <note>original: *Venter equi*</note> is horse dung <note>Used as a source of gentle, constant heat for digesting or fermenting chemical mixtures</note>.

*White wind* <note>original: *Ventus albus*</note> is Mercury.

*Red wind* <note>original: *Ventus rubeus*</note> is red orpiment <note>A red arsenic sulfide mineral</note>.

*Citrine wind* <note>original: *Ventus Citrinus*</note> is Sulfur.

*Winds* <note>original: *Venti*</note> are spirits <note>Volatile or gaseous substances</note>.

*Vermicylum* is either:
- An Elixir, or
- A Tincture.

*Vermilion* is either:
- Cinnabar <note>Mercury sulfide</note>, or
- Minium <note>Red lead</note>.

<page-num>207</page-num>
<header>CHEMICAL DICTIONARY</header>

*Vernece* is gummy varnish <note>original: *vernix guttosa*</note>.

*Vlrach* is dragon's blood <note>original: *sanguis Draconis*; a bright red resin obtained from certain plants, used as a pigment and medicine</note>.

*Vesica* <note>meaning "bladder," named for its shape</note> is a metallic vessel made from copper in the shape of a bladder or an egg. It is fashioned two or three feet high and of a suitable width, with an open base designed to receive a matching alembic <note>a distillation cap</note> within its fold. This alembic must fit the opening of the *vesica* so perfectly that they are tightly sealed together, ensuring not the slightest bit of vapor can escape. A cap placed upon its summit must send out a pipe two or three feet high, which supports another cap at the top. This top piece is surrounded by a kettle that always contains cold water, allowing the spirits rising into the alembic to be cooled and condensed. From this cap, a spigot <note>original: *epistomium*</note> or channel extends, which leads the condensed vapors drop by drop into a vessel placed beneath it.

The use of this *Vesica* should not be neglected when working with vegetable substances.

*Green of copper* <note>original: *Viride aeris*</note>,  
*Greek green* <note>original: *Viride Graecum*</note>, **Is the rust of copper.** <note>original: *aerugo aeris*; commonly known as verdigris</note>  
*Or Spanish green* <note>original: *Vel Hispanicum*</note>,

*Glass* <note>original: *Vitrum*</note>; there are 5 types of glass:

1. *Of European glasses*, the most excellent are those called Venetian, which are compared to Crystal.
2. *Asian glasses* were the most precious ones from Sarepta, called Sidonian; they were compared to Electrum <note>a naturally occurring alloy of gold and silver, or sometimes referring to amber</note>.
3. *Glass dross* <note>original: *recrementa vitri*</note>, which is greenish in white glass.
4. *Reddish glass dross*.
5. *Glass* used to tint metals.

*Vitrification* <note>original: *Vitrificatio*</note> is a burning process that converts limes <note>original: *calces*</note> and ashes into transparent glass.

*Gall of glass* <note>original: *Vitri fel*</note>, **In German: *Glassgall*.** <note>The scum or dross that forms on the surface of molten glass, often used in alchemy as a purifying agent</note>  
*Scum of glass* <note>original: *Spuma vitri*</note>,

<page-num>208</page-num>
<header>CHEMICAL DICTIONARY</header>

*Black glass* <note>original: *Vitrum nigrum*</note>, Smalt <note>original: *Smalta*; a deep blue pigment made from powdered cobalt glass, often appearing nearly black in concentrated form</note>.

*Venus’s Navel* <note>original: *Vmbilicus Veneris*</note> is a stone or gem, sometimes red, white, or purple in color. It is called *Venus’s Navel* because while it is flat on one side, the other side is raised with two lines (much like the shells of small snails), resembling a round, apple-like belly and the navel of a beautiful girl. However, the Germans, looking more to its shape—which on the raised side closely resembles a large bean—and to the place where it is found, call it the "sea bean" <note>likely referring to the operculum of certain sea snails, such as the *Turbo castanea*, sometimes called "Shiva’s Eye" today</note>; for it is found in shells by the sea, along the shores. It is said to grant grace to young women and to be very effective for beauty, love, and for the ailments of girls.

*Vngosa* are the excrements of humans and cattle.
*Vngula* is a disease of the eye, or a "nail" <note>original: *clavus*; typically referring to a pterygium, a fleshy growth on the white of the eye</note>.
*Saffron Ointment* <note>original: *Vnguentum Croceum*</note> is the Elixir <note>the "philosopher's stone" in liquid form, often associated with a golden or saffron color</note>.
*Urine of boys* <note>original: *Vrina puerorum*</note> is mercury <note>in alchemical texts, this can refer to actual distilled urine or, more often, a specific "pure" state of the metallic principle Mercury</note>.
*Vsfida* is the dross of gold <note>original: *Scoria auri*; the refuse or slag left over after smelting</note>.

*Vrsud*, }  
*Vsrub*, } **Is Saturn.** <note>The alchemical name for the metal lead</note>  
*Vzurup*, }

*Oak grape* <note>original: *Vva quercina*</note> was unknown to the ancient writers, yet it is a matter of great importance. It is a certain concretion in the shape of a grape, found at the roots of oak trees beneath the earth during the springtime, perhaps formed from the excess moisture of the roots. It has an entirely astringent <note>sharp or drying</note> taste, with a purple color on the outside and a white, almost milky interior. In the summer, that "grape" perishes and becomes wood-like. Therefore, it must be collected in the spring at the roots of oaks, dried in the shade, and then turned into powder. Nothing is more effective than that powder for dysentery and for every flow of blood. Ruland <note>Martin Ruland the Elder (1522–1602), a noted physician and author of this lexicon</note> affirms that he has cured several patients suffering from dysentery with that single oak grape.

<meta>catchword: Vvae (Grapes)</meta>

<page-num>209</page-num>
<header>CHEMICAL DICTIONARY</header>

*The Grapes of Hermes,* <note>original: *Vuæ Hermetis*; a metaphorical term for the core stages of the alchemical "Great Work"</note>
{ Distillation,
{ Solution,
{ Sublimation,
{ Calcination,
{ Fixation.

*Venus* <note>In this context, the name of the goddess refers to venereal disease</note> is the "French Disease" <note>original: *Morbus Gallicus*; an early name for syphilis</note>. See Paracelsus, in the book *The Great Surgery* <note>original: *Chyrurg. magna*</note>, folio 463, page 2. St. Vitus's Dance <note>original: *S. Viti morbus*; a disorder characterized by rapid, jerky involuntary movements</note> is described by Paracelsus in volume 2, page 360.

*The preparation of white vitriol* <note>original: *Vitrioli albi*; zinc sulfate</note> for defects of the eyes. Paracelsus, *On Preparations*, page 655.

*Volatile* <note>original: *Volatile*</note> is a general term for any very light matter that is easily carried upward, such as ash, dust, and similar things.

*An emetic* <note>original: *Vomitivum*</note> is the name for any medicine that purges by driving matter upward through vomiting.

*Vulcanists,* or *Vulcanics* <note>original: *Vulcanii, vel Vulcanici*; named after Vulcan, the Roman god of fire and smithing</note>, are the names given to laborers or craftsmen who are always working around fire by forging and melting.

*A consuming ulcer* <note>original: *Vlcus æstiomenum*; from the Greek 'esthiomenos' meaning 'eating'</note> is a cancerous abscess that eats away at the flesh.

*Lisbon-wort* <note>original: *Vlisipona*</note> is the plant *serpentaria* <note>likely Snakeroot or Birthwort</note>, which is most excellent for treating colic.

*Shadow-beings* <note>original: *Vmbragines*</note> is another name for pygmies <note>in Paracelsian lore, these are the elemental spirits of the earth</note>.

*Shadowy* or *spectral* bodies <note>original: *Vmbratiles, aut umbratilia*</note> are bodies transformed into human sight by the stars and offered by their own hidden "ascendants." These appear to the eyes no differently than ghosts <note>original: *lemures*</note>, and this occurs through magical power and efficacy.

*Waves,* or *Undines* <note>original: *Vndæ, vel Vndenæ*</note>, are the names for spirits of the air and the earth <note>the text unusually attributes them to air and earth, though they are traditionally water elementals</note>.

*Wavy matter* <note>original: *Vndosa*</note> is the name for the excrement of any animal.

*The Unity of Trithemius* <note>Johannes Trithemius was a 15th-century abbot and occultist</note> is the reduction of the "triad" <note>original: *ternarii*</note> into a union by the casting away of the "duality" <note>original: *binarii*</note>. This process is supernatural and <term>spagyric<gloss>A branch of alchemy focused on the separation and recombination of natural elements for medicine</gloss></term>; it is the foundation for the investigation of hidden things.

<meta>catchword: investigation.</meta>

...ness. <note>original: *nis*; likely the conclusion of a word such as "finis" (the end) or "operationis" (of the operation) from the preceding page</note> By this latter part of Philosophy, a transition is made to the former, through the medium of moral Philosophy.

*Urine* <note>original: *Vrina*</note> is a dissolved salt, generated in the liver, through its emunctory <note>an organ or canal that carries off waste</note>, as a superfluous excrement of salt expelled by nature.

*Urine of wine* <note>original: *Vrina vini*</note> is vinegar <note>original: *acetum*</note>; it is a term used for the urine of a person who drinks wine constantly.

*Uzifur* is what cinnabar <note>mercury sulfide</note> is called. It is also written as *Uritur* and *Uzifar*.

# X.

**X**enechtum is an external preservative against the plague hung around the neck, such as quicksilver <note>mercury</note> enclosed in a hazelnut shell, and similar devices.

*Xeni nephidei* are spirits who find it pleasant, and consider it a delight, to reveal the hidden properties of nature to men—a task which has been granted to them. <note>In the cosmology of Paracelsus, these are specific elemental or spiritual beings who act as intermediaries for natural knowledge.</note>

Xisinum, or } Is what vinegar <note>original: *acetum*</note> is called.
Xissium, }

*Xenechtum* is understood by others to refer to a virgin's first menstrual blood.

*Xenechdon*, according to Paracelsus, is a tablet of arsenic, or arsenic used against the plague; although this composition contains not only arsenic, but also toads <note>original: *rubetas*; dried toads were historically used in amulets to "draw out" plague venom</note>, dittany <note>original: *dictamnum*; a medicinal herb</note>, and other ingredients. Van Helmont calls this amulet a *Zenexton*.

*Xiston* is scraped verdigris <note>original: *ærugo raſa*; the green pigment or rust that forms on copper</note>.

## Z.

...ness. By this latter part of Philosophy, a transition is made to the former, through the medium of moral Philosophy.

**Urine** <note>original: *Vrina*</note> is a dissolved salt, generated in the liver through its emunctory <note>an organ or canal that carries off waste</note>, expelled by nature as a superfluous excrement of salt.

**Urine of wine** <note>original: *Vrina vini*</note> is vinegar; it is used to refer to the urine of a man who constantly drinks wine.

**Uzifur** is a name for cinnabar <note>mercury sulfide</note>. It is also written as *Uritur* and *Uzifar*.

## X.

**Xenechtum** is an external preservative against the plague worn around the neck, such as quicksilver <note>mercury</note> enclosed in a hazelnut shell, and similar devices.

**Xeni nephidei** are spirits who find it pleasant, and consider it a delight, to reveal the hidden properties of secret nature to humans, a task which has been granted to them.

| | |
|---|---|
| **Xisinum**, or | Is a name for vinegar. |
| **Xissium**, | |

**Xenechtum**: others understand this term to mean the first menstrual blood of a virgin.

**Xenechdon** according to Paracelsus <note>a famous Swiss physician and alchemist</note> is a tablet of arsenic, or arsenic used against the plague. Although this composition contains not only arsenic, but also toads <note>original: *rubetas*</note>, dittany <note>a medicinal herb</note>, and other ingredients. Van Helmont <note>a Flemish chemist and follower of Paracelsus</note> calls this amulet a *Zenexton*.

**Xiston** is scraped verdigris <note>original: *ærugo rasa*; the green pigment or rust that forms on copper</note>.

--- Page 276 ---
<meta>This page continues the 'Z' section of the chemical dictionary. While the previous page concluded with brief definitions of terms starting with 'Z', this page transitions into a detailed medical argument against the use of "Zenexton" amulets, citing several early modern physicians and classical authorities.</meta>

->## CHEMICAL DICTIONARY<-

<page-num>214</page-num>

I have judged it worthwhile to attach to Paracelsus’s Zenexton <note>original: *Zenechto*; a specific type of amulet, often made of dried toad or arsenic, intended to protect the wearer from the plague by "magnetic" attraction of the poison</note> the judgment of the most learned physician, Doctor Francis Herring, concerning the wearing of amulets, in his own words. My opinion is that these arsenic cakes <note>original: *Arsenici placentas*; small, flattened discs or troches made of arsenic and other substances</note>, when worn near the region of the heart, do not effect the preservation of the wearers, but are rather destructive and harmful to those carrying them; and he confirms this opinion with the views and judgments of other physicians.

*Gerardus Columbus*, M. D., affirms that he has quite often observed wearers of this kind of cake, due to the corruption of their bodies, fall into sudden fainting fits <note>original: *Lypothimias*; a Greek medical term for a sudden loss of strength or near-fainting</note>, swooning <note>original: *Syncopen*; a temporary loss of consciousness</note>, and other terrifying symptoms, which did not cease until the pouches or cakes had been removed. He also observed others who, though not instantly affected, nevertheless fell into malignant and pestilential fevers a short time later, from which they died.

*Franciscus Alphanus*, a physician of Salerno, affirms that he saw a man who carried arsenic in a pouch die a sudden death while heating himself during a match of handball <note>original: *pilæ palmariæ*; an early sport played by striking a ball with the palm of the hand</note>.

*Mattheus Hessus* also reports that he knew many who, although they carried quicksilver <note>original: *argentum vivum*; mercury</note> enclosed in a hazelnut shell hung around the neck, nevertheless died of the plague—even though the quacks <note>original: *agyrtæ*; itinerant medical charlatans who sold folk remedies</note> had bound themselves by oath to return the wearers of those amulets safe and sound.

We also read that the great Theophrastus <note>a Greek philosopher and successor to Aristotle</note> judged Pericles <note>the famous Athenian statesman</note> to be of unsound mind (and not without cause) simply because he had worn amulets.

And Francis Herring himself affirms that he saw many people in **London** who had foul, hollow holes <note>likely necrotic ulcers caused by the caustic nature of the arsenic against the skin</note> in their chests from the wearing of those cakes.

---
<meta>catchword: Vocabula</meta>

<summary>This page presents a medical critique of Paracelsian plague amulets (Zenextons), arguing that arsenic and mercury worn against the skin are toxic and ineffective. It cites multiple historical physicians and the philosopher Theophrastus to warn against these dangerous "quack" remedies.</summary>

<keywords>Zenexton, Paracelsus, Francis Herring, arsenic cakes, amulets, plague remedies, toxicology, London plague, quicksilver, medical skepticism</keywords>

I have judged it worthwhile to attach to Paracelsus’s Zenexton <note>original: *Zenechto*; a specific type of amulet, often made of dried toad or arsenic, intended to protect the wearer from the plague by "magnetic" attraction of the poison</note> the judgment of the most learned physician, Doctor Francis Herring, concerning the wearing of amulets, in his own words. My opinion is that these arsenic cakes <note>original: *Arsenici placentas*; small, flattened discs or troches made of arsenic and other substances</note>, when worn near the region of the heart, do not effect the preservation of the wearers, but are rather destructive and harmful to those carrying them; and he confirms this opinion with the views and judgments of other physicians.

*Gerardus Columbus*, M. D., affirms that he has quite often observed wearers of this kind of cake, due to the corruption of their bodies, fall into sudden fainting fits <note>original: *Lypothimias*; a Greek medical term for a sudden loss of strength or near-fainting</note>, swooning <note>original: *Syncopen*; a temporary loss of consciousness</note>, and other terrifying symptoms, which did not cease until the pouches or cakes had been removed. He also observed others who, though not instantly affected, nevertheless fell into malignant and pestilential fevers a short time later, from which they died.

*Franciscus Alphanus*, a physician of Salerno, affirms that he saw a man who carried arsenic in a pouch die a sudden death while heating himself during a match of handball <note>original: *pilæ palmariæ*; an early sport played by striking a ball with the palm of the hand, a precursor to modern tennis</note>.

-># CHEMICAL LEXICON.<-

P 3
<meta>catchword: guarding (original: "custo-")</meta>

<image-desc>A decorative horizontal woodcut band featuring a repeating symmetrical pattern of ornate foliage and fleur-de-lis motifs.</image-desc>

## *I have added here at the end of this book some chemical terms that were previously omitted from this Lexicon.*

<image-desc>A large decorative woodcut initial 'A' depicting a landscape with a figure and architectural elements within the frame of the letter.</image-desc>
The **Alchahest** <note>A hypothetical "universal solvent" sought by alchemists, capable of reducing any body to its primary liquid matter</note> of Martin Ruland, Michael Toxites, and Theophrastus Paracelsus, and the **Altahest** of Baptist Van Helmont <note>Jan Baptist van Helmont (1580–1644), a pioneering chemist who bridged the gap between alchemy and modern science</note>, appear to me to be one and the same substance, judging by each of their descriptions.

By Martin Ruland, it is described as follows:

*Alchahest* is said to be a "prepared Mercury" <note>original: "Mercurius præparatus"; mercury that has been purified or chemically altered for medicinal or alchemical use</note>. Some maintain it is tartar <note>original: "tartarum"; a salt found in wine casks, often used as a starting material for solvents</note>; they are convinced of this, I believe, by the words of *Baptist Van Helmont*, who writes as follows on page 118 concerning fevers: 
> "Learn, O Physicians, of a certain solvent that is homogeneous and unchanging, which dissolves its objects into a primary liquid matter; thus you will obtain the innermost essences of things. But if you cannot reach that secret of fire, learn at least to make the salt of tartar volatile <note>original: "salem tartari reddere volatilem"; a difficult chemical process intended to make potassium carbonate more reactive or able to be distilled</note>, so that by this means you may complete your dissolutions." 
Nevertheless, the intent of the author is easily discovered from the description of its preparation.

By Michael Toxites, the *Alchahest* is described thus:

It is Mercury prepared as a medicine for the liver; and he refers us to Paracelsus’s book on the *Powers of the Principal Organs*, where it is described by Theophrastus Paracelsus as follows:

The liquid *Alchahest* is of great power within the liver, for...

<summary>This page begins an appendix of omitted terms, focusing on the definition of the "Alchahest," a universal solvent. The text compares the views of major alchemical authorities like Paracelsus and Van Helmont, debating whether the substance is a refined mercury or a volatile salt of tartar.</summary>
<keywords>Alchahest, universal solvent, Martin Ruland, Baptist Van Helmont, Paracelsus, Michael Toxites, mercury, salt of tartar, iatrochemistry, liver medicine</keywords>

--- Page 280 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Alchahest, specifically its medicinal application for the liver and its definition according to Paracelsus and Van Helmont. It follows the previous page's introduction to the solvent's origins.</meta>

-># CHEMICAL LEXICON.<-

...to guard it from dropsy <note>original: "hydrops"; an old medical term for swelling caused by excess fluid, now called edema</note> and all its types which arise from the liver. The procedure is as follows: after its coagulation, the *Alchahest* is to be dissolved, and afterward it is coagulated into a transmuted form, such that its process occurs through freezing and dissolving. If it unites with its own kind, then it is a medicine for the liver <note>original: "medicina hepatis"</note> above all other medicines; even if the liver were already "broken" <note>referring to a diseased or failing liver</note>, the medicine itself takes the place of the liver, as if it had never been damaged. For this reason, it is of the utmost necessity for you Physicians to learn this medicine through experience, and to know the preparation of the *Alchahest* on account of the many diseases that descend from the liver, so that you may ward them off. Thus says **Paracelsus**. And from there he directs the reader, for the preparation of the *Mercury of the Alchahest*, to the table in the fourth book of *On Degrees*, chapter seven, where under the title of "The Liver" he calls this liquid the "mystery of Mercury" <note>original: "mysterium Mercurii"</note>. Thus far Paracelsus.

By **Jan Baptist Van Helmont**, it is described as follows under the name **Alcahest**:

The liquor *Alcahest* of Paracelsus resolves every visible body into its primary matter <note>original: "primam materiam"; the fundamental substance from which all things are made</note>, while preserving the power of the seeds <note>the inherent formative or life-giving principles within matter</note>. Regarding this, the **Chemists** say: "The common folk burn by fire, we burn by water." <note>original: "vulgus cremat per ignem, nos per aquam"; this refers to the alchemical idea that while heat (fire) destroys substances, the universal solvent (water/liquor) breaks them down into their essence without destroying their vital qualities</note>. **Van Helmont**, in book 2 of *On the Nature of Things*, chapter 3, page 482, calls it *Mercury prepared for the liver*.

The same *Alcahest* of Paracelsus penetrates all bodies of nature and transmutes them by refining them. **Van Helmont**.

From these things it appears that all these named authors agree and conclude that:

| | |
| :--- | :--- |
| **Alchahest**, <br> or <br> **Alcahest**. | **Is Mercury prepared as a medicine against diseases of the liver.** |

And in express words by **Theophrastus Paracelsus**, and by

<meta>catchword: Baptist</meta>

<summary>This page details the medicinal virtues of the Alchahest, specifically its ability to heal and even replace a failing liver. It cites Paracelsus and Van Helmont to define the substance as a "Mercury" that acts as a universal solvent through "water" rather than "fire."</summary>
<keywords>Alchahest, Paracelsus, Van Helmont, Mercury, liver disease, dropsy, primary matter, universal solvent, transmutation</keywords>

--- Page 281 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Alchahest from the previous page, finishing a sentence regarding Jan Baptist van Helmont’s definition before moving into alphabetical entries for "Enigma" and "Chybur."</meta>

-># CHEMICAL LEXICON.<-

...is called a liquor by **Jan Baptist Van Helmont**, but let these details be sufficient for the present. Based on this first taste, let the reader decide whether I should remain silent or speak further.

**Enigma** <note>original: "Ænigma"</note> is a word wrapped in allegory, or an obscure speech covering the entire thing it signifies with indirect meanings. The ancients were accustomed to hide their secrets <note>original: "arcana"</note> under riddles, hieroglyphics, and coverings, so that none but the "sons of the art" <note>a traditional term for initiated alchemists or physicians</note> could understand them. Even Physicians sometimes use these; for example: "Take the fat of a deformed infant and the pulverized tear of the **Dionysian** vine; mix them." By "deformed infant" they mean a bear <note>referring to the ancient belief that bear cubs were born as unformed lumps of flesh</note>, and by "tear of the Dionysian vine" they mean ivy gum <note>ivy was a plant sacred to Dionysus, the god of wine</note>. I will add only one other: "Take the powder of the sphere of the Microcosm and the powder of the male planetary peony; mix." By "sphere of the Microcosm" <note>original: "Microcosmi"; the human body viewed as a miniature reflection of the universe</note> they mean prepared human flesh (for neither the burning of human flesh nor of hartshorn <note>deer antler</note> is pleasing to me); and by "male planetary peony" they mean a peony gathered while the Sun and Moon are in the sign of Leo after the full moon.

**Chybur** <note>a technical term used by Paracelsus</note> is Sulfur. **Paracelsus** reports that nothing is more effective for the lungs than *Chybur* prepared in this manner, as seen on page 488 in his book *On the Nature of Things*. Let the *Chybur* be mixed with mineral calxes <note>the powdery remains left after a metal or mineral has been roasted</note> and then let it be sublimated <note>heated until it turns to vapor and then solidifies again to purify it</note> three times. Give this *Chybur* to the sick person, for it wards off all defects of the lungs and keeps them whole and free from all diseases. It is a balsam for the lungs and the chest, and no similar secret remedy <note>original: "arcanum"</note> is to be sought among the powers of nature; for in no other substance is there a greater secret for the lungs than in *Chybur* alone.

The following things remove Heat <note>referring to an excess of the "hot" humor in traditional medicine</note>:
{ Opiates, Anodynes <note>pain-relievers</note>, Styptics <note>substances that contract tissues to stop bleeding</note>, Laxatives, Vinegar-based remedies.

O 4

<meta>This page continues the alphabetical dictionary of chemical and medical terms, moving from the properties of "Chybur" (Sulfur) on the previous page into the letter G, while also discussing general principles of the "principal organs" and Paracelsian concepts of renewal.</meta>

-># CHEMICAL LEXICON.<-

| | |
| :--- | :--- |
| **They remove coldness** <note>original: "Frigiditatem tollunt"</note> | - Astringent substances <note>original: "Pontica," likely referring to acidic or puckering substances from the Black Sea region</note> |
| | - Bitter substances |
| | - Healing substances <note>original: "Cicatrisantia," agents that promote the formation of scar tissue</note> |
| | - Corrosives |
| | - Cleansing agents <note>original: "Abstersiva"</note> |
| | - Laxatives |

In every **Strengthening remedy** <note>original: "Confortativo"; a tonic intended to bolster the body's vital spirits</note>, one should not worry whether the substance is hot or cold, but rather the power of its hidden essence <note>original: "vis arcani"</note> must be considered.

The **brain** is the root of all ligaments, the **heart** of the limbs, and the **liver** of the blood; therefore, preserve the principal parts, and you will have preserved the lesser parts that proceed from them. For the principal organs do not suffer through their own nature, but through some external "accident" <note>a philosophical term meaning a non-essential or external influence rather than a flaw in the organ's essence</note>; take away the accident, and you will heal the sick person.

If you wish to preserve life, you must preserve not only the **heart** in its own being, but also the **brain**, **liver**, and **lungs**, and every other thing in its own being. For although the **heart** is the center and root of the body itself, nevertheless, the destruction of any principal organ brings death.

**Gelutta**,
*or*
**Geluta**,
is the herb **Carlina** <note>The Carline thistle, traditionally used in herbal medicine</note>.

**Gelutta** and **Melissa** <note>Lemon balm</note> are said by **Paracelsus** in his book *On Long Life* to renew and transfer disease, without any power of the "degrees" <note>referring to the traditional system of grading medicines by degrees of heat, cold, moisture, or dryness</note>, specifically for the restoration and renewal of original youth.

**Guaiacum wood** <note>original: "Guajacum lignum"; a medicinal wood from the Americas used to treat various ailments, including syphilis</note> is truly the ebony wood of **Paracelsus**.

The **degrees** <note>original: "Gradus"</note> of things are four, according to the orders of the matrices <note>in Paracelsian theory, a "matrix" is a womb-like environment or element, such as Earth or Water, that gives birth to specific substances</note>.

For those things that come from the earth are of the first degree.

<meta>catchword: For [those]</meta>

<summary>This page discusses the medical principles of strengthening the body's principal organs (heart, brain, liver, lungs) and defines specific herbal and chemical terms used by Paracelsus, such as Gelutta and Guaiacum.</summary>
<keywords>organs, brain, heart, liver, Paracelsus, Carlina, Melissa, Guaiacum, degrees, matrices, healing</keywords>

--- Page 283 ---
<meta>This page continues the alphabetical Chemical Lexicon, transitioning from general medical principles into specific definitions for "Elixir," a Paracelsian redefinition of the "Humors," and the administration of laxatives.</meta>

<page-num>221</page-num>
-># CHEMICAL LEXICON<-

Those things originating from **air** are of the second degree. 
Those from **water** are of the third. 
And those from **fire** are of the fourth degree. <note>In early medicine, the intensity of a substance’s qualities—heat, cold, moisture, or dryness—was measured on a scale of one to four "degrees."</note> 
Furthermore, the specific power by which one thing overcomes another within a single degree is a matter of "points" and not of degrees. <note>original: "punctorum est, & non graduum"; this suggests a more precise measurement of potency within each major classification.</note>
In the first degree, any substance neither harms nor helps.

**Elixir** is a fermented medicine made from the seven metals. <note>In alchemy, the seven metals are Gold, Silver, Iron, Copper, Mercury, Tin, and Lead, each associated with one of the seven planets.</note>

**Humors**: No man should be called **Melancholic**, **Phlegmatic**, **Choleric**, or **Sanguine** in an absolute sense. For every human being is "sanguine" <note>meaning "of the blood"</note> since blood is a constituent part of the body. Therefore, a person should be described as either **Mercurial**, **Sulphurous**, or **Saline**, depending on which substance predominates. <note>This is a core concept of Paracelsian medicine, which replaced the ancient Four Humors (blood, phlegm, yellow bile, black bile) with the "Tria Prima": Mercury, Sulfur, and Salt.</note> Accordingly, you should note that what the **Galenists** <note>Followers of the ancient Greek physician Galen</note> call **Cholera** <note>yellow bile</note>, you shall call **red sulfur**; what they call **phlegm**, you shall call **white sulfur** with dissolved salts; and what they call **Melancholy** <note>black bile</note>, you shall call the **Mercurial humor**. Furthermore, what they say about "purifying the blood" is false; rather, through those medicines, they purge the nature of salt from **Alum** and wondrous varieties. <note>original: "magalia genera"; likely referring to *magnalia*, the "great works" or hidden virtues of nature. This section is attributed to Paracelsus ("Para.").</note>

**Laxatives**:
Every **Laxative** contains two evils within itself:
1. One, that it weakens.
2. The other, that it usually evacuates more from the body than is proper.
Therefore, they must be used with great caution or even avoided.
In the **first degree** <note>the mildest category of medicine</note>, there is no need for strengthening the body against the weakening effect of the laxative.
In the **second degree**, strengthen the body with **cinnamon**, **cloves** <note>original: "gyryophitis," likely a corruption of *caryophyllis*</note>, **zedoary** <note>a root similar to ginger</note>, **sweet flag** <note>original: "calamo aromatico"</note>, and similar substances.

<summary>This page defines the "Elixir" as a metallic medicine and presents a Paracelsian rejection of the four Galenic humors in favor of a chemical system based on Salt, Sulfur, and Mercury. It also provides practical advice on the degrees of medicinal intensity and the dangers of laxatives.</summary>

<keywords>Elixir, Humors, Tria Prima, Paracelsus, Galenists, Sulfur, Mercury, Salt, Laxatives, Medical Degrees, Spices</keywords>

--- Page 284 ---
<meta>This page continues the "Chemical Lexicon," specifically concluding the discussion on degrees of medicines and moving into alphabetical definitions for terms ranging from "Epilathera" to "Quality." It maintains the Paracelsian focus on the "Tria Prima" and the correspondence between the microcosm and macrocosm.</meta>

<page-num>222</page-num>
->### CHEMICAL LEXICON ###<-

In the **third degree**, [medicines act] by means of nutmeg, Indian leaf, <note>original: "folium Indi"; likely Malabathrum, used in ancient and medieval pharmacy</note> and juniper berries.
In the **fourth degree**, through sealed earth, <note>original: "terra sigillata"; a medicinal clay stamped with a seal of authenticity, traditionally from the island of Lemnos</note> white and red coral, amber, <note>original: "carabe"; a Persian-derived term for amber</note> raw silk, bone from a stag's heart, and similar things.
However, to mitigate a laxative, use anodynes and narcotics. <note>Anodynes are pain-relieving medicines.</note>

***Epilathera***, that is, "particular."
***Epilathera*** should be considered in this manner: that in every tiny drop, the anatomy of the microcosm <note>The "microcosm" refers to the human being as a miniature reflection of the "macrocosm," or the universe</note> exists. For every **spagyric** <note>Spagyric refers to alchemical medicine, specifically the process of separating and recombining substances</note> degree is a tincture of its own virtue; and every Physician must understand that in each single drop—however many there may be—there are just as many whole anatomies as there are in the Microcosm and in the elemental world.

***Magale*** is a type of *Magalium* <note>A Paracelsian term for a subtle, ethereal mineral substance or virtue</note>; whatever is made from minerals that can consist of an oily nature; therefore, any part of the body is cured not by purging, but by cleansing. <note>In this context, "cleansing" refers to a more subtle purification than the harsh "purging" of standard medieval medicine.</note>

Every **Metal** is coagulated **Mercury**. <note>A fundamental alchemical belief that all metals are composed of a primal Mercury fixed or "frozen" into different forms.</note>

***Nectar*** is a preparation of egg whites.

***Laruscus***, that is, mouse-ear. <note>The herb *Pilosella officinarum*, traditionally used in respiratory and wound care.</note>

***Halereon***, according to **Hermes**, <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary founder of alchemy</note> is the true **Eagle**. **Paracelsus.** <note>In alchemy, the "Eagle" often refers to the volatility of a substance, specifically sal ammoniac.</note>

Use nothing superfluous in your recipes: for the more **simples** <note>Individual medicinal herbs or ingredients</note> or compounds that are combined in recipes, the more unlearned the presiding Physician is considered; for no recipe should universally contain more than six simples. **Paracelsus**, *On Degrees*.

***Peony***, when rightly gathered and prepared, is the **arcanum** <note>A "secret" or powerful higher-order medicine</note> for **Epilepsy**. **Paracelsus.**

**Quality**, among the Spagyrics, is everything that defends any principal organ, as in the brain between the **pia mater** <note>The delicate innermost membrane surrounding the brain</note> and the brain itself.

<meta>This page continues the "Chemical Lexicon," transitioning from terms starting with "E" and "M" on the previous page to definitions for "Lunaria," the alchemical "Lily of Paracelsus," the "Spirit of Life," and the significance of the number "Seven" in the natural world.</meta>

<page-num>223</page-num>

-> # CHEMICAL LEXICON # <-

**Lunaria,** |
or | These are things that preserve the brain; hence every herb that strengthens the
**Cerebella,** | brain can be called by this name. — *Paracelsus*

**Lily of Paracelsus** <note>original: "Lillium Paracelsi"</note>: Instead of the tincture of antimony, let the true composition of the Lily of Paracelsus be substituted from the book *On Long Life*. This composition can eventually contribute much labor toward extracting the tincture from it. It is a remarkable **diaphoretic** <note>a medicine that induces sweating</note> and **hydrotic** <note>original: "hydro-ticum"; a medicine that promotes the discharge of fluids</note>, and is of great power. It is administered for any diseases whatsoever, in doses from two to five or six grains, in wine or in a cordial water. 

If this tincture of **Antimony** is subsequently exalted into a most noble essence through many **cohobations** <note>original: "cohobia"; the alchemical process of redistilling a liquid over its own solid remains to increase its potency</note> with some suitable **menstruum** <note>a solvent used in alchemy to extract essences</note>, it successfully cures **contracture** <note>the permanent tightening of muscles or joints</note>, especially those caused by **gout** <note>original: "podagra"</note> in the external joints. This is successful if the necessary precautions regarding a proper diet and the cleansing of the body are observed. One drop of the exalted tincture should be given daily or on alternating days with an appropriate **vehicle** <note>a substance like water or wine used to deliver a medicine</note> at dawn, and a sweat should be induced either in bed or in a **Laconicum** <note>a dry sweat bath or sauna</note>. This should be done as the contracture begins to fade; in this way, no arthritic contraction can resist it. **Beguinus** <note>Jean Beguin (c. 1550–1620), an influential chemist and author of "Tyrocinium Chymicum"</note> teaches the composition of this tincture of Antimony on page 425.

**Spirit of Life** <note>original: "Spiritus vitæ"</note> is the spirit that dwells in all parts of the body, however they are positioned. And all members, whether they are **noble** <note>referring to vital organs like the heart, brain, or liver</note> or not, have a single spirit of life, which possesses the same virtue, force, and operation in all of them, and from which all members live. In us, the spirit of life lives in the form of a smoke <note>original: "fumi"</note>, by reason of the **second generation** <note>a Paracelsian concept referring to the spiritual refinement of a substance after its initial creation</note>, which occurs after the first nature. — *Paracelsus*

**Seven.** In fire there are seven planets, in water seven metals, in earth seven herbs, in air there are seven **tereniabin** <note>a term for "manna" or "heavenly dew" believed to condense from the air</note>, and in animals there are seven principal members. — *Paracelsus*

<summary>This page defines alchemical and medicinal terms, specifically detailing the "Lily of Paracelsus" as a potent cure for gout and explaining the "Spirit of Life" as a singular vital force existing as a smoke within the body.</summary>

--- Page 286 ---
<meta>This page continues the "Chemical Lexicon" from page 223. While the previous page discussed the "Lily of Paracelsus" and antimony, this page focuses on various "earths" and derivatives of vitriol (sulfates), citing Jean Béguin and Martin Ruland the Elder.</meta>

<page-num>224</page-num>

-> ### CHEMICAL LEXICON ### <-

**Holy Earth** <note>original: "Terra sancta"</note>: Martin Ruland the Elder calls **saffron of metals** <note>original: "crocum metallorum"; an alchemical preparation, usually of antimony, named for its reddish-orange color</note> "holy earth." Béguin, page 292.

**Sweet Earth of Vitriol** <note>original: "Terra vitrioli dulcis"</note> is the reduction of the salt of **colcothar** <note>original: "Colcotharis"; the red iron oxide residue remaining after the distillation of sulfuric acid from iron sulfate</note> to a "sweet" state through **calcination** <note>the process of heating a substance to high temperatures to change its physical or chemical constitution</note>, achieved by washing away its saltiness. Béguin, page 374.

**Tincture, or Narcotic Sulfur of Vitriol** <note>original: "Tinctura, aut Sulphur Narcoticum vitrioli"</note> is an excellent **anodyne** <note>a medicine that kills pain</note> that should rightly be preferred above all others; Paracelsus commends it most highly and values it greatly. It is described in Béguin, page 431.

**Liquor of Vitriol** <note>original: "Vitrioli Liquor"</note> is an excellent **arcanum** <note>a powerful secret remedy</note> for the **falling sickness** <note>original: "morbo caduco"; an old term for epilepsy</note>.

**Liquor of Vitriol** is also excellent for bile, and it is the **oil of colcothar**.

**Roman Vitriol** <note>original: "Vitriolum Romanum"</note>: Béguin writes that Cyprian and Roman vitriol should rightly be viewed with suspicion because they are almost always adulterated, counterfeited, varied, and of differing colors, forms, and potencies; furthermore, no vitriol is actually mined in Rome.

## *Conclusion of the Work.* <note>original: "Conclusio Operis"</note>

In this small volume <note>original: "fasciculo"; literally a small bundle or packet of papers</note>, whatever I have gathered together 
from the writings of ***Paracelsus*** <note>Philippus Aureolus Theophrastus Bombastus von Hohenheim (1493–1541), a Swiss physician and alchemist who pioneered the use of chemicals and minerals in medicine</note>, or from other 
authors following in his path, I have done so 
according to the limited time at my disposal 
and my own meager talent. I have done this 
so that it may not always be necessary to return to 
whichever books of the author you might need, 
but instead, having this at hand in all places and 
wherever you go, you may be able to carry 
this small collection <note>original: "congeriem"</note> with you. 
Read and study it in your leisure hours; 
in that leisure, you will find both occupation and 
delight for yourself. No longer will any 
cause for complaint be given to you in the future 
concerning the obscurity of things and words; 
for those things which were dispersed and 
interspersed in various places, I have 
brought together from many sources into one, 
in such a way that when individual points 
are compared with one another, one may 
explain the other. But here, I would wish 
the **Reader** to be warned, lest the foreign 
words <note>original: "voces peregrinas"; referring to the specialized, often invented, terminology used in alchemical texts</note> occurring everywhere...

<meta>...throughout the text [referring to the word "occurrentes" which breaks across the page].</meta>

<summary>In this concluding note, the author explains that they have compiled various Paracelsian writings into this portable volume to make the often obscure and scattered ideas of alchemy more accessible and easier to cross-reference.</summary>
<keywords>Paracelsus, conclusion, alchemical collection, study of alchemy, accessibility, paracelsian terminology</keywords>

--- Page 288 ---
<meta>This page continues the "Conclusion of the Work" from page 225. The previous page ended with a warning to the reader about "foreign words occurring everyw[here]" (occurrentes). This page picks up with the end of that word and warns against interpreting Paracelsian terms through the lens of traditional Greek philosophy or medicine.</meta>

<page-num>226</page-num>

<header>Conclusion of the Work.</header>

[occuring] everywhere, should be interpreted in any way other than how they have been defined and described here. For if one explains them according to **Greek phrasing** (with which they may seem to harmonize in some way), he will wander "by the whole heaven" <note>original: "toto cœlo"; a Latin idiom meaning to be as far off as possible or completely mistaken</note> from the author’s true meaning. And because he [Paracelsus] rebukes the errors of the Greeks and the unfaithful, which have been spread everywhere by them, their **followers** try to make ***Paracelsus*** appear unfaithful with their sophistical <note>fallacious or deceptively subtle</note> arguments; they accuse him of being impious, sacrilegious, imprudent, a meddler <note>original: "ardelionem"; a person who busies himself with things that do not concern him</note>, and a heretic. But these envious and puffed-up <note>original: "buccati"; literally "puffed-cheeked," referring to those who speak with empty or boastful arrogance</note> men may easily find an answer to these slanders in that book by **Gerard Dorn** <note>Gerhard Dorn (c. 1530–1584), a Belgian philosopher and one of the most important editors and defenders of Paracelsus's works</note> which he titled *On the Light of Nature*. 

But [some say] he has too sharply stirred up the disciples of the ancient Greeks by calling their leaders **pseudo-physicians** <note>false doctors; a common Paracelsian insult for those who relied on ancient theories rather than chemical experimentation</note> and erroneous; are these the harsher words by which they have been pierced and transfixed? But they have sworn so stubbornly to the words of their masters that nothing tastes right to them unless it has been brought forth from those masters' mouths, nor does anything please their palates unless it was drawn from those specific fountains: so zealously and 

<meta>catchword: de (of/concerning)</meta>

<summary>This page concludes the text by defending Paracelsus against critics who label him a heretic or a fool. The author warns readers not to use Greek definitions for Paracelsian terms and points to Gerard Dorn's writings as a proper defense of the "Light of Nature."</summary>

<keywords>Paracelsus, Gerard Dorn, Greek medicine, pseudo-physicians, Light of Nature, alchemy, defense of Paracelsus, medical terminology</keywords>

--- Page 289 ---
<meta>This page continues the "Conclusion of the Work." The previous page ended with a rhetorical question regarding the harsh words used by Paracelsus against the followers of ancient Greek medicine.</meta>

<page-num>227</page-num>

# Conclusion of the Work

...they would rather intentionally resist the truth than confess the manifest error of their teachers; and like hired horses, you refuse to turn away from the common path, even though a safer, easier, and more direct way is shown to you. You truly fear to retract those things which you have professed throughout your whole life, adhering too closely to an old proverb in your opinion:

->*The smoke of the fathers is brighter than a foreign fire.*<-
<note>original: "Fumus patrum, non patria, luculentior igne alieno." This is a variation of a classical proverb. While it usually refers to one's homeland, here it suggests that people prefer the familiar, "smoky" errors of their ancestors over a brilliant, "fiery" new discovery from a stranger.</note>

They admit that **Paracelsus** was most excellent in preparing purging medicines, as one who well knew how to draw out the metallic and vegetable virtues while leaving the dregs <note>original: "faecibus"; the waste or impure sediment left over after a chemical process</note> behind, separating the pure from the impure. He did not, like the **Greeks** <note>the followers of the traditional medical system established by Galen and Hippocrates</note>, teach his disciples to devour substances in their medicine along with their corrupted bodies, no differently than food; as if there were no distinction between a **Physician** and a **cook**. For this reason, **Paracelsus** surpasses **Galen** <note>Claudius Galenus (129–c. 216 AD), the Greek physician whose theories dominated Western medicine for over a millennium until challenged by chemically-minded doctors</note> by many **parasangs** <term>parasangs: an ancient Persian unit of distance, used here metaphorically to mean "by a great distance" or "by a long shot"</term>, because he [nourished the sick] with delicate food and

<meta>catchword: drink</meta>

<summary>This page defends Paracelsus against his critics, arguing that traditional physicians cling to ancient errors like "hired horses" on a well-worn path. It highlights the Paracelsian method of chemical extraction, which separates medicinal "virtues" from useless waste, contrasting it with traditional Greek medicine.</summary>

<keywords>Paracelsus, Galen, medical reform, spagyric extraction, pharmacy vs. cookery, traditionalism, chemical medicine</keywords>

--- Page 290 ---
<meta>This page concludes the "Conclusion of the Work." The text continues comparing the methodology of traditional Greek (Galenic) medicine, which focuses on nourishing the physical body, with Paracelsus’s chemical approach, which seeks to revive the internal vital spirits using metallic essences.</meta>

<page-num>228</page-num>
<header>Conclusion of the Work.</header>

...by drink he should nourish the solid body of the sick; he <note>Referring to the Paracelsian physician.</note>, on the contrary, by the extracted virtues of hard metals, restores and refreshes the sparse spirits of life <note>original: "spiritus vitæ"; in the medical theory of the time, these were subtle substances that circulated through the body to provide vitality and movement.</note>. In summary, the **Greeks** ascribed the entire task and duty to themselves, and nothing to nature. **Paracelsus**, however, attributes all of this to nature, and makes the **Physician** its servant <note>original: "hujus Ministrum"; a core Paracelsian tenet was that "Nature is the doctor, and the physician is her assistant."</note>. Leaning upon this foundation, he not only does not fear the harmless, though violent, attacks of his adversaries, but treats them with contempt.
